**Summary.** This paper introduces CTEMPO, a novel tensor network path integral method designed for numerically exact simulation of open quantum systems with strong coupling. It overcomes the limitations of previous techniques by maintaining small bond dimensions, enabling the study of complex models like photosynthetic complexes and large quantum dot arrays. This provides a turnkey solution for non-Markovian quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it provides a powerful, scalable numerical tool to simulate open quantum dynamics in complex, strongly coupled environments where standard methods fail due to memory or computational cost.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of numerically simulating non-Markovian open quantum networks, especially those involving strong system-bath couplings, which typically lead to exponentially scaling state spaces.

**Main result.** The proposed CTEMPO scheme provides a significant computational speed-up (orders of magnitude) over established methods like TEMPO and PT-MPO, allowing for the simulation of complex systems like the 7-site FMO complex and large QD arrays.

**Method.** The core method is CTEMPO, a novel tensor network path integral scheme that combines insights from TEMPO and causal contraction order to maintain small bond dimensions when simulating the time evolution of the system density matrix.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general open quantum networks, including the spin-boson model, the 7-site Fenna-Matthews-Olson (FMO) photosynthetic complex, and arrays of quantum dots (QDs) coupled to structured phonon baths.

**Key observables.** Time evolution of expectation values (e.g., $\langle \sigma_z angle$), superradiant emission intensity, and computational metrics like bond dimensions ($\chi$) and run time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong coupling regimes ($\alpha=1$), spectral density structure (super-Ohmic), and system size ($N$ up to 20 QDs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the environment coupling can be effectively mapped onto a tensor network structure, and the simulation is limited by available computer memory for very large systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the tensor network structure comparison (CTEMPO vs. others), the time evolution of $\langle \sigma_z angle$ showing performance gains, and the superradiant emission intensity decay curves.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the CTEMPO algorithm and its multi-site extension (comb tensor network), validates it by benchmarking against existing methods on the spin-boson model, and then applies it to complex physical systems like FMO and QDs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Markovian open quantum networks are among the most challenging problems in quantum theory, as they suffer from both, the exponentially scaling many-body state space as well as long memory times when the sites are strongly coupled to structured environments. Recent efforts to address them by combining tensor networks with real-time path integrals, such as TEMPO and process tensors, were however limited to systems with rather weak system-environment couplings because strong couplings lead to large bond dimensions. Here, we devise a tensor network scheme (CTEMPO) resulting in particularly small bond dimensions for single-site problems. We then extend this to a practical scheme to simulate non-Markovian quantum networks by time-evolving a corresponding comb tensor network. Benchmarking the single-site algorithm on the spin-boson model we find orders-of-magnitude improvement over established methods. The multi-site algorithm is shown to solve the notoriously difficult problem of the 7-site Fenna-Matthews-Olson photosynthetic complex, whose spectral density contains 62 peaks. We then apply our approach to explore the scaling behavior of the superradiance of up to $N=20$ quantum dots individually coupled to super-Ohmic phonon baths. Our approach provides a turnkey solution to non-Markovian quantum networks and is compatible with more general tensor network topologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that the symmetry of dissipation in a tripartite Mollow system can actively control the quantum emission pathway. By tuning the dissipation symmetry, the system can be forced to emit either highly correlated photon-phonon pairs or distinct photon bundles. This establishes dissipation symmetry as a powerful resource for programmable hybrid quantum emitters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the frontier of open quantum systems, demonstrating that engineered dissipation can be a constructive tool for quantum state engineering, moving beyond its traditional role as a source of loss.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether dissipation can actively determine or select the type of quantum resource (e.g., correlated pairs vs. photon bundles) emitted from an open hybrid quantum system, rather than simply causing decoherence.

**Main result.** Dissipation symmetry acts as a controllable resource, allowing the system to steer quantum emission between correlated photon-phonon pairs (under symmetric dissipation) and purely photonic nonclassical states (under asymmetric dissipation).

**Method.** The analysis uses the Lindblad master equation to model the open system dynamics and characterizes emission using time-resolved correlation functions, Fock-state distributions, and Wigner quasiprobability distributions.

**Model / system.** The system is a tripartite Mollow setup involving a strongly driven two-level emitter coupled simultaneously to cavity photons and motional phonons. The dynamics are governed by an effective Hamiltonian derived in the Mollow regime.

**Key observables.** Time-delayed second-order correlation functions ($g^{(2)}$), Fock-state distributions, and Wigner quasiprobability distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dissipation ratio ($\kappa_a/\kappa_b$) is the key control parameter, determining the emission regime (symmetric vs. asymmetric dissipation).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system dynamics are modeled using effective Hamiltonians derived under the rotating-wave approximation, and the selection mechanism is attributed to the symmetry of the bosonic dissipation channels.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the switching between pair and bundle emission by tuning the dissipation ratio ($\kappa_a/\kappa_b$), and another figure shows the dressed-state structure in the Mollow regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the tripartite Mollow model, deriving effective Hamiltonians for $N$-pair processes, and then analyzing the resulting emission regimes—symmetric (pair emission) versus asymmetric (bundle emission)—by controlling the dissipation symmetry.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dissipation is generally regarded as detrimental to nonclassical-state generation, yet whether it can determine which quantum resource is emitted from an open hybrid quantum system remains largely unexplored. Here, we show that the symmetry of bosonic dissipation channels can select and switch quantum-emission pathways in a tripartite Mollow system, where a strongly driven two-level emitter simultaneously couples to cavity photons and motional phonons. In the Mollow regime, higher-order tripartite interactions generate resonant photon-phonon pair excitations, whose emitted quantum states are subsequently selected by dissipation. Under symmetric dissipation, the system produces strongly correlated photon-phonon pairs, enabling both single-pair emission and correlated multipair bundles with pronounced nonclassical correlations. Breaking the dissipation symmetry progressively suppresses the hybrid correlations and dynamically decouples the photonic and phononic degrees of freedom. In the strongly asymmetric regime, long-lived phonons accumulate in highly excited Fock states, while rapidly leaking cavity photons are converted into high-purity single-photon and two-photon bundles. The crossover between these distinct emission regimes is characterized through time-resolved correlation functions, Fock-state distributions, and Wigner quasiprobability distributions. Our results establish dissipation symmetry as a resource for steering quantum emission between hybrid and purely photonic nonclassical states, providing a route toward programmable hybrid quantum emitters and dissipative quantum-state engineering.

</details>

(c),(d) Signed in- tensity asymmetry An = log10(n+/n−). (e) Conditional single-photon path concurrence C−(∆res c,L); the black dashed contour marks C−= 0.7. (f) Worst-case branch-direction concurrence mismatch ∆CBD. Branch switching reverses both photon-statistical and intensity...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes that path complementarity, arising from the interplay between cavity backscattering and Zeeman splitting in a ring-cavity QED system, is the key mechanism for generating directional quantum light. The authors demonstrate that this mechanism allows for the simultaneous observation of directional photon blockade and strong single-photon path entanglement. This establishes a general design rule for controlling quantum light sources for applications in quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, general design principle—path complementarity—for engineering nonclassical light sources, linking fundamental concepts of coherence preservation with directional quantum control, which is highly relevant to quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is achieving directional quantum light that exhibits asymmetric photon statistics while maintaining high coherence between counterpropagating paths.

**Main result.** The interplay between cavity backscattering and Zeeman splitting establishes 'path complementarity' as a general design principle, allowing directional photon blockade and strong path entanglement to coexist.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using the Hamiltonian and master equation in the driven-dissipative regime to derive intrinsic polariton properties and steady-state quantum correlations.

**Model / system.** The system is a ring-cavity Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) setup hybridized with a three-level emitter, modeled by a Hamiltonian incorporating cavity detuning, coupling, backscattering, and Zeeman splitting.

**Key observables.** Directional photon blockade (antibunching in one direction, bunching in the other), single-photon path entanglement, and path concurrence ($C_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak driving regime, Zeeman splitting ($\delta_f$), cavity backscattering ($J$), and the resonance branch selection.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the weak-drive regime, allowing the intrinsic polariton structure to dictate the directional response.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the physical setup, the dressed spectrum, and the intrinsic photonic path concurrence; later figures establish the coexistence of directional blockade and path entanglement across parameter regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the physical ingredients (backscattering and Zeeman splitting) leading to the problem. It then analyzes the intrinsic polariton structure to derive path predictability and concurrence. Finally, it extends this analysis to the driven-dissipative steady state to demonstrate the coexistence of directional photon statistics and path entanglement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Directional quantum light requires asymmetric photon statistics without sacrificing coherence between propagation paths, a combination that remains difficult to achieve within a single quantum-optical platform. Here we identify a microscopic path-complementarity mechanism in hybridized cavity polaritons that reconciles these two requirements. In a Zeeman-split ring-cavity QED system, cavity backscattering hybridizes the counterpropagating modes, whereas the Zeeman splitting creates spectrally asymmetric excitation channels. Their interplay gives rise, under weak driving, to directional photon blockade coexisting with strong single-photon path entanglement: one input direction exhibits antibunching while the opposite direction is bunched. Remarkably, switching between the two one-photon polariton resonances reverses both the photon-statistical and intensity asymmetries while nearly preserving the path concurrence over a broad parameter regime. Our results establish path complementarity as a general design principle for directional nonclassical light with retained quantum coherence.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates geometric heat pumping on a superconducting quantum processor by cyclically driving a qubit coupled to two thermal reservoirs. The authors show that the measurable, non-zero heat pumped is a geometric quantity determined by the path taken in parameter space. This bridges fundamental concepts from quantum thermodynamics and geometric phases to near-term quantum hardware implementations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally relevant platform (superconducting qubits) to test fundamental non-equilibrium thermodynamic principles, specifically linking quantum work extraction to geometric phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates geometric heat pumping—the transfer of energy between reservoirs at equal temperature—by cyclically driving a quantum system.

**Main result.** The pumped heat is shown to be a purely geometric quantity, depending only on the area enclosed by the driving contour in parameter space, and this is confirmed by comparing collision models to the adiabatic limit.

**Method.** The authors employ a collision model implemented on a quantum processor, analyzing the system dynamics using both discrete time-stepping (collision model) and continuous-time adiabatic expansions.

**Model / system.** The system is a working qubit coupled to two thermal reservoirs (L and R), modeled via a collision process involving ancilla qubits. The dynamics are described by a time-dependent Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Stochastic heat exchanged per collision ($Q_{\alpha,j,k}$), ensemble average heat ($Q_c^\alpha$), and the geometric heat contribution ($Q_{geo}^\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The slow-driving regime, the area enclosed by the driving contour, and the ratio of pumped heat to noise-induced heating.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the adiabatic approximation in the slow-driving limit to extract the geometric contribution, and the collision model assumes ancillas are reset after each interaction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum circuit for a single time cell, detailing the sequence of collisions and the structure of energy measurements ($E_{in}$ and $E_{out}$) used to calculate heat.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the physical setup and the collision model to deriving the continuous-time Lindblad dynamics. It then analyzes the slow-driving limit using adiabatic expansions to isolate the geometric heat term, benchmarking these results against direct simulations of the collision model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Geometric heat pumping is the transfer of energy between reservoirs at equal temperature where the heat exchanged in an adiabatic cycle depends only on the driving contour in parameter space. We implement this mechanism on a superconducting quantum processor using a collision model, where a driven qubit interacts repeatedly with two ancilla qubits that are prepared in thermal states and emulate the reservoirs. Energy measurements before and after each interaction provide a calorimetric record of the heat exchanged with each reservoir. By reversing the driving contour, we extract a heat contribution associated with pumping. Its dependence on the period and on the contour follows exact simulations of the collision model, without adjustable parameters, and approaches in the slow-driving regime the geometric limit of the corresponding Lindblad dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reveals a fundamental duality connecting slow, non-thermalizing states (Quantum Many-Body Scars) in closed quantum systems to diffusive Nambu-Goldstone modes in open systems. By establishing a spectral correspondence between the parent Hamiltonian and the effective Lindbladian, the authors unify the understanding of slow relaxation across different physical regimes. This has broad implications for understanding quantum dynamics in both isolated and dissipative environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, unifying mathematical framework connecting two distinct physical phenomena—non-thermal scars in isolated systems and diffusive relaxation in open ones—using spectral theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a deep theoretical correspondence between slow relaxation mechanisms in closed quantum systems (Quantum Many-Body Scars) and diffusive dynamics in open quantum systems (Nambu-Goldstone modes).

**Main result.** It shows that a common underlying structure links weak ergodicity breaking in closed systems to diffusive hydrodynamics in open systems, specifically mapping an exact quadratic parent-Hamiltonian branch to a diffusive NG mode.

**Method.** The authors use spectral analysis, variational constructions, and the comparison between the parent Hamiltonian of the scar states and the effective Lindbladian of the open system.

**Model / system.** The study considers general spin, fermionic, and bosonic systems, exemplified by models like the XXZ Hamiltonian on a two-leg ladder. The dynamics are governed by a closed system Hamiltonian and an open system Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** The key observables are the spectral properties: the gap scaling ($O(k^2)$) in the parent Hamiltonian for scars, and the decay rate $\lambda(k) \simeq Dk^2$ for the diffusive NG modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The correspondence holds under the assumption of strong U(1) symmetry and local charge-dephasing Lindblad operators, and for uniformly bounded positive transition rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The correspondence is derived assuming the existence of asymptotic scar states and the applicability of the Lindblad formalism for the open system dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the scar states in closed systems, then analyzing the dynamics in open systems via the Lindbladian, and finally establishing the spectral correspondence between the two frameworks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hydrodynamic relaxation in open quantum many-body systems can be understood in terms of diffusive Nambu--Goldstone (NG) modes associated with strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SWSSB). Here, we find a common structure underlying weak ergodicity breaking in closed quantum systems and diffusive hydrodynamics in open quantum systems. We establish a spectral correspondence between the parent Hamiltonian of rainbow quantum many-body scar (RQMBS) states in closed systems and the effective Lindbladian of strongly dissipative open systems with strong $U(1)$ symmetry and local charge-dephasing Lindblad operators. For broad classes of spin, fermionic, and bosonic systems, we construct asymptotic RQMBS (ARQMBS) states. Within the enlarged scar subspace, the parent Hamiltonian admits a local Rokhsar--Kivelson-type frustration-free decomposition. A single-mode variational construction gives an $O(k^2)$ upper bound on its lowest excitation energy and yields states satisfying the defining ARQMBS criteria: orthogonality, vanishing energy variance, and characteristic entanglement scaling. Under vectorization, RQMBS states are mapped to maximally mixed infinite-temperature states in fixed-charge sectors, which exhibit long-range SWSSB order under finite-density conditions. Assuming uniformly bounded positive transition rates, the effective Lindbladian has the same local projectors as the parent Hamiltonian but with positive transition-dependent weights, yielding two-sided spectral bounds and the same system-size scaling of their gaps. For uniform rates, the two operators are proportional to each other and their eigenmodes coincide. Therefore, an exact quadratic parent-Hamiltonian branch maps directly to a diffusive NG mode. Our results connect different mechanisms of slow relaxation in closed and open quantum many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes the superradiant phase in a quantum Rabi ring, showing that the system possesses extensive degeneracy in the mean-field limit. Crucially, it demonstrates that quantum fluctuations introduce a non-classical Ising coupling that selects between ferro- and antiferro-superradiant states. The methodology combines mean-field theory with advanced quantum perturbation techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical understanding of how quantum fluctuations can stabilize and select specific ordered phases (FSR/AFSR) in cavity QED systems, demonstrating a quantum order-by-disorder effect absent classically.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the superradiant phase transition and the extensive degeneracy of superradiant configurations in an N-site quantum Rabi ring.

**Main result.** The system exhibits extensive degeneracy in the mean-field limit ($\eta 	o \infty$), which is stabilized by quantum fluctuations, and finite-frequency corrections select specific ferro- or antiferro-superradiant states via a quantum order-by-disorder effect.

**Method.** The analysis employs mean-field theory, followed by a Schrieffer–Wolff transformation and second-order perturbation theory to derive an effective Ising Hamiltonian describing quantum corrections.

**Model / system.** The model is an N-site quantum Rabi ring with two-level atoms, where each atom couples to two cavities two sites apart. The system is governed by a Hamiltonian involving cavity fields and atomic spins.

**Key observables.** Superradiant excitation gap ($\varepsilon_{\min}$), effective Ising coupling constants ($J_r$), and the degeneracy of superradiant configurations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The atomic-to-cavity frequency ratio $\eta = \Delta/\omega$, and the coupling ratio $eta$ that determines the phase (FSR, AFSR, CSR).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the mean-field approximation and subsequent quantum corrections derived via perturbation theory, assuming the photon band remains dispersive.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the model setup, momentum-dependent critical coupling, phase diagrams, and show the evolution of correlation lengths and sign structure factors distinguishing different superradiant phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the mean-field degeneracy in the infinite limit, to deriving the effective low-energy Hamiltonian using quantum corrections, and finally analyzing the resulting quantum order-by-disorder selection mechanism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that an $N$-site quantum Rabi ring with matched giant-atom couplings has $2^N$ degenerate superradiant configurations as the atomic-to-cavity frequency ratio $η=Δ/ω$ tends to infinity. Each atom couples to two cavities two sites apart, and the photon hopping carries a phase $π/2$. Tuning the ratio of contact strengths to the photon hopping reduces the mean-field energy to a sum of single-site Rabi energies, with a common critical coupling for all momentum-pair sectors. The degeneracy survives quadratic quantum fluctuations despite a dispersive excitation spectrum. At finite $η$, second-order processes involving the local cubic anharmonicity generate a same-sublattice Ising coupling of order $ω/η$, selecting ferro- and antiferro-superradiant configurations. This quantum order-by-disorder effect has no classical counterpart. Four-site calculations retaining all spin states quantitatively support the predicted energy coefficients. The separation between the induced coupling and the excitation gap allows a broad temperature interval with macroscopic photon occupation and short-range correlations between displacement signs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the strong coupling between color center quantum emitters in hBN and a plasmonic cavity. Using CL and PL spectroscopy, the authors observed spectral splitting in one emitter's vibronic transition, confirming cavity-mediated strong coupling. The results demonstrate that vibronic pathways offer a controllable mechanism for engineering interactions between multiple quantum emitters in nanoscale photonic structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it experimentally realizes and models cavity QED effects using solid-state emitters, demonstrating control over coupling regimes via specific vibrational modes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to demonstrate and characterize cavity-mediated strong coupling between multiple quantum emitters (color centers) embedded in a plasmonic nanophotonic cavity.

**Main result.** The research successfully observed spectral splitting in the emission of one emitter, confirming strong coupling via vibronic transitions, while another emitter remained in the weak-coupling regime.

**Method.** The work combines complementary experimental techniques, including Cathodoluminescence (CL) and Photoluminescence (PL) spectroscopy, with theoretical validation using Maxwell-Bloch simulations.

**Model / system.** The system involves color centers (e.g., VACD, structural defects) in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) coupled to a plasmonic gold (Au) void cavity. The interaction is modeled using cavity QED principles, focusing on the role of vibronic transitions.

**Key observables.** Energy splitting ($\approx 36.2 	ext{ meV}$ in CL vs. $\approx 24.1 	ext{ meV}$ in PL), spectral linewidths, and the relative coupling strength (strong vs. weak).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature operation, specific emitter wavelengths (443 nm, 537 nm), and the coupling energy scale relative to dissipation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed spectral changes are attributed to hybridization with the cavity mode, and the collective response of multiple emitters is crucial for observing the splitting.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare CL/PL spectra at different positions (coupled vs. uncoupled) showing the emergence of split features near the cavity resonance, and simulations confirm the hybridization mechanism.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical system and measurement techniques, then presenting evidence for strong coupling via spectral splitting, confirming this with simulations, and finally detailing the role of vibronic pathways in controlling these interactions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum emitters in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) are promising building blocks for solid-state quantum photonics because of their bright room-temperature emission. These emitters strongly interact with lattice vibrations, which partially underpins their excitation efficiency. Here, using complementary cathodoluminescence (CL) and photoluminescence (PL) spectroscopy, we demonstrate cavity-mediated interactions between hBN quantum emitters coupled to the same plasmonic Au void cavity at the room temperature. Cathodoluminescence measurements reveal that a vibronic transition of the 443 nm emitter hybridizes with the cavity mode, giving rise to a spectrally resolved splitting, while the neighboring 537 nm emitter remains in the weak-coupling regime despite spectral overlap. The energy split of the CL peak is at the order of 36.2 meV, whereas the PL-resolved peak is approximately 24.1 meV, which is due to the collective excitations of multiple emitters per electron excitation and their mutual interactions. Maxwell-Bloch simulations reproduce the experimental spectra and identify the observed splitting as the result of cavity-induced hybridization. Excitation-dependent PL further shows that the hybridized state can be accessed through phonon-assisted excitations below the zero-phonon transition. These results demonstrate that vibronic transitions provide an effective pathway for controlling interactions between multiple quantum emitters in hybrid plasmonic nanophotonic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper studies how quantum correlations dictate the maximum extractable work (ergotropy) in a system of multiple two-level atoms coupled to a thermal cavity field. By analyzing the interplay between mutual information and relative entropy, the authors show that the initial state is crucial, with partially entangled states maximizing work extraction. The findings highlight that controlling quantum correlations is key to advancing quantum power applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum thermodynamics in cavity QED settings, providing quantitative insights into how engineered quantum correlations (beyond simple entanglement) dictate energy harvesting capabilities in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the dynamics of quantum ergotropy (extractable work) in multipartite atomic systems, specifically analyzing how quantum correlations and relative entropy govern this work extraction.

**Main result.** The maximum extractable work is highly dependent on the initial quantum state, with partially entangled states yielding the largest and most persistent work. Furthermore, the dynamics are governed by a competition between mutual information (resource) and relative entropy (penalty).

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical derivations based on the Tavis-Cummings model Hamiltonian and employs numerical simulations to track the time evolution of quantum ergotropy, mutual information, and relative entropy.

**Model / system.** The system consists of $N$ two-level atoms coupled to a quantized single-mode cavity field, modeled by the Tavis-Cummings Hamiltonian. The initial state can be varied between pure, mixed, and partially entangled configurations, and the field is subjected to thermal photons.

**Key observables.** Quantum ergotropy (extractable work), multipartite quantum mutual information, and quantum relative entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($N=2$ to $5$), average thermal photon number ($ar{n}$), and the initial state preparation parameters ($	heta, p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed using the Tavis-Cummings model, and the initial state is assumed separable ($\hat{ho}(0) = \hat{ho}_A(0) \otimes \hat{ho}_{th}^F$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of ergotropy, mutual information, and relative entropy as a function of system size, thermal photon number, and initial state type.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Hamiltonian, defines central quantities (ergotropy, mutual information, relative entropy), presents the main numerical results concerning system size and temperature effects, and concludes by summarizing the role of correlations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the dynamics of quantum ergotropy in a multipartite system of two-level atoms interacting with a quantized field. The role of multipartite quantum mutual information and quantum relative entropy is analyzed to understand how correlations and the distinguishability between the passive state and the product of local states affect the extractable work. Numerical results for systems containing two to five atoms show that the maximum ergotropy does not increase linearly with the system size, while the intervals of zero ergotropy are gradually modified and suppressed for larger systems. We also study the effect of the average thermal photon number and different initial atomic states. Increasing the thermal photon number mainly reduces the relative-entropy contribution, whereas the initial state strongly influences both the magnitude and persistence of ergotropy. Among the considered states, the partially entangled state provides the largest and more persistent extractable work, while the mixed GHZ state gives the smallest ergotropy. These results highlight the importance of multipartite correlations, thermal effects, and state preparation in controlling quantum work extraction and may be useful for future studies of quantum batteries and quantum power.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates the use of Multimode Optical Parametric Amplification (MOPA) to characterize a large, 60-node spectral cluster state. By measuring spectral correlations, the authors confirm strong squeezing and genuine multipartite entanglement across the entire state. This establishes MOPA as a promising, scalable platform for advanced quantum information processing in the spectral domain.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly advances the experimental realization of high-dimensional quantum resources (spectral cluster states) and proposes a robust, loss-tolerant detection platform (MOPA) for continuous-variable quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently detecting spectral cluster states, which are crucial resources for measurement-based quantum computation using continuous-variable quantum information.

**Main result.** They successfully demonstrated the spectral multimode capability of Multimode Optical Parametric Amplification (MOPA) by characterizing a 60-node cluster state, confirming genuine multipartite entanglement across all nodes.

**Method.** The measurement relies on using MOPA as an all-optical analyzer to characterize the state by measuring spectral intensity correlations and reconstructing the input quadrature variances.

**Model / system.** The system involves generating and characterizing a broadband 60-node dual-rail continuous-variable cluster state using multimode squeezing via type-I collinear degenerate parametric down-conversion (PDC) in a BiBO crystal.

**Key observables.** Squeezing levels (up to -7.2 dB), reconstructed nullifier variances, and the violation of the van Loock–Furusawa criterion for entanglement verification.

**Important parameters / regimes.** 60-node state size, MOPA gain (G = 4.5 ± 0.2), and the spectral bandwidth (limited to 8.5 nm by dispersion).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The retrieval of quadrature variance is exact only if the overlap matrix is real, and the measurement is currently limited by spectral dispersion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup, the adjacency matrix of the 60-node state, measured spectral intensity correlations, and the reconstruction of nullifier variances across various bipartitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework for using MOPA for detection, presents the experimental characterization of the 60-node state, verifies entanglement using the vLF criterion, and concludes by establishing MOPA as a scalable platform.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multimode entangled states of light are key resources for high-dimensional photonic quantum technologies, particularly for continuous-variable quantum computing and quantum information processing. Exploiting their full capabilities requires detection strategies that can efficiently and simultaneously access a large number of modes and their quantum correlations. Multimode optical parametric amplification (MOPA) provides a promising approach by amplifying multiple quadratures prior to detection, rendering the measurement robust against subsequent optical losses while enabling broadband operation. Here, we demonstrate the spectral multimode capability of MOPA by simultaneously characterizing a broadband 60-node dual-rail continuous-variable cluster state. We reconstruct the cluster-state nullifiers across the spectral modes and observe nearly uniform squeezing ranging from -7.2 to -6.5 dB. Furthermore, by evaluating the van Loock-Furusawa criterion across all relevant bipartitions, we verify genuine multipartite entanglement throughout the 60-node state. These results establish MOPA as a promising platform for scalable high-dimensional quantum information processing in the spectral domain.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models high-fidelity entangling gates for dual-species neutral atom arrays using two-photon Rydberg excitation. It specifically addresses and mitigates the influence of laser crosstalk, a key challenge in scaling these quantum processors. The results show that fidelities exceeding 99.8% are achievable, paving the way for robust quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles a critical hardware-level limitation (crosstalk) in a leading platform for quantum computation (neutral atoms), providing concrete, high-fidelity gate protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve the fidelity of inter-species Rydberg gates in neutral atom arrays by modeling and suppressing the detrimental effects of laser crosstalk during two-photon driving.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that crosstalk can be suppressed to arbitrarily small levels, achieving gate fidelities greater than 99.8% using conservative parameters in an $^{85}	ext{Rb}/^{87}	ext{Rb}$ system.

**Method.** The study develops a detailed model incorporating crosstalk into the Hamiltonian and uses time-optimal gate design principles to engineer the pulse sequence.

**Model / system.** The system involves neutral atom arrays using dual-species isotopes ($^{85}	ext{Rb}/^{87}	ext{Rb}$) modeled as five-level systems. The dynamics are governed by a master equation incorporating the full Hamiltonian, including van der Waals interactions and laser driving fields.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity (target $>99.8\%$), unitary infidelity, and full infidelity including decoherence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Near-detuned regime, specific parameters for $^{85}	ext{Rb}$ and $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ (listed in Table I), and the gate time ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes global and overlapping laser fields, and the gate design is optimized using time-optimal gate approaches.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy level diagrams, the evolution of infidelity vs. time (comparing unitary vs. full), and the required phase modulations for the gate operation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical system and the problem of crosstalk, develops the comprehensive model and Hamiltonian, details the time-optimal gate design, presents the results showing high fidelity, and concludes by outlining future enhancement possibilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, neutral atom arrays have become known for their promising potential in quantum information processing. High-fidelity entangling operations are typically achieved by exciting atoms to high-lying Rydberg states through two-photon processes. To improve the performance of these systems at the hardware level, dual-species strategies have been applied to enhance readout, error correction, and reloading in neutral atom systems. However, the influence of laser crosstalk arising from two-photon excitation for dual-species systems has remained largely unexplored in the literature. Here, we develop a model that incorporates the influence of crosstalk in inter-species Rydberg gates with two-photon driving. We demonstrate that the influence of this crosstalk can be suppressed to arbitrarily small levels through appropriate gate design, with gate fidelities greater than $99.8\%$ achievable with conservative experimental parameters in the near-detuned case of an $^{85}$Rb/$^{87}$Rb system. We also outline how this fidelity can be further enhanced through improvements to the Rydberg excitation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines how quantum systems behave when suddenly quenched in the presence of attractive interactions, focusing on 1D Fermi gases with an impurity. Using the Bethe ansatz, the authors characterize the dynamic evolution, revealing distinct behaviors based on the total momentum. Crucially, they derive a robust relationship for the quantum flutter period linked directly to the impurity's chemical potential.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep theoretical insights into non-equilibrium dynamics in attractive quantum many-body systems, directly supporting experimental efforts using ultracold atoms to probe impurity interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quench dynamics and quantum flutter properties in one-dimensional Fermi gases with attractive interactions, an area lacking systematic theoretical treatment.

**Main result.** The paper establishes that the quantum flutter period is consistently determined by the chemical potential of the impurity particle, $\Delta E = 2\pi/|\mu_{\downarrow c}|$, and details distinct dynamic behaviors in weakly versus strongly attractive regimes.

**Method.** The research employs the exact Bethe ansatz solution method to simplify correlation matrix elements into finite sums, allowing for the calculation of time evolution of correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional ideal Fermi gas containing a single spin-down impurity fermion interacting attractively ($g<0$). The Hamiltonian is analyzed using the Bethe ansatz framework.

**Key observables.** Time-evolution of interspin two-body correlations, impurity momentum, and the characteristic quantum flutter (periodic oscillation) of the impurity momentum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The total momentum relative to the Fermi momentum, the attractive interaction strength ($g$), and the chemical potential of the impurity ($\mu_{\downarrow c}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Bethe ansatz for exact solutions and involves approximations such as the thermodynamic limit ($\Lambda 	o \infty$) in some derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the time evolution of the opposite-spin two-body correlation function for a specific total momentum ($Q=k_F$), showing correlation peaks and moving correlation holes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the problem by first introducing the Bethe ansatz solutions (Section 2), simplifying correlation functions (Section 3), analyzing quench dynamics via two-body correlations (Section 4), and finally discussing quantum flutter and revival phenomena (Section 5).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Impurity nonequilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems is a frontier subject in ultracold-atom physics, which helps uncover microscopic mechanisms of polaron and collective excitation phenomena. Although repulsive interacting systems have been extensively investigated, attractive-interaction dynamics lacks systematic studies owing to intricate couplings among different states. We study a one-dimensional ideal Fermi gas containing an attractive spin-down impurity with initial momentum and analyze time evolution of interspin two-body correlations and impurity momentum. Using exact Bethe ansatz solutions, we simplify correlation matrix elements to finite sums for efficient calculations of eigenstate occupations and long-time dynamical evolution. In the weakly attractive regime, bound-state features appear when the total momentum is below or equal to the Fermi momentum, while mixed oscillatory behaviors arise at larger total momentum. The strongly attractive regime shows locally bound-state dominated dynamics with localized correlation peaks and scattering-induced Friedel-like oscillations. We characterize quantum flutter, the periodic oscillation of impurity momentum, and obtain consistent critical chemical potential values of the impurity via four independent Bethe ansatz approaches. This work clarifies quench dynamics and quantum flutter under attractive interactions, improves the understanding of nonequilibrium quantum many-body properties, and provides theoretical support for relevant ultracold-atom impurity experiments.

</details>

In each subfigure, we show the normalized correlation matrix at the start of the simulation at t = 0.0µs (left column), in the approximate middle of the simulation t = 1.67µs (middle column) and at the end of the simulation at t = 3.0µs (right column).</sub>

**Summary.** This paper details the quantum simulation of the Heisenberg XXZ model using Rydberg atom arrays. The core innovation is using domain wall encoding to simulate the full XXZ model by mapping it onto an effective Ising Hamiltonian realizable on the hardware. The authors successfully validate this method by reproducing expected physical behavior across different quantum phases while rigorously accounting for realistic experimental noise sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of quantum simulation by demonstrating how to engineer complex Hamiltonians (like XXZ) on platforms (Rydberg atoms) whose native interactions might otherwise be limited, thereby expanding the accessible model space for quantum many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of simulating the Heisenberg XXZ model on quantum hardware by implementing a method that allows for the simulation of necessary coupling types beyond conventional ZZ-couplings.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrate the implementation of this simulation method on a Rydberg atom quantum device, reproducing the expected dynamical behavior across XY, Ising, and critical phases of the model.

**Method.** The simulation employs domain wall encoding to map the target XXZ spin chain Hamiltonian onto an effective Ising Hamiltonian, which is then realized using the native interactions of the Rydberg atom array.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a Rydberg atom array (e.g., QuEra's Aquila device) used as an analog quantum simulator. The target model is the Heisenberg XXZ chain, and the simulation relies on mapping its dynamics onto an effective Ising model via domain wall encoding.

**Key observables.** Site populations ($p_i$), normalized correlations ($\Gamma_{ij}/\max_{ij} \Gamma_{ij}$), and fidelity (state and classical).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Anisotropy ($\delta$) is probed across XY ($\delta=0.0$), isotropic ($\delta=1.0$), and Ising ($\delta=4.0$) regimes. Error sources include thermal motion, laser fluctuations, and readout errors.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that undesired interactions beyond next-nearest-neighbor are negligible, and the error model incorporates specific approximations for disorder and readout errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the zig-zag atom arrangement realizing required interactions, the mapping process from XXZ to Ising via domain wall encoding, and comparisons of fidelity and correlation functions across different error models and anisotropies.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical framework by first defining the required Hamiltonian, then detailing the domain wall encoding transformation, and finally implementing and analyzing the simulation results using a comprehensive error model that includes disorder and readout errors.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A major application of analog quantum hardware is quantum simulation, where a system evolves over time according to a given Hamiltonian. One challenge when implementing the Hamiltonian on a programmable quantum simulator is the availability of distinct coupling types, many of which go beyond the conventional ZZ-couplings found in Ising Hamiltonians that are naturally implemented by various platforms. In prior work, we have developed a method based on domain wall encoding to simulate one-dimensional spin chains using only Ising Hamiltonians, which would otherwise require XX+YY-couplings as well. Here, we implement the method on a commercially accessible Rydberg atom quantum device, and simulate the time evolution of the Heisenberg XXZ model with various anisotropies. We successfully probe the XY and Ising phases of the model and reproduce the qualitative behavior of several one- and two-body observables in both phases, as well as at the critical point. Our work augments the toolbox to simulate canonical quantum many-body systems on analog quantum simulators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a method to enhance the sensitivity of Mid-infrared photothermal measurements by utilizing quantum noise reduction. By generating and detecting twin beams via seeded OPA, the measurement noise is suppressed below the standard shot-noise limit. This quantum enhancement allows for lower probe powers, mitigating sample damage while maintaining or improving the signal-to-noise ratio.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, demonstrating a practical application of quantum noise reduction techniques (squeezing) to a complex, non-linear sensing modality (MIP).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve the sensitivity of Mid-infrared photothermal (MIP) measurements by reducing measurement noise below the fundamental shot-noise limit.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated sub-shot-noise MIP measurements using twin beams generated via seeded optical parametric amplification, achieving significant SNR enhancements over conventional coherent-beam detection.

**Method.** The technique relies on balanced detection of quantum-squeezed twin beams to suppress noise below the shot-noise level, which is then applied to the MIP measurement setup.

**Model / system.** The system uses seeded Optical Parametric Amplification (OPA) in a PPLN waveguide to generate twin beams. The MIP measurement itself involves using a modulated QCL as the pump source and probing the sample's refractive index change.

**Key observables.** Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR), Noise Reduction Factor (NRF), and the measured MIP signal power as a function of probe power.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter ($r_0$), optical loss (transmittances $T_a, T_b$), and the modulation frequency ($f_{mod}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes statistical independence of photocurrent and electronic noise, and that the noise reduction factor is robust against certain levels of optical loss when balanced detection is used.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the principle of sub-shot-noise measurement, compare RF noise power between twin beams and coherent beams, and show the MIP signal scaling for both quantum and classical probe sources.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for noise reduction in MIP measurements, detailing the generation and characterization of sub-shot-noise twin beams, and finally applying this quantum enhancement to the actual MIP sensing measurement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mid-infrared photothermal (MIP) measurement probes refractive-index changes induced by mid-infrared (MIR) absorption using visible or near-infrared light and has recently emerged as a powerful modality for MIR spectro-imaging in the life sciences. Reducing measurement noise allows the probe power to be lowered without compromising the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), thereby mitigating the risk of sample damage. However, even in the absence of classical noise, the SNR of conventional MIP measurements using a classical coherent probe is fundamentally limited by shot noise. Here, we demonstrate sub-shot-noise MIP measurement using twin beams generated through seeded optical parametric amplification. Balanced detection of the twin beams suppresses the noise by more than 5 dB below the shot-noise level established using a coherent beam. As a proof-of-principle demonstration, we measure the MIP signal from liquid water using balanced twin-beam detection and achieve an SNR enhancement of more than 2 dB over the balanced coherent-beam detection.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the time-bin characteristics of photon pairs generated using a PPLN waveguide resonator. By comparing measurements in multi-mode versus single-mode operation, the authors demonstrate that spectral filtering removes interference fringes and that the resulting time-bin profiles are dictated by the optical losses of the signal and idler photons. These findings are crucial for engineering pulsed quantum sources for future quantum networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the temporal characterization of entangled photon sources, which is fundamental for developing robust quantum communication protocols and quantum networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on characterizing the time-bin properties of degenerate photon pairs generated via parametric down-conversion (PDC) in a waveguide resonator.

**Main result.** The time-bin duration varies significantly based on the optical losses of the signal and idler photons, and the presence of spectral filtering (SRM) removes interference fringes seen in the multi-mode regime (MRM).

**Method.** The authors combine direct time-domain measurements of coincidence counts with theoretical simulations utilizing the two-dimensional Fourier transform of the Joint Spectral Intensity (JSI).

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Fabry-Pérot (FP) waveguide resonator made of periodically-poled Lithium Niobate (PPLN), used as the source for degenerate photon pairs in the telecom C-band.

**Key observables.** Time-bin durations, Joint Spectral Intensity (JSI), Joint Temporal Intensity (JTI), and the Coincidences-to-Accidentals Ratio (CAR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** 2 GHz bandwidth, sub-nanosecond pulse shapes, and the Free Spectral Range (FSR) of the resonator ($\approx 6.7 	ext{ GHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a Gaussian pump spectrum and treats the FP Resonator classically; simulation requires choosing linear loss parameters ($\alpha$) for qualitative agreement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the JSI, spectral marginals, JTI, and measured/simulated time bins for both multi-resonator mode (MRM) and single-resonator mode (SRM) operation, showing the disappearance of interference fringes upon filtering.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using JSI and 2D Fourier transforms, then presenting experimental measurements comparing the MRM and SRM regimes, and finally analyzing the resulting time-bin characteristics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We produce degenerate photon pairs called signal and idler via parametric down-conversion (PDC) in a Fabry-Pérot waveguide resonator having close to a 2GHz bandwidth in the telecom C-band. Our PDC source is based on a type-II periodically-poled Lithium Niobate waveguide with high-reflecting end facets forming the resonator. We measure the strength of the photon-pair correlation between signal and idler after selecting a single resonator mode from the multimode PDC emission. Thereafter, we investigate their sub-nanosecond long pulse shapes, in other words the time bins, with standard photon counting. We find that the time-bin durations vary significantly from each other, which can be predicted by the different optical losses of the cross-polarized signal and idler. Further, we simulate the temporal properties of signal and idler via the Fourier transform of their joint spectral characteristics. Our results deliver empiric insight about the time bins of photon pairs and are relevant for driving future quantum networks at the edge to narrow bandwidths but nevertheless in the pulsed regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the engineering of chirped and apodized nonlinear crystals, such as KTP, to create superior quantum light sources. By tailoring the crystal's nonlinear profile, the authors achieve unprecedented broadband emission while suppressing spectral ripples. This advancement provides a powerful tool for next-generation quantum spectroscopy and sensing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it details the engineering of quantum light sources—a crucial resource for quantum information processing and sensing protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing highly efficient, spectrally engineered, and bright quantum light sources necessary for advanced quantum sensing and spectroscopy applications.

**Main result.** The authors report that engineering chirped and apodized nonlinear crystals (caKTP) successfully generates broadband, flat-spectrum correlated photon pairs, enabling quantum spectroscopy over wide spectral ranges with shorter measurement times.

**Method.** The methodology involves theoretical simulation and experimental characterization of nonlinear processes like SPDC using tailored crystal geometries (chirping and apodization) to control the spectral output.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the nonlinear crystal, specifically KTP, which is engineered with a chirped and apodized nonlinear coefficient profile. The process relies on Type-0 Spontaneous Parametric Down-Conversion (SPDC) to generate correlated photon pairs.

**Key observables.** Broad spectral bandwidth, flat intensity distribution, high pair generation rate, and preservation of quantum correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinear crystal length (e.g., 2 mm vs 30 mm), pump wavelength (e.g., 532 nm), and the degree of chirp/apodization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes the ability to implement precise nonlinear coefficient engineering (chirping/apodization) and operates under conditions where quantum correlations are maintained despite high count rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the effect of nonlinear-chirp apodization on phase-matching spectra, showing broadband visible-MIR generation, and detail the experimental setup for source characterization.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the need for engineered quantum sources, detailing the theoretical basis of nonlinear-chirp apodization, presenting simulation results demonstrating bandwidth enhancement, and concluding with the practical advantages for quantum spectroscopy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum sources of light are of paramount importance for the development of photonic quantum technologies. The growing demand for bright, efficient, and spectrally engineered photonic sources for applications in quantum sensing, spectroscopy, and imaging has motivated the development of tailored nonlinear crystals. Here, we report on the development and characterization of chirped, apodized KTP crystals specifically engineered for quantum spectroscopy. Our source exhibits an unprecedented bandwidth in a highly non-degenerate configuration, demonstrating its potential to support quantum spectroscopy protocols over broad spectral ranges while enabling significantly shorter measurement times.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates a method to create noise-resilient qubits using superconducting Kerr oscillators driven by low-frequency charge signals. By exploiting the frequency dependence of the charge sensitivity, the system suppresses detrimental low-frequency noise while enabling fast quantum gates. This results in high-fidelity quantum operations suitable for quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides an experimental blueprint for building fault-tolerant quantum hardware by engineering the coupling mechanism to actively suppress environmental noise sources like 1/f noise.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing noise-resilient quantum processing schemes for superconducting qubits while minimizing hardware overhead, especially against low-frequency 1/f noise.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated dynamical protection for a dual-rail qubit, achieving low error rates ($1.5	imes10^{-4}$ after post-selection) using sub-GHz charge driving on Kerr oscillators.

**Method.** They exploit the quadratic frequency dependence of the charge sensitivity (AC Stark shift) to suppress low-frequency noise while maintaining strong coupling to microwave drives.

**Model / system.** The system uses superconducting Kerr oscillators (KOs) in the transmon regime, implemented in a planar circuit, and employs a dual-rail encoding scheme for logical control.

**Key observables.** KO population (reset/cooling), logical error per Clifford, Rabi oscillations, and the AC Stark shift measurement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sub-GHz charge drive frequency, coupling strength ($g/2\pi \sim 50–200 	ext{ MHz}$), and detuning ($\Delta/2\pi \sim 0.5–2 	ext{ GHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the quadratic scaling of the Stark shift, which suppresses low-frequency noise, and the use of single-photon loss as the dominant error channel for the dual-rail code.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental schematic, AC Stark shift measurements, pulse sequences for parametric reset/cooling, and Rabi oscillations for qubit control.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from demonstrating basic cooling/reset of a single KO using low-frequency driving, to implementing logical control and dynamical protection on a dual-rail qubit, and finally quantifying the resulting error rates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dynamical protection via strong driving can enable resilient quantum processing on imperfect physical hardware. However, the practical utility of such schemes is frequently limited by parasitic processes such as drive-induced dephasing and leakage. Here, we demonstrate that sub-GHz charge driving of superconducting Kerr oscillators (KOs) in the transmon regime circumvents this trade-off, simultaneously activating fast parametric interactions and protecting the encoded qubit from low-frequency noise. The key is the frequency dependence of the charge sensitivity: using the AC Stark shift as a probe, we find that the KO frequency sensitivity to a charge drive grows quadratically with both drive frequency and amplitude, making the oscillator weakly sensitive to $1/f$ charge noise yet strongly coupled to drives near 1~GHz. Exploiting this, we first demonstrate cooling and reset of the KO in 82~ns, to a residual population below $0.7\%$---lower than its $2.5\%$ steady-state thermal population. Next, using two KOs, we demonstrate logical control and dynamical protection of a dual-rail qubit with nearly fourfold erasure bias. Finally, using a single end-of-circuit erasure check, we achieve an error per Clifford of $5.6\times10^{-4}$, which falls to $1.5\times10^{-4}$ after post-selection, with 25~ns gates.

</details>

**Authors:** Nada Ali, Mark Hillery  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09022v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `QC/QI experiment` **4/5** · `quantum measurements` **2/5**

**Summary.** This paper explores the theoretical and experimental feasibility of teleporting a programmable controlled phase gate (CPHASE($	heta$)) between remote qubits using an intermediary. The authors show that the protocol works by encoding the phase information quantum mechanically and confirming this capability experimentally on a superconducting quantum processor. This validates a key primitive for building distributed quantum computation networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it demonstrates a practical, hardware-verified method for implementing complex quantum operations (like controlled phase gates) across physically separated nodes using quantum communication primitives, which is central to quantum networking and distributed quantum computing architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the protocol for teleporting a programmable two-qubit gate, specifically the CPHASE($	heta$) gate, between two remote parties (Alice and Bob) using an intermediary (Charlie). It compares this remote protocol against a direct implementation.

**Main result.** Both the remote, measurement-mediated protocol and the direct gate implementation successfully reproduce the expected $\cos 	heta$ and $\sin 	heta$ dependencies in measured correlation functions, confirming the programmable transfer of the phase gate.

**Method.** The authors analyze theoretical protocols involving entanglement and local corrections, and experimentally test the remote protocol on a superconducting quantum processor by measuring expectation values of Pauli operators as a function of the phase parameter $	heta$.

**Model / system.** The system involves qubits held by Alice, Bob, and Charlie, potentially augmented by auxiliary program states (qutrits or two-qubit states). Experimentally, the work is performed on the IBM Torino 133-qubit superconducting processor.

**Key observables.** Single-qubit expectation values ($\langle X_A angle$) and two-qubit correlators ($\langle Y_A Z_B angle$, $\langle Z_A Z_B angle$, $\langle X_A Y_B angle$) are measured as functions of the phase angle $	heta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The programmable phase angle $	heta$, the success probabilities of the state transfer protocols (e.g., $2/3$), and the coherence cost ratio ($V_r/V_d = 0.83$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocols rely fundamentally on the ability of Alice and Bob to perform local correction operations based on Charlie's measurement outcomes. The experimental fidelity is limited by circuit depth and decoherence compared to the direct gate.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum circuits for both the remote CPHASE($	heta$) protocol and the direct $R_{ZZ}(	heta)$ baseline. Measured data plots show the expected $\cos 	heta$ and $\sin 	heta$ fringes for the respective observables, confirming the gate's functionality.

**Paper structure.** The paper first discusses theoretical protocols for gate teleportation using various auxiliary systems (qutrits, program states). It then details the general theory of remote gate application based on group representations. Finally, it presents an experimental realization and comparison of the remote protocol against a direct gate implementation on superconducting hardware.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Alice and Bob each have a qubit, and they would like to perform a two-qubit gate on them with the assistance of Charlie. The action of this gate depends on a parameter, which either Charlie knows, will receive later as classical information, or is encoded in a program state. We discuss both the case in which Alice and Bob send Charlie their qubits, which he entangles with his state, and the case where Alice and Bob use teleportation to entangle their states with Charlie's state. All of these procedures depend on the ability of Alice and Bob to apply local correction operations that depend on the results of measurements that Charlie makes, and we discuss under what conditions such operations are possible.

</details>

### [Witnessing Quantum Bayesian Inference beyond Classical Learning](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09293v1)

**Authors:** Francesco Buscemi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09293v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `quantum measurements` **4/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.09293_figures/2610.09293_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Five species arranged in a cycle, with drawing proba- bilities uj. An edge connects neighboring species; hence, the probability of neighboring outcomes in two draws with re- placement is f(u) = 2 P4 j=0 ujuj+1, with indices understood modulo five.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that quantum Bayesian inference is more powerful than classical Bayesian learning by finding a mathematical constraint that classical models must obey but quantum models can violate. This separation is demonstrated using a five-outcome measurement on a single qubit, which violates a derived classical bound. The work establishes a fundamental difference in the expressive power of quantum vs. classical inference.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, model-independent witness to the non-classical nature of quantum inference, which has implications for how we model learning processes in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether quantum Bayesian retrodiction can generate predictions that are fundamentally incompatible with any explanation derived from classical Bayesian learning models.

**Main result.** Quantum Bayesian retrodiction is strictly more expressive than classical Bayesian learning, demonstrated by a sharp threshold: five-outcome measurements on a single qubit violate a necessary classical bound.

**Method.** The authors compare the joint probability table derived from quantum minimum-change principles against the constraints imposed by classical factorizations (e.g., $T=BB^T$).

**Model / system.** The comparison uses abstract probabilistic models involving latent variables (classical) versus quantum measurements (POVMs) on a single qubit, focusing on the joint probabilities of two successive draws.

**Key observables.** The joint probability table $T_{xy}$ of two successive draws, and the derived classical bound $B_5(T) \le 1/2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of outcomes ($n$), specifically the threshold between $n \le 4$ (classical realizable) and $n=5$ (quantum violation).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The classical explanation assumes the sampling law is fixed and unknown, while the quantum model uses quantum retrodiction based on minimum-change principles.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates five species in a cycle, showing the probability of neighboring outcomes in two draws with replacement, used to derive the classical bound.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mathematical frameworks for classical and quantum retrodiction, derives the necessary classical constraints using matrix factorization, and then constructs a specific quantum counterexample (the five-outcome qubit measurement) that violates these constraints.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can an agent's predictions reveal whether its reasoning is based on a classical or a quantum model? Using known results on matrix factorizations, here we show that quantum Bayesian retrodiction can produce one-step-ahead forecasts incompatible with every classical Bayesian explanation based on learning about a fixed but unknown sampling law from conditionally independent observations. The separation has a sharp threshold in the number of outcomes: for any measurement with at most four outcomes, the quantum predictions admit a classical realization, irrespective of the prior state and the finite Hilbert-space dimension, whereas a five-outcome measurement on a single qubit already violates an explicit classical bound. This bound holds for arbitrary classical latent states, prior probabilities, and sampling laws, and involves only the agent's initial and updated predictions. Thus, in this setting, quantum Bayesian retrodiction is strictly more expressive than classical Bayesian learning, and this difference can be witnessed without observing or specifying the agent's internal description.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates a method to engineer complex hybrid quantum states in a spin-photon-phonon system by combining two effects: double squeezing, which enhances correlation generation, and cavity Kerr nonlinearity, which acts as a selective filter. This combination allows researchers to control the final composition of the quantum state, revealing rich superradiant phases and underlying symmetries.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it combines multiple advanced topics in AMO physics: cavity QED (photon-matter coupling), quantum optics (squeezing, Kerr nonlinearity), and open quantum systems (dissipation), to achieve state engineering.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the limitation where the composition of a multipartite quantum state is intrinsically locked to the microscopic process generating its correlations, aiming to selectively reconfigure the state composition.

**Main result.** The combination of double squeezing and Kerr filtering enables the controllable engineering of Kerr-filtered hybrid quantum states, leading to distinct superradiant phases (SR I and SR II) characterized by a fourfold symmetry-related manifold.

**Method.** The analysis uses Bogoliubov transformations, master equations for open systems, and mean-field approximations to study the ground-state phase diagram and phase-space structure.

**Model / system.** The system is a tripartite spin-photon-phonon setup involving an alkaline-earth atom coupled to a cavity and trapped in a 1D harmonic trap. The dynamics are governed by a tripartite Hamiltonian modified by Kerr nonlinearity.

**Key observables.** Bosonic order parameters, Fock distributions ($P_c(n), P_p(n)$), subsystem von Neumann entropies, and the joint cavity-phonon Husimi-Q distribution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Double squeezing strength, Kerr nonlinearity strength, and system decay rates ($\kappa_a, \kappa_b, \gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the Kerr nonlinearity selectively modifies the photonic component without disrupting the underlying tripartite conversion channel, and uses leading-order self-consistent approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ground-state phase diagram (N, WSR, SR I, SR II) and show the evolution of the joint Husimi-Q distribution, revealing the fourfold symmetry structure in the SR II regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the problem of state composition control, detailing the physical model and Hamiltonian, analyzing the interplay between squeezing and Kerr effects in the ground-state phase diagram, and finally examining the phase-space signatures and robustness against dissipation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multipartite interactions provide a unique route to generate hybrid quantum states, yet their intrinsically weak nonlinear coupling limits the controllable manipulation of the resulting correlations. Here we show that cooperative double squeezing and cavity Kerr nonlinearity together enable controllable engineering of Kerr-filtered hybrid states in a tripartite spin-photon-phonon system. Double squeezing exponentially enhances the spin-mediated photon-phonon pair-generation channel, whereas the Kerr interaction selectively reshapes the photonic component while maintaining the underlying tripartite conversion process. This mechanism reconstructs the composition of the hybrid excitation and gives rise to a fourfold phase-space manifold associated with spontaneous $Z_{2}^{(a)}\times Z_{2}^{(b)}$ parity breaking. Our results reveal a strategy for engineering hybrid quantum states by combining interaction amplification with mode-selective nonlinear filtering.

</details>
