**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes stimulated emission from a three-level artificial atom coupled to a waveguide. By solving the system's dynamics, the authors demonstrate that the third level strongly modifies the scattering process, suppressing emission compared to a simple two-level model. Crucially, they predict the emergence of photon bunching correlations in the reflected field, offering a diagnostic tool for characterizing the emitter's internal structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it explores how higher-level structure (the third level) modifies fundamental quantum optical processes like stimulated emission and photon correlation in solid-state quantum emitters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper theoretically investigates stimulated emission—the scattering of a single-photon pulse by an excited three-level emitter (3LE) in a 1D waveguide.

**Main result.** The presence of the third level significantly suppresses stimulated emission for low anharmonicity (like transmons) but can induce novel correlation effects, such as photon bunching, in the reflected field.

**Method.** The study employs the real-space formalism, solving coupled differential equations derived from the time-dependent Schrödinger equation for the system's probability amplitudes.

**Model / system.** The system models a quantum three-level ladder system coupled to a one-dimensional open waveguide, closely mimicking artificial atoms like transmons.

**Key observables.** Probability amplitudes for the excited state ($|fangle$), single-photon amplitudes ($eta_{R/L}$), and two-photon correlation functions ($P_{RR}, P_{LL}, P_{RL}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low relative anharmonicity ($|\alpha_r| < 4\%$), incident pulse spectral width ($\Delta\omega$), and decay rates ($\Gamma_1, \Gamma_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes photon indistinguishability and is restricted to the two-excitation subspace.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the spatial distribution of two-photon states for 3LE vs. TLS, showing bunching in 3LE and antibunching in TLS, and illustrate the dependence of scattering probabilities on anharmonicity and pulse width.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formalism by deriving coupled differential equations for various probability amplitudes, solves these equations for specific pulse shapes (exponential), and then analyzes the resulting probabilities to compare 3LE dynamics against the standard two-level system (TLS) benchmark.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work is devoted to a theoretical study of the interaction of a quantum three-level ladder system with a continuous electromagnetic field in a one-dimensional open waveguide. We consider a situation closely resembling stimulated emission - the scattering of a single-photon exponential pulse by an excited three-level emitter. Using the real-space formalism, we obtain an analytical expression for the wavefunction of our system. We show that, for the low anharmonicity of a three-level system inherent to a transmon (the most common type of artificial atom), the influence of the third level can have a significant effect on the system's behavior. Namely, the presence of the third level largely suppresses the stimulated emission effect if the relative anharmonicity is lower than $| α_r | < 4\%$. However, for a three-level system, it is possible to obtain correlation effects such as photon bunching in the reflected field, which can also be 'switched off' by tuning the incident photon parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops rigorous, operational benchmarks to quantify quantum-state transfer efficiency between a cavity photon mode and a magnon mode. It demonstrates that standard strong-coupling criteria based on spectroscopy are insufficient predictors of actual transfer performance. The results provide quantitative guidelines for controlling thermal noise to ensure high-fidelity quantum information transfer in cavity magnonics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial quantitative metrics for assessing the viability of quantum interfaces, showing that simple spectral measurements are insufficient for guaranteeing high-fidelity quantum information transfer in realistic, noisy environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish operational benchmarks for quantum-state transfer from an intracavity photon mode to a magnon mode, moving beyond simple spectroscopic strong-coupling criteria.

**Main result.** Conventional linewidth-based strong-coupling criteria do not guarantee quantum-transfer performance; entanglement or squeezing can survive below these spectroscopic thresholds if noise is low enough.

**Method.** The authors analyze the system dynamics using open-system master equations and derive time-dependent quantum channels, comparing fidelity and entanglement survival against theoretical benchmarks.

**Model / system.** The system is a passive, linear cavity–magnon system coupled to independent Markovian thermal reservoirs, described by an interaction Hamiltonian and analyzed via Langevin equations.

**Key observables.** Phase-corrected average fidelity ($F_{avg}$), reference–magnon entanglement survival, and the presence of sub-vacuum magnon fluctuations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coherent transmissivity ($\eta$), output-referred thermal population ($
u$), and the coupling strength ($g$) relative to cavity/magnon decay rates ($\kappa, \gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is treated as a passive, linear coupling, and the thermal reservoirs are assumed to be independent and Markovian.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the three transfer tasks, steady-state cavity occupation vs. bath temperature, spectroscopic thresholds, and time evolution of cavity occupation.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the physical system and the limitations of spectroscopic criteria to deriving quantitative benchmarks based on fidelity, entanglement, and squeezing survival, culminating in task-dependent performance metrics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Normal-mode splitting reveals coherent cavity--magnon hybridization but does not certify finite-time quantum-state transfer. We describe quantum transfer in a passive, linear cavity--magnon system coupled to thermal reservoirs as a thermal-loss channel specified by its coherent transmissivity $η$ and output-referred thermal population $ν$. For single-rail qubits, we calculate the phase-corrected average fidelity and compare it with the deterministic measure-and-prepare benchmark of $2/3$. A Bell pair shared by the cavity and an isolated reference retains reference--magnon entanglement if and only if $η>ν$. An input squeezed vacuum of strength $r$ yields sub-vacuum magnon fluctuations if and only if $ν<η(1-e^{-2r})/2$. When the initial magnon and both reservoirs have the same occupation, an analytically determined peak-transmissivity time optimizes all three tasks. These task-dependent benchmarks show that a conventional linewidth-based strong-coupling criterion does not guarantee quantum-transfer performance. Conversely, entanglement or squeezing can survive below that spectroscopic reference when the added noise is sufficiently low. These benchmarks provide quantitative targets for transfer timing and thermal-noise control in intracavity-to-magnon quantum interfaces.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models energy transfer in Photosystem II using a detailed molecular junction approach, incorporating cotunneling effects. By quantifying non-equilibrium energy using ergotropy and energetic capacitance, the authors reveal how electron transport redistributes extractable energy across different molecular sites. The findings highlight the interplay between microscopic quantum transport mechanisms and macroscopic thermodynamic energy ordering.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of ergotropy and energetic capacitance to quantify non-equilibrium energy flow in a complex, open quantum system like a photosynthetic reaction center is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and quantum thermodynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the inclusion of cotunneling affects the passive-state ordering and the extractable non-equilibrium energy within a Photosystem II reaction center model.

**Main result.** Cotunneling redistributes the extractable non-equilibrium energy among different molecular components, and the analysis shows that thermodynamic smoothness can mask underlying microscopic state rearrangements.

**Method.** The authors employ a Nakajima-Zwanzig projection-operator formalism to model the system dynamics, quantifying state ordering using ergotropy and energetic capacitance.

**Model / system.** The system is a Photosystem II reaction center modeled as a 13-state nonequilibrium molecular junction, incorporating excitonic, charge-transfer, and cotunneling pathways.

**Key observables.** Ergotropy (extractable work), energetic capacitance, and steady-state populations of various electronic states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coulomb interaction energy (U), transition rates ($\Gamma$, $\omega$), and the degree of non-equilibrium excitation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis treats cotunneling via a microscopically constructed doubly reduced acceptor state and uses a projection-operator approach to derive the master equation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate steady-state population maps as a function of Coulomb interaction energy and transition rates, alongside uncertainty analyses of ergotropy and capacitance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the kinetic model, then introducing cotunneling, quantifying non-equilibrium energy using ergotropy/capacitance, and finally analyzing how these quantities change under varying interaction strengths and rates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how cotunneling affects passive-state ordering in a Photosystem II reaction center modelled as a nonequilibrium molecular junction. We use a full pigment excitation manifold, with excitonic and charge-transfer rates computed within a Nakajima-Zwanzig formalism using realistic spectral densities. Cotunneling is introduced through a microscopically constructed doubly reduced acceptor state obtained from relevant quinone-side two-electron configurations, while photoexcitation is allowed into the full excitonic manifold. Passive-state permutations are quantified through ergotropy and energetic capacitance. We find that thermodynamic smoothness in ergotropy and energetic capacitance can conceal microscopic state rearrangements. Cotunneling redistributes the extractable nonequilibrium energy among the quinone acceptor, the donor-pheophytin radical pair, and excitonic states with strong contributions from pigments on the inactive branch. At high Coulomb interaction, cotunneling produces no passive-state reordering. Piecewise-linear relations among ergotropy, energetic capacitance, and mean energy further reveal the population reorderings generated by cotunneling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced variational polaron master equation to model non-Markovian dynamics in the spin-boson system. It overcomes major theoretical hurdles by incorporating the effects of arbitrary initial states and general observables into the master equation framework. The resulting formalism offers a highly efficient method to study complex open quantum dynamics across various coupling regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, generalized, and computationally efficient theoretical tool for simulating open quantum systems, which is crucial for understanding decoherence and relaxation in quantum materials and light-matter interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Accurately describing the non-Markovian dynamics of open quantum systems, particularly when environmental coupling is strong or when initial states are complex.

**Main result.** The authors developed a unified, computationally efficient variational polaron master equation formalism that correctly incorporates inhomogeneous terms arising from transformed initial states and general observables.

**Method.** The approach uses an extended auxiliary Liouville space to convert complex integro-differential equations into solvable coupled ordinary differential equations, allowing for the unified treatment of coherence and population dynamics.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the quantum spin-boson model, which describes a two-level system coupled to a bosonic environment. The Hamiltonian includes system, bath, and interaction terms, and the dynamics are governed by a master equation.

**Key observables.** Population dynamics ($\langle\sigma_z(t)angle$), coherence dynamics ($\langle\sigma_x(t)angle$, $\langle\sigma_y(t)angle$), and trace-distance analysis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tunneling amplitudes, bath temperatures, and system-bath coupling strengths, analyzed across weak, intermediate, and strong coupling regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism extends previous methods by explicitly handling inhomogeneous terms from transformed initial states and general observables, overcoming limitations in previous variational polaron approaches.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 compares the variational polaron results with numerically exact HEOM calculations for population and coherence dynamics across various parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the model Hamiltonian and master equation, through deriving the variational polaron transformation, and finally extending the framework to incorporate transformed initial states and general observables, benchmarking against HEOM.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the effects of initial states on the dynamics of a quantum spin-boson model using an efficient variational polaron master equation approach. The transformation of a factorized system-environment initial state into the variational polaron frame introduces inhomogeneous terms in the master equation, essential for accurately describing non-Markovian dynamics beyond the weak coupling regime. To efficiently account for these effects, we formulate the dynamics in an extended auxiliary Liouville space, converting the resulting integro-differential equations into coupled ordinary differential equations. We further introduce a simplified variational polaron technique for computing the time evolution of expectation values of general observables, including those that do not commute with the polaron transformation, overcoming a major limitation of previous approaches and enabling the unified treatment of coherence and population dynamics. The effects of transformed initial states generated from arbitrary factorized initial states are systematically characterized through expectation values and trace-distance analysis, providing insight into the associated bath-relaxation dynamics over a broad range of tunneling amplitudes, temperatures, and coupling strengths. Comparison with the weak-coupling master equation reveals a delay phenomenon associated with multi-bath-quanta processes, pronounced at intermediate coupling strengths and tunneling amplitudes. We further show that coherent superposition initial states induce stronger nonequilibrium effects than localized states, with inhomogeneous terms playing a crucial role in the short-time dynamics. The proposed formalism applies to both undriven and resonantly driven spin-boson models, providing a computationally efficient framework for open quantum-system dynamics with arbitrary factorized initial states and general physical observables.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes non-reciprocal SSH chains to understand how internal scattering affects external signal transmission. It develops theoretical tools to separate the contributions from graph path structure, coherent port response, and dynamic settling. The findings offer clear criteria for predicting local transmission enhancement based on system parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, channel-specific criteria for interpreting internal scattering effects in open, non-Hermitian quantum systems, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how enhanced internal scattering in non-reciprocal lattices translates into measurable transmission between fixed external ports.

**Main result.** The leading behavior of the end-to-end kernel is determined by the signed sum of shortest directed paths, and local enhancement can be predicted by the coefficient $\chi$ in the weak-insertion regime.

**Method.** The analysis uses resolvent techniques, strong-insertion expansions, and similarity transformations to relate the insertion kernel $K_p$ to the coherent transmission intensity $T$.

**Model / system.** The model is a non-reciprocal Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chain incorporating staggered gain and loss, analyzed using non-Hermitian Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Coherent transmission intensity ($T$), insertion kernel ($K_p$), and the local predictive coefficient ($\chi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The gain/loss parameter ($\gamma$), the chain length ($N$), and the degree of non-reciprocity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fixed frequency baselines and assumes the separation of graph-path structure, coherent port response, and dynamical establishment.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the distance scaling of the end-to-end insertion kernel $|(K_p)_{B N-1, A0}|$ versus $\gamma/J$ for various chain lengths, confirming power-law decay.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by relating the insertion kernel to path sums in the strong-insertion limit, analyzes the weak-insertion limit using the coefficient $\chi$, and finally separates the total response into three distinct physical contributions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-reciprocal lattices offer a flexible setting for directional wave transport, raising the question of how enhanced internal scattering translates into transmission between fixed external ports. We address this question for non-reciprocal Su--Schrieffer--Heeger (SSH) chains with staggered gain and loss by relating the reference-dependent insertion kernel $K_p=VG_pV$ to the coherent transmission intensity $T$. On a fixed finite graph, a strong-insertion expansion gives a contribution proportional to $γ^{1-d}$, whose coefficient is the signed sum of shortest directed paths of length $d$. When this sum is nonzero, it sets the leading behavior independently of fixed diagonal losses; when it cancels, those losses can enter the first surviving higher-order term. For a fixed cross-sublattice endpoint channel, transmission has a strictly negative slope at every nonzero differentiable stationary point of the kernel magnitude. In the weak-insertion regime, the reference-system coefficient $χ=-\mathrm{Re}\,\mathrm{Tr}[(G_{0p}Z)^2]$ predicts the initial transmission change for symmetric SSH ports, and $χ>0$ together with a strictly stable baseline guarantees local stable enhancement. Finite-chain calculations and driven dynamics connect these results to observable response and distinguish asymptotic convergence from transient settling. A strictly dissipative short-chain example exhibits local enhancement without net power gain. Together, these results separate graph-path structure, coherent port response, and dynamical establishment, providing channel-specific criteria for interpreting internal scattering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that microwave shielding in polar molecules creates a powerful, tunable interaction resource for cavity QED. This interaction simultaneously enhances photon blockade by increasing spectral anharmonicity and protects quantum states, such as Bell pairs, by suppressing energy loss to the cavity. The findings establish a unified control mechanism for nonlinear optics and quantum state preservation in molecular systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the frontier of cavity QED by showing how external fields (microwaves) can engineer internal molecular interactions to control fundamental quantum phenomena like photon statistics and coherence protection, which is highly relevant for quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate how microwave-shielded interactions in polar molecules can serve as a unified resource for engineering strong photon blockade and protecting fragile quantum states within a cavity Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) platform.

**Main result.** The anisotropic, interaction-enhanced spectral anharmonicity induced by microwave shielding provides a unified mechanism that simultaneously achieves strong photon blockade and protects molecular Bell states from cavity dissipation.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving an effective Hamiltonian based on projecting the dipole-dipole interaction onto the dressed molecular ground state, followed by solving the dynamics using the Lindblad master equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a cavity-QED setup coupling two microwave-shielded polar molecules to a single optical cavity mode. The key interaction resource is the anisotropic van der Waals-like repulsion engineered via microwave dressing of the molecular ground state.

**Key observables.** Photon blockade ($g^{(2)}(0) < 1$), spectral anharmonicity, negative longitudinal spin correlations ($C_{zz}$), and fidelity decay rate of a prepared Bell state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Microwave Rabi frequency ($\Omega_m$), cavity decay rate ($\kappa$), molecular decay rate ($\gamma$), and the relative molecular separation ($r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the strong-microwave regime where weak optical couplings can be neglected, and that the effective potential derived from second-order virtual couplings is accurate.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup, the derived effective potential $V_{	ext{eff}}(r)$ showing its dependence on molecular separation, and plots demonstrating $g^{(2)}$ and spin correlations as a function of detuning and molecular configuration.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical platform and the role of microwave dressing. It then derives the effective interaction potential, using this potential to analyze the spectral structure, which leads to the demonstration of enhanced photon blockade. Finally, it extends this interaction resource to show the protection of quantum states like Bell states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Microwave shielding has recently emerged as a powerful tool for engineering interactions in ultracold polar molecules, yet its potential for controlling cavity quantum electrodynamics remains largely unexplored. Here, we investigate a molecular cavity quantum electrodynamics platform in which two microwave-shielded polar molecules are coupled to a single optical cavity mode and demonstrate that shielding-induced interactions provide a unified mechanism for both photon blockade and Bell-state protection. The anisotropic interaction reshapes the few-excitation spectrum by enhancing its anharmonicity, thereby suppressing multiphoton transitions and improving the single-photon purity by more than three orders of magnitude. The enhanced blockade is accompanied by the emergence of negative longitudinal spin correlations, revealing the interaction-induced suppression of simultaneous molecular excitations. We further show that the photon statistics are highly sensitive to the relative molecular configuration, with positional variations on the scale of the relative zero-point fluctuation substantially modifying the blockade performance. Beyond few-photon nonlinear optics, the same interaction protects an initially prepared molecular Bell state by dispersively decoupling molecular excitations from the lossy cavity mode, thereby suppressing cavity-mediated dissipation and slowing the fidelity decay. Our results establish microwave-shielded interactions as a unified interaction resource for engineering few-photon nonlinearities and protecting quantum states in molecular cavity-QED systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores deep thermalization in free-fermion systems by studying the ensemble of pure states remaining in a subsystem after measurements on its environment. The key finding is that in the thermodynamic limit, the statistics of these conditional states are universal, described by a fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble. This extends thermalization theory to account for the full statistics of quantum measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, non-equilibrium generalization of thermalization concepts, moving beyond just the reduced density matrix to characterize the statistics of conditional pure states, which is highly relevant for open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates deep thermalization, specifically how the universal ensemble of pure states in a subsystem emerges after local measurements on its environment.

**Main result.** The partial information about local conserved charges becomes negligible in the thermodynamic limit, leading to a universal description governed by a fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble.

**Method.** The authors analyze the projected ensemble by considering the first and higher moments of the conditional pure states resulting from projective measurements on the environment.

**Model / system.** The system is a free-fermion chain governed by a Gaussian integrable Hamiltonian, bipartitioned into a subsystem A and an environment B.

**Key observables.** The projected ensemble, characterized by its correlation matrix and its moments, and the reduced density matrix $ho_A$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The thermodynamic limit ($L_B 	o \infty$) and the late-time limit ($t 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system Hamiltonian is Gaussian and integrable, and that the partial information about conserved charges becomes irrelevant in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the process: an initial Gaussian state evolves, is measured on the bath B, and the resulting ensemble approaches a Gaussian Scrooge ensemble whose first moment matches the reduced GGE.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the measurement process, analyzing the role of local conserved charges, and deriving the universal form of the projected ensemble (the Scrooge ensemble) in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Deep thermalization indicates the emergence of universal ensembles of pure states in a subsystem after measuring its environment. We investigate this phenomenon in a free-fermion chain using local measurements that preserve Gaussianity and break particle-number conservation. Although the measurement outcomes provide partial information about local conserved charges within a finite subsystem, we argue that this information becomes negligible in the thermodynamic limit. This effective non-revelation underlies the emergence of a fermionic Gaussian Scrooge ensemble, determined by the subsystem's generalized Gibbs ensemble. We construct this universal ensemble directly at the subsystem level and provide numerical evidence that it describes the late-time projected ensemble at finite temperature. Our results extend the description of thermalization in non-interacting integrable systems beyond the reduced density matrix to universal statistics of conditional pure states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that a Chern insulator can be realized in a static lattice solely by combining on-site loss and non-reciprocal couplings, bypassing the need for external magnetic fields. The mechanism involves the slow-decaying mode of a chiral non-Hermitian molecule generating an effective synthetic magnetic flux. This establishes non-Hermitian cooperation as a powerful new resource for topological engineering in open systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a general theoretical framework for engineering topological phases using only intrinsic non-Hermitian couplings, which is highly relevant for designing open quantum systems and non-reciprocal devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a fundamentally new paradigm for inducing Chern topology in a static lattice without relying on external magnetic fields, synthetic gauge potentials, or pre-existing Hermitian topology.

**Main result.** The synergy between on-site loss and non-reciprocal coupling is sufficient to open a topological gap and generate robust chiral edge states with a quantized Chern number.

**Method.** The mechanism is analyzed by projecting the full Hamiltonian onto the dynamically dominant mode of a non-Hermitian chiral molecule, effectively mapping the system to a synthetic Hofstadter model.

**Model / system.** The model uses a static lattice structure containing four non-Hermitian sites, characterized by on-site loss ($-i\gamma$) and non-reciprocal couplings ($\kappa e^{\pm \alpha}$). The system is analyzed via its Bloch Hamiltonian, which is block-form.

**Key observables.** Quantized Chern number (C), chiral edge states, and the effective synthetic magnetic flux ($\pi/2$ per plaquette).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coexistence regime ($\gamma 
eq 0$ and $\alpha 
eq 0$) is crucial; individual non-Hermitian effects are insufficient.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a projection reduction technique that eliminates fast-decaying degrees of freedom to isolate the slowest-decaying, dynamically dominant mode.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lattice structure, the spectral analysis of the chiral molecule, the resulting effective Hofstadter unit cell, and the projected band structures showing gap opening only in the coexistence regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of field-free topology, then details the Hamiltonian structure involving loss and non-reciprocity. The core of the work involves analyzing the chiral molecule's dynamics and projecting the system to reveal the effective synthetic flux, culminating in the calculation of the non-zero Chern number.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional Chern insulators rely on breaking time-reversal symmetry via magnetic fields or synthetic gauge fields. Here we introduce a fundamentally different paradigm: a static lattice without any gauge field becomes a Chern insulator solely through the cooperation of two non-Hermitian ingredients: on-site loss and non-reciprocal coupling. Neither effect alone suffices; only their synergy opens a topological gap and gives rise to robust chiral edge states with a nonzero Chern number. The underlying mechanism originates from a non-Hermitian chiral molecule formed by the lossy sites, whose dynamically dominant mode carries an orbital angular momentum that effectively generates a synthetic flux after the fast-decaying degrees of freedom are eliminated. Our results establish non-Hermitian cooperation as a versatile resource for inducing Chern topology without external fields or temporal modulations, opening a new route to topological phases in open classical and quantum systems.

</details>

### [When the coupler is the channel: an AND condition on cavity photon-number means](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09103v1)

**Authors:** Yueying Wu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09103v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `Full counting statistics` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `Tavis-Cummings & cavity-many-emitter` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `interference shaping light` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.09103_figures/2610.09103_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Analytical predictions, not experimental data. (a) Mean photon numbers for the two-pump input 11 as a function of pulse area Θ. The other three inputs retain both means at 1/2. The marked pulse area π/4 produces (1, 0). (b) Exact finite-block failure probability from Eq. (12), with M independent repetitions per block, τ = 1/8, and ∆= 1/2. The dashed curve is the Hoeffding upper bound, capped at the trivial bound 3/4. These are performance curves for the interacting protocol, not finite-sample Bell bounds.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates a physical mechanism using coupled optical cavities to realize an exact 'AND' logic gate based on mean photon number measurements. By engineering a controlled beam-splitter interaction, the authors show that the system's mean output perfectly reflects the logical AND operation, surpassing the limits imposed by classical no-signalling theories, all without requiring entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, controllable quantum optical realization of a non-classical correlation (the AND gate) using mean photon number measurements, which is highly relevant for quantum communication protocols and resource quantification in cavity QED.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates realizing an exact 'AND condition' on the mean photon numbers of two coupled cavities, meaning the means are equal if at least one control is off, and unequal only if both controls are on.

**Main result.** The authors provide an elementary construction using a two-pump beam-splitter interaction that achieves this AND condition exactly at the mean level, and they show this resource does not require entanglement.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical construction based on controlled unitary evolution and compares the achievable mean response against the bounds set by no-signalling distributions.

**Model / system.** The physical system consists of two cavity modes (A and B) coupled via a two-pump beam-splitter interaction, described by a controlled Hamiltonian $H_{xy}(t) = \hbar xy g(t)(a^\dagger b + b^\dagger a)$.

**Key observables.** Conditional mean photon numbers ($\mu_{xy}^A, \mu_{xy}^B$), the ideal population-level score ($\omega_\mu$), and the no-signalling score ($\omega_{NS}^\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction strength $\Theta$ (pulse area) and the input controls $x, y \in \{0, 1\}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on mean values and provides a finite-sample counting protocol, though the resource is shown to be achievable even with coherent product states.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(a) illustrates the mean photon numbers for the $xy=1$ input as a function of pulse area $\Theta$. Figure 1(b) compares the finite-block failure probability against the Hoeffding upper bound.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of the AND condition, presents the physical model and the ideal construction achieving $\omega_\mu=1$, derives the no-signalling bound ($\omega_{NS}^\mu = 3/4$), and concludes by showing the resource is realizable without entanglement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can two cavities have equal mean photon numbers whenever at least one of two binary controls is off, and unequal means only when both controls are on? We give an elementary construction using a two-pump beam-splitter interaction whose strength is proportional to the product of the controls. A phase-coherent single-excitation state yields mean occupations $(1/2,1/2)$ for inputs $00$, $01$, and $10$, and $(1,0)$ for input $11$. This realizes the desired AND condition exactly at the level of population means. It also makes the communication resource explicit: the interaction acts after the inputs are supplied and changes local statistics with the remote input. For any no-signalling distribution with finite means, the corresponding exact-mean score is at most $3/4$ under uniform inputs. We give a robust consistency inequality, a finite-sample counting protocol, and a coherent-state realization showing that this mean response does not require entanglement. The construction provides a concrete example for distinguishing controlled interactions from nonlocal-game resources.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes coupled quantum critical states in a two-site quantum circuit simulator. Using advanced techniques like NRG and Bosonization, the authors map out the phase diagram, showing that while individual critical points persist, strong inter-site coupling ultimately drives the system into a conventional Fermi Liquid state. This work provides a detailed understanding of quantum criticality in tunable mesoscopic conductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of quantum critical points and their stability under coupling is highly relevant to understanding emergent quantum phases in artificial quantum materials and mesoscopic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how coupled quantum critical states, specifically those arising from the two-channel-Kondo (2CK) point, behave when subjected to inter-site coupling.

**Main result.** Weak coupling preserves the individual critical points, but increasing coupling drives a quantum phase transition from a Non-Fermi Liquid (NFL) state to a screened Fermi Liquid (FL) state.

**Method.** The research employs a combination of theoretical techniques, including Bosonization, Quantum Brownian Motion, and Numerical Renormalization Group (NRG) calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid metal-semiconductor quantum circuit simulator comprising two coupled metallic islands, modeled by a Hamiltonian incorporating continuum electron spectra, tunneling (QPC), and charging energies.

**Key observables.** Impurity entropy (Simp(T)), local susceptibilities ($\chi_{loc}^{s,u}(T)$), and transport/conductance exponents ($\eta, 
u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inter-site coupling strength ($t_C$), island-lead coupling ($t$), and gate-tuned filling ($	ext{Ng}_\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the charge dynamics onto pseudospin-$rac{1}{2}$ degrees of freedom and analyzing the system in both Kondo and quasiballistic limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the circuit schematic, NRG results showing entropy and susceptibilities, and a phase diagram mapping out different quantum regimes based on coupling ratios and gate detuning.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical circuit and Hamiltonian, applying NRG and Bosonization methods to analyze the crossover between decoupled 2CK states, intermediate NFL regimes, and the final screened FL state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid metal-semiconductor quantum circuits offer a controlled setting for realizing and probing strongly correlated quantum matter. A largely open question that may be addressed by such simulators is whether coupled critical states can combine into new non-Fermi liquid (NFL) phases. Inspired by the recent realization of a two-site charge-Kondo circuit [W. Pouse \textit{et al.}, \href{https://www.nature.com/articles/s41567-022-01905-4}{Nat. Phys. \textbf{19}, 492 (2023)}], we uncover the fate of coupled Kondo anyons emerging from the two-channel-Kondo critical point at each site. Bosonization and quantum Brownian motion methods yield the full quasiballistic phase diagram and low-temperature transport properties, while numerical renormalization group calculations resolve the complementary weak-coupling limit. Together, these provide a unified description, and reveal robust NFL phases with continuously tunable transport and susceptibility exponents. Stronger inter-site coupling ultimately produces a screened Fermi liquid.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work demonstrates how to overcome the limitation of mean-field interactions washing out nonlinear features in matter-wave interferometry. By applying a precisely engineered momentum anti-chirp to a Bose-Einstein condensate, the authors predict the realization of matter-wave bistability and high-contrast resonance spectra. This opens pathways for new quantum technologies like matter-wave switching and high-precision velocity sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of using BECs for high-precision quantum measurements by providing a specific, controllable technique (momentum chirping) to counteract mean-field decoherence effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is observing nonlinear matter-wave features, such as bistability, in interferometry experiments using Bose-Einstein condensates (BECs) because mean-field interactions typically broaden and wash out these resonances.

**Main result.** The authors theoretically show that engineering a momentum anti-chirp in the BEC wavepacket can counteract the interaction-induced resonance shifts, thereby enabling the observation of enhanced resonant spectra and bistability features.

**Method.** The analysis uses the 1D Gross-Pitaevskii equation (GPE) framework, modeling the system dynamics by coupling the incident matter wave to the quasi-bound states of the cavity barriers.

**Model / system.** The system is a quasi-one-dimensional BEC interacting with a matter-wave Fabry-Perot (FP) cavity formed by light-induced Gaussian potential barriers. The dynamics are governed by the mean-field GPE, incorporating repulsive interatomic interactions ($g|\psi|^2\psi$).

**Key observables.** Transmission spectrum ($T$) as a function of incident wavepacket velocity ($v_0$), and the observation of bistability features.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required optimum momentum chirp (anti-chirp) needed to balance the interaction-induced energy shift.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the initial state as Gaussian and employing various time-dependent approximations (e.g., assuming the source term is slowly varying) to solve the coupled differential equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the setup (BEC through a double-barrier FP), comparing non-interacting vs. interacting spectra, and showing the mechanism involving the anti-chirped wavepacket.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the physical limitation (interaction-induced broadening) to proposing the anti-chirp solution. It then develops the theoretical framework by modeling the time evolution of the coupling amplitudes and deriving the final transmission probability integral.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

While a Fabry-Perot (FP) cavity interferometer is a standard tool in modern optics, analogous physics can also be observed with matter waves such as Bose-Einstein condensates (BECs). Interatomic collisions in BECs can induce nonlinear features such as multi-valued transmission spectra for interferometry experiments. Observation of interaction effects in the matter-wave analogue of FP interferometry has not yet been experimentally achieved, mostly due to broadening of transmission peaks by interaction-induced shifts of cavity resonances. In this work, we theoretically show that this limitation can be overcome by engineering a momentum chirp in a BEC wavepacket. By balancing this chirp against the dynamical mean-field energy shift of the cavity during transmission, we predict enhanced resonant spectra and bistability features due to interaction-assisted tunneling that would be otherwise inaccessible. We show that this physics is readily observable with quasi-one-dimensional BECs of cold atoms colliding with a pair of light-induced Gaussian potential barriers forming the cavity. We also benchmark the requisite optimum chirp needed and characterize the contrast of near-discontinuous FP transmission spectra at an interacting resonance. Our findings enable the realization of technologies like matter-wave switching protocols, and high-precision velocity measurements; estimates of achievable sensitivity for the latter are provided.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously characterizes quantum Dirichlet forms arising from KMS-symmetric quantum Markov semigroups. It achieves this by showing that these forms are completely determined by derivations and by establishing a deep connection between the semigroup's dynamics and the algebraic structure of a Tomita correspondence. This provides a powerful mathematical tool for analyzing open quantum systems at thermal equilibrium.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural insights into the mathematical foundations of open quantum systems dynamics, linking thermal equilibrium conditions (KMS) to rigorous algebraic structures (Dirichlet forms and Tomita correspondences).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a complete mathematical characterization of quantum Dirichlet forms associated with KMS-symmetric quantum Markov semigroups.

**Main result.** It establishes that these quantum Dirichlet forms can be completely characterized in terms of derivations, and it shows that the associated structure leads to a Tomita correspondence.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced techniques from operator algebras, including the use of the GNS Hilbert space, modular groups, and quadratic form theory to relate the semigroup structure to derivations.

**Model / system.** The study concerns open quantum systems whose time evolution is modeled by quantum Markov semigroups exhibiting KMS-symmetry. The mathematical framework involves von Neumann algebras and their associated quadratic forms.

**Key observables.** Quantum Dirichlet forms, the generator of the semigroup, and the associated Tomita correspondence structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry condition (KMS-symmetry) and the accretivity of the square root of the analytic generator with respect to the form norm.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis builds upon the existence of a reference state $\varphi$ and the assumption of KMS-symmetry, which refines the standard GNS-symmetry conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first characterizing bounded quantum Dirichlet forms via derivations, then extending this characterization to unbounded forms using ultraproduct constructions, and finally relating the resulting structure to a Tomita correspondence via deformation arguments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A KMS-symmetric quantum Markov semigroup is completely determined by the quadratic form in induces on the GNS Hilbert space. The quadratic forms that arise in this way are called quantum Dirichlet forms. In this article we give a complete description of bounded quantum Dirichlet forms in terms of derivations. In particular, we exhibit an explicit characterization of the bounded quantum Dirichlet forms on type I factors. We also show that any unbounded quantum Dirichlet form can be represented in terms of derivations. The key technical insight is that the modular group always restricts to a strongly continuous group on the domain of a quantum Dirichlet form such that the square root of the analytic generator is accretive with respect to the form norm.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the challenge of model selection in quantum dynamics forecasting by proposing ProtocolMatch, a framework that evaluates models based on the entire operational protocol, not just the architecture. By testing on driven quantum-spin systems, the authors show that model performance is highly protocol-dependent, necessitating that any reported predictor must be accompanied by its specific operational context and separate reliability metrics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, machine-learning-informed methodology for assessing the reliability of quantum dynamics models, which is crucial for building trustworthy simulations of open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Scientific dynamics forecasting requires model selection that accounts for the entire 'protocol'—including observation history, rollout rules, and physical constraints—rather than just the model architecture.

**Main result.** The optimal forecasting model depends critically on the specific protocol used, as demonstrated by performance reversals when changing data size, history length, or evaluation loop (observed vs. closed-loop).

**Method.** The authors introduce ProtocolMatch, a framework that evaluates model candidates by explicitly conditioning the risk calculation on the entire forecasting protocol $P$, separating accuracy, physical validity, and shifted-distribution reliability.

**Model / system.** The study applies this framework to driven quantum-spin dynamics, simulating systems ranging from two-spin to six-spin quantum chains governed by Hamiltonians like the transverse-field Ising model.

**Key observables.** Mean Squared Error (MSE) for prediction error, physical consistency (e.g., trace normalization, positivity), and interval coverage under shifted distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** History length (1, 5, 20), rollout rule (Observed-history vs. Closed-loop), and the specific protocol tuple $P$ defining the evaluation regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the protocol cannot be treated as a nuisance variable; changes in performance across protocols indicate fundamentally different deployment behaviors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between observed-history and closed-loop evaluations, and show how the preferred model architecture reverses when varying training data size or rollout rules.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of architecture-only model selection, introduces the ProtocolMatch framework to condition model selection on the full protocol $P$, and then empirically tests this framework across various quantum-spin dynamics systems and protocols.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scientific dynamics forecasting is often framed as an architecture choice, although deployment is also determined by observed history, rollout feedback, compute budget, physical objective, and test distribution. We formulate protocol-dependent model selection and introduce ProtocolMatch, a compute-matched, validation-selected, and failure-preserving evaluation framework. On driven quantum-spin dynamics, we compare recurrent, patched-attention, causal-attention, and low-rank linear predictors across three independently generated datasets. The causal-attention--recurrence ordering reverses as the training set grows within a fixed two-spin task, while a linear predictor has the lowest mean error in the six-spin local-observable comparison. Restricting observed history worsens every refreshed-history view but improves every closed-loop view in the four-spin study. A latest-state MLP has lower error than persistence on every dataset under state refresh across all five cells, yet its closed-loop rank varies by system and includes finite explosive errors. Physical penalties improve targeted consistency without reliably improving prediction error, and in-distribution intervals lose most coverage after a driving-frequency shift. Thus scientific model selection should return a predictor with its protocol and report accuracy, physical validity, and shifted-distribution reliability separately.

</details>

### [On Bonart's interpretation of the Square-Root Impact Law](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10053v1)

**Authors:** J. -P. Bouchaud, I. Mastromatteo, B. Toth

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the theoretical underpinnings of the Square-Root Impact Law (SRIL) in financial markets. It argues that the law's consistency requires a specific scaling exponent ($\delta=1/2$) derived from volume conservation, suggesting the law stems from equating mean impact to standard deviation. The work highlights the tension between theoretical assumptions (like stream whitening) and observed market behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** While applied to finance, the rigorous mathematical techniques used—analyzing scaling laws, variance budgets, and the failure of simple diffusive assumptions—are highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper seeks to reconcile the empirical Square-Root Impact Law (SRIL) in financial markets with a consistent theoretical mechanism derived from market microstructure physics.

**Main result.** A volume-conservation argument strongly selects the exponent $\delta=1/2$ for the impact law, suggesting the SRIL arises from a mean-equals-standard-deviation identification rather than purely diffusive processes.

**Method.** The authors use mathematical derivations based on variance budgets, conservation laws, and comparing theoretical models (like the Lillo–Mike–Farmer model) against the required scaling behavior.

**Model / system.** The system is a financial market microstructure, modeling the price impact ($I$) of metaorders ($Q$) relative to total volume ($V$) and volatility ($\sigma$). The analysis involves decomposing returns into background and impact components.

**Key observables.** Price impact ($I$), traded volume ($Q$), daily volatility ($\sigma$), and the participation rate ($\varphi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exponent $\delta$ governing the power-law dependence of impact on volume, and the participation rate $\varphi$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumptions involve the market whitening the *stream* of metaorders, and the analysis relies on volume-conservation arguments to constrain the scaling exponent $\delta$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the empirical requirements of the SRIL, then reviewing and simplifying Bonart's whitening argument, and finally using volume-conservation and scaling analysis to derive the necessary exponent $\delta=1/2$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The square-root impact law (SRIL), $I = Yσ\sqrt{Q/V}$, bundles two facts that a single mechanism must explain at once: a shape (impact proportional to square-root of traded volume $Q$) and an amplitude ($Y=O(1)$, independent of the participation rate $\varphi$). Bonart has recently proposed an elegant solution: if realized and counterfactual prices are both diffusive, information-neutral impact must have white increments and the SRIL follows without the wart. We reformulate and simplify his argument, and foreground the ingredient he himself regards as essential --- that the market whitens the stationary stream of each participant's metaorders, not any isolated one. The Lillo--Mike--Farmer model satisfies his premises, yet gives an isolated metaorder the super-square-root impact $\varphi^{(1-γ)/2}Q^{(1+γ)/2}$, $γ\in(0,1)$, because single metaorders (i.e. not part of a stream) are statistically invisible and thus priced mechanically. We then construct an explicit multi-agent propagator that realizes Bonart's whitening, and show it reproduces the clean SRIL precisely when the market can attribute trades to their issuer. But correct re-attribution of anonymous trades is a formidable task, which requires markets to behave as implausibly efficient signal processing machines. The stream hypothesis makes a sharp, falsifiable prediction --- isolated metaorders, well separated in time, should not obey the SRIL --- that does not seem agree with empirical data known to us. Independently, a volume-conservation argument singles out the square-root law and lays bare the step the diffusivity route must assume: diffusivity fixes the variance of impact --- an additive, stationary, age-blind quantity --- whereas the SRIL is a law for the mean impact, which marginally decays as $1/\sqrt{\text{age}}$ and therefore demands an origin of time.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how atoms trapped in a potential escape due to colored noise, modeling the dynamics using a Langevin equation. The authors derive theoretical expressions for the escape time, showing that while noise color is important, stray fields causing quasi-static barrier tilting can dominate the escape rate. The findings are highly relevant for interpreting experimental lifetime measurements in trapped-ion quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial theoretical tools for understanding noise-induced escape dynamics in trapped-ion and cold-atom systems, highlighting the importance of non-Markovian noise effects and external field perturbations beyond simple spectral analysis.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the heating and subsequent escape of atoms confined in a finite-depth potential when subjected to colored noise.

**Main result.** The escape rate is not solely determined by the spectral color of the noise; quasi-static barrier tilting caused by stray fields of plausible magnitude can significantly accelerate the escape process.

**Method.** The authors employ stochastic averaging techniques, comparing analytical derivations (like those from action-angle variables) with full Monte Carlo simulations of the Langevin equation.

**Model / system.** The system models the motion of an atom in a confining potential, such as those found in electromagnetically trapped ions or atoms. The dynamics are governed by a Langevin equation driven by a colored noise force with a power-law spectral density.

**Key observables.** Mean escape time ($T_1$), escape rate ($\dot{E}_{	ext{esc}}$), linear heating rate ($\dot{E}_{	ext{cold}}$), and the first-passage-time distribution ($P_{	ext{FPT}}(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $	au_{	ext{esc}} = \dot{E}_{	ext{cold}} / \dot{E}_{	ext{esc}}$ is key, alongside dimensionless parameters like $\omega_c/\omega_0$ and the barrier tilting parameter $\chi$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as the weak-noise regime ($\chi \ll 1$ and $h \ll 1$) and the use of amplitude-phase or action-angle averaging techniques.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the confining potential and the noise PSD, and compare escape times calculated using different averaging methods (action-angle vs. amplitude-phase) and show the dynamics for different initial preparation states (released vs. cooled).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical model and key observables, developing weak-noise theories using stochastic averaging, comparing different averaging methods, and finally applying the results to interpret experimental measurements from ion traps.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The motion of a particle in a finite-depth confining potential, such as that of an electromagnetically trapped atom or ion, is approximately harmonic at low energy and softens as the escape energy is approached. When the particle is driven by a stochastic force, the heating and escape times depend sensitively on the noise power spectrum. We study escape times of particles in softening potentials driven by power-law colored noise. We identify two nested conditions for weak noise: the particle must execute many oscillations before escape, and noise forces must be small relative to forces exerted by the trap so as not to tilt the potential appreciably. In this regime, we derive expressions for the mean escape time using stochastic averaging techniques and compare them with Monte Carlo simulations of the full equations of motion. We also examine the crossover to regimes where these conditions are not met and large quasi-static forces give rise to additional escape mechanisms. Applying these results to an experimental measurement of lifetimes in a microfabricated ion trap, we find that spectral color alone cannot account for the rapid escape rates observed, whereas quasi-static barrier tilting by a stray field of plausible magnitude could. These results are relevant to the heating and escape of trapped atoms due to noisy force fields, as well as to other nonlinear oscillators subject to colored noise.

</details>

### [Interaction-Assisted Spin Coherence in a Multimode Trapped Atomic Ensemble](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09073v1)

**Authors:** Aleksandre Merabishvili, Ebubechukwu O. Ilo-Okeke

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how collective spin coherence in trapped atoms is lost due to inhomogeneous detunings across multiple motional modes. It shows that particle interactions generate a self-correcting field that partially restores the coherence. Crucially, it quantifies that while interactions can refocus coherent phase errors, they cannot overcome irreversible environmental noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding coherence preservation in complex, inhomogeneous quantum systems, directly relevant to quantum sensing using trapped atoms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how collective spin coherence in a multimode trapped atomic ensemble is degraded by inhomogeneous phase evolution due to thermal occupation, and how atomic interactions can restore this coherence.

**Main result.** Interactions generate a self-consistent restoring field that counteracts phase dispersion, leading to a measurable recovery of the Ramsey contrast, though this recovery is ultimately limited by irreversible pure dephasing noise.

**Method.** The authors develop a weak-axis-resolved Hamiltonian theory, employing mean-field approximations and analyzing the dynamics in the continuum limit for high temperatures.

**Model / system.** The system is a gas of neutral atoms with two internal pseudospin states confined in a harmonic trap, modeled by an effective spin Hamiltonian incorporating mode-dependent detunings and overlap-dependent exchange interactions.

**Key observables.** Collective transverse spin (Ramsey-fringe contrast), the decay envelope of the Ramsey contrast, and the ratio $\Lambda$ comparing exchange scale to detuning spread.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless control parameter $\Lambda$, the temperature parameter $\eta$, and the pure dephasing rate $\gamma_{\perp}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a weak-axis-resolved description, thermally averaging transverse degrees of freedom, and distinguishing between coherent phase mixing and irreversible noise.

**Figures summary.** Figures conceptually illustrate the competition between mode-dependent detunings and exchange-assisted rephasing, and later show the robustness of the recovered contrast against varying levels of pure dephasing.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the non-interacting, phase-mixing baseline, to deriving the full interaction Hamiltonian, analyzing the mean-field dynamics, and finally quantifying the crossover between coherent rephasing and irreversible decoherence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a thermally occupied multimode trapped ensemble, mode-dependent detunings can strongly suppress Ramsey contrast even when the underlying dynamics remains coherent. We develop a weak-axis-resolved Hamiltonian theory in which collective mode spins experience longitudinal detunings and overlap-dependent exchange interactions. Without exchange, the distribution of detunings across thermally occupied modes produces coherent phase mixing and an algebraically decaying Ramsey-contrast envelope in the many-mode continuum regime. At the mean-field level, exchange generates a self-consistent restoring field that reduces phase dispersion and restores collective contrast. The crossover is governed by the competition between the collective exchange scale and the thermally induced detuning spread: when the late-time contrast is expressed in terms of their ratio, results at different temperatures collapse onto a common trend, and the crossover coupling follows the predicted inverse-square thermal scaling, with a numerical exponent of 2.06. Weak irreversible pure dephasing limits, but does not eliminate, this recovery, demonstrating that exchange refocuses coherent inhomogeneous phase mixing but cannot reverse irreversible phase noise.

</details>

### [Black hole radial geometry from a quantum spin chain](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10504v1)

**Authors:** Boris A. Khanikati  
**Type:** both · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.10504v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `QC/QI experiment` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.10504_figures/2610.10504_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Schematic representation of the dictionary between the black brane geometry and the spin chain. The dimensionless radial coordinate w runs from the d-dimensional boundary (w = 0) to the horizon (w = 1), with w &gt; 1 corresponding to the black hole interior. In the asymptotic regime, the radial coordinate is mapped onto the spin-chain quasimomentum. Real q ∈(0, π) describes the black brane bulk, whereas its analytic continuation, q = iκ with κ &gt; 0, describes the black hole interior.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a profound connection between gravity in Anti-de Sitter space and quantum many-body physics by encoding the radial geometry into a solvable spin chain model. By simulating this chain on a quantum processor, the authors successfully extract gravitational observables, such as the time to the black hole singularity, validating the holographic principle in a quantum computing setting.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, experimentally verifiable realization of the AdS/CFT correspondence, linking bulk gravity dynamics (black holes) directly to measurable quantum many-body physics on superconducting qubits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a dictionary showing how the radial differential equation for a scalar field in an AdS black brane background can be mapped onto an exactly solvable semi-infinite quantum spin chain.

**Main result.** The model successfully reproduces key gravitational observables, such as the Breitenlohner-Freedman bound and the proper time to the singularity, using quantum mechanical calculations.

**Method.** The authors use spectral analysis, mapping the continuum differential equation to a discrete spin chain Hamiltonian, and employing techniques like analytic continuation and fitting to extract geometric information.

**Model / system.** The system is an AdS black brane geometry dual to a scalar field, whose radial dynamics are encoded in a nearest-neighbor, number-conserving spin chain Hamiltonian. This chain is simulated on a superconducting quantum processor.

**Key observables.** Screening spectrum, radial coordinate emergence from quasimomentum, thermal one-point function coefficient c(h), and the ratio R(h) used for phase fitting.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $d$, the black brane proper radial distance $\ell$, and the ratio $	au_s/R$ (compared to the exact value $\pi/4$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the continuum problem to a discrete chain, and the final results are subject to finite-size and finite-time simulation errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the measured quantum propagator evolution against exact theoretical limits, showing the reconstruction of the coefficient c(h) and the phase fit for the radial time scale $	au_s/R$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the mapping from the radial equation to the spin chain, then calculating spectral sum rules, and finally using experimental simulation and analytic continuation to reconstruct the geometric observables like the proper time to the singularity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the radial differential equation for a scalar field in an anti-de Sitter black brane background can be encoded in an exactly solvable semi-infinite spin chain independent of dimensionality. The radial coordinate emerges as lattice quasimomentum. Spin coherence encodes the geometry, while the spectrum and response recover gravitational observables and yield exact screening-mass sum rules. We implement the finite chain on a superconducting processor. At finite filling and low energies, the model yields a conformal field theory in a coordinate proportional to proper radial distance.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'coherent consensus,' a quantum protocol that uses entangled GHZ states to combine results from multiple quantum classifiers for phase estimation. It rigorously proves that this method offers a significant $\sqrt{n}$ improvement in precision over classical majority voting. The results are validated both theoretically and experimentally using a trapped-ion quantum computer.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, entanglement-based quantum primitive for improving the performance of multiple quantum measurements, which is highly relevant for quantum sensing and distributed quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes and analyzes a 'coherent consensus' primitive to enhance the collaborative performance of independent quantum classifiers for estimating and classifying unknown quantum phases.

**Main result.** Coherent consensus achieves a superior $\sqrt{n}$ precision gain in phase estimation compared to incoherent majority voting, which is demonstrated both theoretically and experimentally on a trapped-ion platform.

**Method.** The technique involves preparing a Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) state, encoding individual classifier outputs as relative phases, and then collecting these phases onto a single qubit to amplify the signal.

**Model / system.** The core task is phase estimation, analogous to quantum metrology, using an ensemble of $n$ imperfect phase gates. The analysis compares the coherent consensus protocol against classical majority voting.

**Key observables.** Estimated phases ($\phi$), sampling error, precision gain ($\sqrt{n}$ factor), and relative error bounds ($\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n$), number of shots ($m$), noise parameters ($\sigma$), and the required accuracy ($\epsilon$) and failure probability ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to prepare GHZ states and relies on concentration inequalities (Hoeffding/Bernstein) for theoretical guarantees; practical application is limited by device drift.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare estimated phases using both majority voting and coherent consensus on a trapped-ion quantum computer, illustrating the expected $\sqrt{n}$ precision gain.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem, develops the theoretical framework by comparing the estimators (MV vs. CC) using concentration inequalities, derives success guarantees (Theorems 1 & 2), and finally validates these findings through both theoretical bounds and experimental implementation on a trapped-ion quantum computer.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inspired by quantum metrology, we propose a coherent consensus primitive to enhance the collaborative performance of independent quantum classifiers at the decision boundary. The basic idea consists of three steps: i) prepare a Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) state on $n$ qubits; ii) apply a relative phase to each qubit, which encodes the output of one classifier; and iii) undo the GHZ preparation to collect all phases onto the first qubit. The signal is amplified by a factor of $n$, which reduces the sampling error by a factor of $\sqrt{n}$ relative to incoherent majority voting, that is, the direct combination of estimates from individual classifiers. Coherent consensus is valid for general estimation and classification of phases. We derive guarantees on the resources needed to estimate phases using this coherent consensus primitive as compared to majority voting. We implement both techniques on a trapped-ion quantum computer using a given ensemble of imperfect phase gates. We observe the expected $\sqrt{n}$ gain in precision of coherent consensus, with excess dispersion at the largest $n$ consistent with device drift. Coherent consensus can thus be understood as a form of sensing inside a quantum computer.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel quantum algorithm using Quantum Signal Processing to estimate thermal properties for quantum systems, specifically focusing on particle-number-conserving Hamiltonians. It introduces a powerful grand-canonical reconstruction technique that reuses quantum measurement data when varying the chemical potential. The work proves that this quantum approach can achieve better scaling than classical methods for calculating key thermodynamic observables.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a quantum computational pathway to calculate fundamental thermodynamic quantities (like partition functions) that are central to condensed matter physics, offering potential speedups over established classical numerical techniques.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an efficient, end-to-end quantum framework to estimate canonical and grand-canonical thermal properties for particle-number-conserving quantum systems.

**Main result.** The proposed quantum algorithms achieve lower asymptotic time complexity than classical Lanczos methods in system-size-independent temperature regimes, and the grand-canonical reconstruction avoids re-running quantum circuits when varying the chemical potential.

**Method.** The framework uses Quantum Signal Processing (QSP) combined with particle-number-preserving Trotterized quantum simulations to estimate thermal expectation values and partition functions.

**Model / system.** The study applies to second-quantized Hamiltonians, exemplified by the two-dimensional Hubbard model, which describes interacting lattice electronic systems.

**Key observables.** Canonical partition functions ($Z_{L,s}(eta)$), canonical expectation values ($\langle O angle_{eta,s}$), and grand-canonical observables ($\langle O angle_{	ext{GC}}^{eta, \mu}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and coupling strengths (e.g., $U=8t$ for the Hubbard model).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing Trotter circuits that preserve particle number and uses classical reweighting techniques for grand-canonical reconstruction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 shows simulation results evaluating a figure of merit ($\gamma_2$) for the conventional grand-canonical approach across varying system sizes, temperatures, and electron densities for the Hubbard model.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the canonical estimation scheme using QSP, followed by the grand-canonical reconstruction scheme using classical reweighting. Theoretical proofs establish complexity bounds compared to classical methods, and the results are numerically validated on the 2D Hubbard model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop an end-to-end framework for estimating canonical thermal properties of particle-number-conserving second-quantized Hamiltonians using quantum simulations. The framework integrates initial state preparation with mixed Dicke states, particle-number-preserving Trotterized simulation, and generalized quantum signal processing to estimate canonical partition functions and observables. Building on this canonical scheme, we introduce a grand-canonical reconstruction scheme in which the same quantum measurement data can be reused for arbitrary chemical potentials through classical reweighting, thereby avoiding repeated quantum circuit executions as the chemical potential is varied. We prove the existence of system-size-independent temperature regimes in which both the canonical and reconstructed grand-canonical schemes have lower asymptotic time complexity than classical Lanczos methods, up to polynomial factors, for physically relevant Hamiltonians. We numerically validate our theoretical results for the two-dimensional Hubbard model in both weak- and intermediate-coupling regimes.

</details>
