**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework using nonlinear modes to analyze the energy landscape of a mean-field model for glasses. It derives universal scaling laws for the statistics of activation barriers and plasticity-onset forces. The results provide quantitative predictions for glassy dynamics that are expected to hold for real amorphous solids.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides concrete, analytically derived scaling laws for non-equilibrium phenomena (plasticity, activated dynamics) in disordered systems, which are highly relevant to understanding energy dissipation and structural failure in complex materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to predict the statistical distributions of activation barriers ($\Delta U$) and the critical external force ($f_c$) required to induce plasticity in a model of amorphous solids (glasses).

**Main result.** The authors derive asymptotic scaling laws, predicting $p(\Delta U_c) \sim (\Delta U_c)^{1/4}$ and $p(f_c) \sim f_c^{2/3}$, and confirm these predictions using specialized sampling algorithms.

**Method.** The analysis employs a nonlinear modes framework applied to an anharmonic mean-field Hamiltonian, focusing on 'cubic modes' to characterize the energy landscape's soft directions.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by an anharmonic mean-field Hamiltonian for continuous degrees of freedom, incorporating random quadratic stiffnesses and disordered Gaussian couplings, characteristic of glassy materials.

**Key observables.** Activation barriers ($\Delta U$), plasticity onset force ($f_c$), and the frequency distribution ($ho(\omega_\pi)$) of the softest vibrational modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The shape parameter $\eta = b_2^3/(b_2 b_4)$ is crucial, with physical relevance restricted to $\eta < 3$. System size $N$ dictates the scaling of mean values ($\langle\Delta Uangle \sim N^{-4/5}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The predictions are assumed to apply universally to both the mean-field model and realistic, finite-dimensional glasses.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the parameter space of cubic modes ($\eta$ vs. $b_2^3 - b_2 b_4$). Figure 2 confirms the universal quartic tail ($ho(\omega_\pi) \sim \omega_\pi^4$) in the frequency spectrum. Figure 3 shows numerical confirmation of plastic instability detection.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the anharmonic mean-field model and the nonlinear modes framework. It then analyzes the relevant parameter regimes ($\eta < 3$) and derives the asymptotic scaling laws for $\Delta U$ and $f_c$. Finally, it develops and uses efficient algorithms to sample the potential energy landscape to quantitatively confirm these theoretical predictions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A recently introduced anharmonic mean-field model unifiedly reproduced a broad range of low-temperature glass phenomena --- including harmonic nonphononic spectral properties, linear micromechanics and strongly driven elasto-plastic dynamics --- indicating that its underlying energy landscape is intrinsically glassy. Here, we apply a nonlinear modes framework to the model and derive analytic predictions for the asymptotic distributions of activation barriers $p(Δ{U})\!\sim\!(Δ{U})^{1/4}$ and the external force needed for the onset of plasticity $p(f_{\rm c})\sim f_{\rm c}^{2/3}$, for their extreme-value scaling and for $\langleΔ{U}\rangle$ beyond the asymptotic regime. These predictions are expected to equally apply to the mean-field model and to finite-dimensional glasses. We develop efficient algorithms for sampling minima and saddles of the model's glassy potential energy landscape, and quantitatively confirm the theoretical predictions. This progress is enabled by identifying a subset of collective degrees of freedom that are physically relevant for activated glassy dynamics, which like the theoretical predictions should apply to realistic glasses.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a unified theoretical framework to consistently treat both direct Coulomb and phonon-mediated interactions in correlated metals. By unifying the GW and Fan-Migdal self-energies, the authors derive and solve extended Eliashberg equations for the quasiparticle spectrum. The method is applied to $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$, demonstrating its utility while also pointing toward necessary vertex corrections for quantitative accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state correlations, the rigorous treatment of coupled electronic and vibrational dynamics using advanced Green's function techniques provides a powerful, generalizable framework for open quantum systems where environment coupling (phonons) is crucial.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a unified theoretical framework for treating both direct electron-electron and phonon-mediated dynamical interactions in correlated metals.

**Main result.** The method successfully calculates the temperature-dependent quasiparticle spectrum for $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$, showing qualitative agreement with experiments, though vertex corrections are suggested for accurate resistivity.

**Method.** The approach unifies GW and Fan-Migdal self-energies into a single effective interaction, leading to normal-state Eliashberg equations solved via a local approximation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on correlated metals, using $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$ as a benchmark system. The theory treats coupled electrons and phonons dynamically.

**Key observables.** Temperature-dependent quasiparticle spectrum, electrical resistivity (via Green-Kubo formalism), and spectral function $A(\epsilon)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-energy window, temperature ($T$), and the coupling strengths between electronic and vibrational excitations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation is performed in a local approximation, and the need for vertex corrections is highlighted as a key limitation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the crystal structure, reciprocal space, and electron/phonon dispersion relations for $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the unified self-energy formalism, derives the resulting Eliashberg equations, solves these dynamical equations for the quasiparticle spectrum, and applies the framework to calculate transport properties for $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{RuO}_4$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

First-principles studies of materials with strong electronic correlations introduce additional theoretical complexities and computational costs, even more so if lattice degrees of freedom also play a role. To address these challenges, we show how direct and phonon-mediated electron-electron interactions can be treated consistently. The key observation is that the GW and Fan-Migdal self-energies are topologically the same diagram, so that they can be unified using a single effective interaction, which is the sum of the two interactions. The Dyson equation for the electron Green function then yields normal-state Eliashberg equations, which can be viewed as a subset of the Hedin-Baym equations for coupled electrons and phonons [Phys. Rev. X 13, 031026 (2023)]. We solve these dynamical equations in a local approximation to calculate from first principles the temperature-dependent quasiparticle spectrum, with close attention to the low-energy window determining transport. We apply this extended dynamical Hubbard approach to the correlated metal Sr$_2$RuO$_4$, which has long served as a benchmark system for advanced electronic-structure methods. The resulting renormalization of quasiparticle bands is in qualitative agreement with experiments and results from dynamical mean-field theory. However, here the low-energy linewidth and associated resistivity within the Green-Kubo formalism in the bubble approximation are underestimated, suggesting the need for vertex corrections. This work highlights how simple dynamical formulations can provide an extensible framework for studying correlated materials, which treats electronic and vibrational excitations on equal footing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the resource bottleneck in quantum energy estimation by optimizing how Pauli observables are grouped for measurement. It formulates this as a binary optimization problem, using MILP for non-overlapping groups and introducing an 'O-clique' method for overlapping supports. The results demonstrate significant, transferable reductions in required measurement repetitions across various quantum Hamiltonians.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource optimization in variational quantum algorithms, providing concrete, mathematically rigorous methods (MILP) to reduce the required sampling overhead for estimating ground state energies in quantum chemistry and condensed matter simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is minimizing the required number of repeated measurements for accurate quantum energy estimation, as this resource requirement can dominate computational costs.

**Main result.** Variance-informed optimization techniques, particularly using Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) for non-overlapping groups, substantially reduce the measurement requirement compared to standard methods.

**Method.** The authors formulate the measurement grouping problem as a classical binary optimization problem, utilizing MILP for non-overlapping sets and introducing 'O-clique' for optimizing overlapping supports.

**Model / system.** The methods are benchmarked on molecular and lattice Hamiltonians, including Fermi-Hubbard, Kitaev-Heisenberg-$\Gamma$, and XYZ lattice Hamiltonians, which are represented by Pauli observables.

**Key observables.** The primary metric is $\epsilon_{2M}(G)$, the required measurement repetitions, derived from minimizing the variance $S(G)$ associated with the Pauli observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization depends on node features ($v_i$, variance) and edge features ($w_{ij}$, covariance) derived from the Pauli terms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The MILP formulation is used to linearize the selection process, and the comparison separates gains from measurement-support selection versus coefficient refinement.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the pipeline, detailing the MILP approach for non-overlapping grouping and the O-Clique method for overlapping grouping, showing how weights are adjusted.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by defining the measurement cost function, proposing MILP for non-overlapping groups, extending to O-clique for overlapping groups, and finally benchmarking these optimized groupings across various lattice and molecular Hamiltonians.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Repeated measurements can dominate the resources required for quantum energy estimation, making the choice of which Pauli observables to measure together a central optimization problem for variational quantum algorithms. We formulate fully commuting measurement grouping as a classical binary optimization problem based on clique selection and construct non-overlapping groups using mixed-integer linear programming (MILP). For a benchmark of molecular Hamiltonians, groupings optimized with approximate covariances reduce the non-overlapping measurement requirement $\varepsilon^2M$ by $51.8\%$ on average relative to sorted insertion (SI). Using the MILP groups to initialize iterative coefficient splitting (ICS), denoted MILP-ICS, yields an average $24.3\%$ reduction relative to ICS initialized from SI (SI-ICS). The optimized groups also transfer across nearby molecular geometries while preserving substantial measurement savings. We further introduce O-clique, which directly optimizes overlapping commuting supports through candidate-clique selection and coefficient profiles. Although O-clique provides only modest additional reductions beyond MILP-ICS, it reduces the measurement requirement by $27.3\%$ on average relative to SI-ICS with 100 iterations, despite using only five final coefficient refinement iterations, indicating improved support quality. Finally, we extend the comparison to Fermi--Hubbard, Kitaev--Heisenberg--$Γ$, and XYZ lattice Hamiltonians, where MILP-based groupings substantially outperform the corresponding SI-based strategies. Together, these results show that variance-informed optimization of measurement-group structure can substantially reduce sampling costs across molecular and lattice Hamiltonians, with optimized non-overlapping groups providing strong, transferable initializations and direct overlapping optimization offering further gains.

</details>

**Summary.** This work pioneers a method using Neural Quantum States to solve the electronic structure of moiré superlattices, revealing that the true ground state is highly fluctuating and low-spin, contrary to mean-field predictions. By developing a wavefunction readout, the authors successfully bridge the gap between the complex continuum solution and a manageable effective model, specifically a two-orbital Hubbard model.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology provides a powerful, general framework for deriving effective lattice Hamiltonians directly from complex, continuum quantum wavefunctions, which is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated systems where simple band theory fails.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of developing accurate effective low-energy descriptions for quantum materials when competing energy scales break down the standard energy hierarchy.

**Main result.** The continuum Neural Quantum State (NQS) approach successfully reveals that the ground state of moiré superlattices is a low-spin state characterized by significant charge fluctuations, which necessitates an effective description via a spinful two-orbital Hubbard model.

**Method.** The authors use Continuum Neural Quantum States (NQS) to solve the microscopic many-body problem directly, followed by a novel wavefunction readout technique to map the continuum state onto site-resolved charge and spin statistics.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on moiré superlattices formed by semiconducting transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) at a specific electron filling ($
u=3$). The system is governed by a continuum Hamiltonian incorporating kinetic, lattice potential, and interaction terms.

**Key observables.** Site charges ($Q_A$), site spins ($\langle S_{z_A} angle$), charge fluctuation variance ($	ext{Var}(Q)$), and spin correlation functions ($\langle S_A \cdot S_B angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron filling $
u=3$, moiré potential strength, and the comparison between mean-field ferromagnetic states and the true low-spin ground state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the effective low-energy physics can be extracted by analyzing the local statistics (charge/spin) derived from the highly accurate continuum NQS solution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the coarse-graining process of the NQS wavefunction onto moiré sites, comparing charge and spin distributions between different total spin sectors (e.g., $S_{z_{tot}}=0$ vs. $S_{z_{tot}}=N_e/2$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of scale separation breakdown, introduces the NQS method with a readout, applies it to moiré TMDs, and systematically analyzes the resulting charge and spin correlations to derive the effective Hubbard model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Effective descriptions of quantum materials rely on a separation of energy scales that identifies their low-energy degrees of freedom. When kinetic energy, lattice potential, and interactions compete, this hierarchy breaks down, and both the microscopic state and its effective description are unknown. Continuum neural quantum states (NQS) can find the ground state directly without projecting onto prescribed bands, orbitals, or spins, but their microscopic wavefunctions are difficult to interpret in terms of effective variables. Here, we introduce a wavefunction readout that maps the continuum NQS onto site-resolved charge and spin statistics, allowing effective degrees of freedom to emerge from the microscopic solution. Focusing on moiré transition metal dichalcogenides at filling $ν= 3$, the NQS reveals a low-spin ground state below the ferromagnetic crystal of spin-polarized Wigner molecules. Although the charge density retains triangular molecular motifs, our readout reveals significant charge fluctuations through bound nearest-neighbor doublon--hole pairs. Each molecular charge state has an inert spin-singlet core and rim electrons that carry the spin. Charge and spin fluctuations are therefore intertwined, with antiferromagnetic spin-correlations between sites, motivating an effective description by a spinful two-orbital Hubbard model. More generally, our results demonstrate a route from continuum wavefunctions to effective lattice models when competing scales preclude a controlled reduction.

</details>

### [Design-Time Conformance Checking for Pulse-Level Quantum Control](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10427v1)

**Authors:** Rylan Malarchick  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.10427v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `QC/QI experiment` **3/5** · `quantum measurements` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.10427_figures/2610.10427_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Fig. 1. The differential loop. The generator derives programs from the descriptor, the checker and the vendor toolchain each answer, and triage dispositions every row. A value the lowering cannot carry exactly is attributed to the harness, not counted as agreement or disagreement.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces qconform, a formal checker designed to verify if a quantum pulse control program is physically realizable on a quantum device. It achieves this by comparing the program against a versioned capability descriptor, using exact arithmetic to avoid floating-point errors. The evaluation demonstrates that the checker successfully catches discrepancies between different versions of vendor toolchains.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, formal verification layer for quantum control sequences, which is crucial for moving quantum algorithms from simulation to reliable hardware implementation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is ensuring that a pulse-level quantum control program is physically realizable on a target quantum device, as vendor documentation often fails to capture all operational constraints.

**Main result.** The proposed checker, qconform, successfully accepted no program that the vendor toolchains refused during extensive differential testing, identifying discrepancies between toolchain versions.

**Method.** The authors developed qconform, a design-time, offline checker that verifies a pulse program against a versioned capability descriptor using exact rational arithmetic.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on pulse-level quantum control for superconducting qubits, using vendor toolchains like QICK and Qblox to define the operational constraints of the physical hardware.

**Key observables.** The checker reports a verdict, the specific rules applied, and a coverage manifest detailing what was not checked.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Device constraints include minimum pulse length, timing grid, frequency band, and amplitude resolution, all defined using exact rational numbers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The checker does not decide the converse (it might reject a program the toolchain accepts), nor does it cover run-time behavior or data-dependent control flow.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a differential loop diagram illustrating the flow from program generation to vendor toolchain testing, and tables summarizing differential results and defects found.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of hardware constraint verification, details the qconform checker and its descriptor format, presents the methodology using differential testing against QICK and Qblox, and concludes with evaluation results and identified limitations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A pulse-level quantum control program is written against a device whose limits are recorded, if at all, in vendor documentation and source code. A program that exceeds them can be refused at compile time. It can also be accepted and silently altered, or compile and then fail at the board. In the last two cases the experiment runs, and the data does not correspond to the program that was written. We present qconform, a checker that decides whether a pulse program is realizable on a device, given a versioned capability descriptor for that device. Every constraint in a descriptor cites the toolchain observation that established it. The checker is design-time, offline, and deterministic, and it uses no floating point. It reports a verdict, the rules it applied, and a coverage manifest that names what it did not check. We evaluate qconform by differential testing against the QICK and Qblox toolchains, on three QICK board configurations and one Qblox cluster. Over 1263 program runs of 969 distinct programs, the checker accepted no program that a toolchain refuses. On both vendors, a descriptor pinned to one toolchain release accepts programs that older releases refuse. The evaluation also found ten defects in the checker and its descriptors, all fixed.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the probability distribution of counts in a Markov multinomial model, simulating a ball on a generalized Galton board. It determines the precise conditions under which the most probable outcome shifts from being concentrated at the corners (vertices) of the simplex to the center. The work uses advanced asymptotic analysis to show this transition occurs predictably as the system size increases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical machinery used—analyzing phase transitions in high-dimensional probability distributions—is highly relevant to understanding critical phenomena and equilibrium states in complex many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the crossover point in a symmetric Markov multinomial model where the most likely outcome (bin) shifts from being near the vertices (corners) of the simplex to the center.

**Main result.** For large systems, the most likely bin transitions from the vertices directly to the center as the expected number of turns grows to a fixed multiple of the logarithm of the steps.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced asymptotic techniques, including second-order analysis, generating functions, and saddle-point approximations to compare the probability of interior points against those at the vertices.

**Model / system.** The model is a ball bouncing down a generalized Galton board, modeled as a Markov chain. The observable is the count vector $\mathbf{n}$ of directions used over $N$ steps, which resides on a $d$-dimensional simplex.

**Key observables.** The probability distribution of the count vector $\mathbf{n}$, the ratio of the probability of an interior bin to the probability of a vertex ($P_{\mu}^N(n)/V_N$), and the crossing point $p_{N,k}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of steps $N$, the dimension $d$, the expected number of turns $T$, and the logarithmic scale $L = \log N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are asymptotic, valid for large $N$, and the analysis often relies on specific scaling regimes for the parameters (e.g., $T$ growing proportionally to $L$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the crossover behavior on a triangle ($d=3$) at $N=24$, showing the ratio $P_N(n)/V_N$ crossing a threshold.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from first-order analysis (suggesting simultaneous crossing) to a second-order analysis to break ties, establishing explicit asymptotic formulas for the crossing time $T_{N,k}$ and proving the general transition from vertices to the center.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We observe a ball bouncing down a Galton board with any number of directions. At each peg it either keeps its direction with some fixed probability or randomly turns to one of the other directions. Its bin measures how often it went each way, a point of a simplex. When the ball rarely turns, the most likely bins are the corners, which only a ball that never turns can reach. We ask when the best bin of each face of the simplex becomes as likely as a corner. To first order every face catches up at the same moment. We break this tie at second order, with an explicit constant for each face. Hence on a long board, as the expected number of turns grows to any fixed multiple of the length's logarithm, the most likely bin jumps once, from the corners straight to the center. A nonuniform start or a weak external field changes the constants, potentially allowing an intermediate face to win.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work derives exact rate equations governing the redistribution of quasiparticle weight across an interaction-deformed Fermi surface. By tracking the change in particle deficit and surplus populations as interactions are turned on, the authors establish fundamental kinematic identities. These results provide a robust, general framework for studying spectral function evolution in strongly correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of these kinematic identities provides a powerful, general framework for understanding how spectral weight and particle distribution evolve in strongly correlated systems, independent of specific approximations like the Luttinger theorem itself.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives exact kinematic identities describing how the occupation deficit inside and surplus outside the Fermi surface change when interactions are switched on at fixed particle density.

**Main result.** It establishes rate equations for these populations, showing that when Luttinger's theorem holds, the rates of change for the deficit and surplus are equal, balancing surface flux with bulk redistribution.

**Method.** The analysis uses a continuum of interaction parameters ($\lambda$) and applies transport theorems (like the Reynolds theorem) to derive continuity equations for the occupation numbers and the Luttinger integrand.

**Model / system.** The system is spinless fermions on a lattice in $d$ dimensions at zero temperature, modeled by tracking the evolution of the interacting Green's function and self-energy as an interaction parameter $\lambda$ is varied.

**Key observables.** Quasiparticle weight ($Z_{\lambda k}$), occupation deficit ($N_1$), quasiparticle surplus ($N_2$), and the Luttinger integrand ($L_{\lambda k}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction switch parameter ($\lambda$), Fermi momentum ($k_F$), and chemical potential ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivations rely on the existence of a quasiparticle pole and the application of transport theorems across moving discontinuities; some results require assumptions related to functional invariance.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the momentum distribution $n_{\lambda k}$ along a line through the Fermi sea, comparing free and interacting fermion cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formalism by defining the populations ($N_1, N_2$), deriving the rate equations using transport theorems, establishing conservation laws, and finally relating these rates to the Luttinger integrand to confirm the kinematic identities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Luttinger's theorem fixes the volume of the Fermi sea of a Fermi liquid, but not how interactions redistribute occupation around its surface. For an interaction switched on and tuned at fixed density, we derive the exact rates of change of the occupation deficit inside the interacting Fermi surface and of the surplus outside it. Each is the weight swept by the deforming surface plus a change of Luttinger's integrand at fixed momentum; the difference of the surface terms is flux of incoherent weight $1 - Z_{\vec k}$. These identities hold with or without the Luttinger theorem.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Measurement-Efficient Differentiable Quantum Architecture Search (ME-DQAS) framework for optimizing quantum circuits used in combinatorial problems. By exploiting the structural properties of the cost Hamiltonian and circuit, the authors derive a method that significantly reduces the number of required quantum measurements for gradient estimation. This lowers the practical overhead, making DQAS more feasible for current quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical hardware limitations of quantum machine learning algorithms by providing a concrete, theoretically derived method to reduce the required quantum measurement budget for gradient estimation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is the high measurement cost associated with Differentiable Quantum Architecture Search (DQAS) when applying variational quantum algorithms to combinatorial optimization.

**Main result.** The authors propose a measurement-efficient scheme that reduces the required gradient measurement cost by 39% to 41% compared to standard two-shift estimates on benchmark problems like 3-SAT and MaxCut.

**Method.** They derive and implement a measurement-saving gradient estimator that exploits the diagonal structure of the cost Hamiltonian and the structure of the quantum circuit suffix to replace standard two-shift rules with one-shift or zero-measurement estimates.

**Model / system.** The work uses parameterized quantum circuits $U(	heta)$ to estimate an objective function $F(	heta) = \langle 0|U(	heta)^\dagger H_f U(	heta)|0angle$, where $H_f$ is a diagonal cost Hamiltonian derived from combinatorial problems like 3-SAT or MaxCut.

**Key observables.** Normalized loss (objective function value), number of DQAS updates, and the requested $	heta$-gradient shot cost.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The reduction factor (39%-41%), the gate parameterization type (rotational), and the structure of the Hamiltonian (diagonal in the computational basis).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the cost Hamiltonian $H_f$ is diagonal in the computational basis and that the circuit suffix possesses structural properties (e.g., Clifford or commuting) that allow for the gradient simplification.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares DQAS vs. ME-DQAS across final quality, objective updates, and $	heta$-gradient shot cost ratio for 3-SAT and MaxCut, showing ME-DQAS superiority. Figure 2 quantifies the source of cost reduction via zero-gradient and one-shift rules.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the bottleneck in DQAS gradient estimation. It then theoretically derives the measurement-saving gradient estimator based on structural properties. Finally, it validates the approach experimentally on 3-SAT and MaxCut, comparing performance metrics against the standard DQAS baseline.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Differentiable quantum architecture search (DQAS) is a promising framework for the automated design of quantum circuits, particularly for variational quantum optimization algorithms. However, its practical deployment on quantum hardware is limited by the large number of circuit measurements required during optimization, making hardware execution costly. In this work, we show that for a broad class of combinatorial optimization problems and commonly used rotational gate parameterizations, the measurement cost of DQAS can be significantly reduced without changing the optimization objective. We derive the proposed measurement reduction scheme theoretically and validate it experimentally on 3-SAT and MaxCut benchmark problems. Our approach reduces the requested gradient measurement cost by about 39 to 41% while introducing only negligible classical post-processing overhead, lowering the practical cost of executing DQAS on quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates exciton binding in flat Chern bands using a kagome lattice model. It demonstrates that the Coulomb interaction's phase matching stabilizes quantum-vortex excitons, causing a topological transition as spin-orbit coupling increases. This reordering is predicted to manifest as a distinct change in the material's optical absorption spectrum.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a microscopic link between exciton topology, Coulomb binding energy, and optical response, showing that phase effects beyond simple band Chern numbers dictate the ground state, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum emitters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to explain how Coulomb phase matching stabilizes a quantum-vortex exciton as the lowest bound state in flat Chern bands, extending understanding beyond the underlying electronic band Chern numbers.

**Main result.** Increasing spin-orbit coupling drives a transition of the lowest exciton from a quantum-vortex state to a zero-winding state, which is observable as a transition from a dark vortex exciton to a bright zero-winding exciton in the optical spectrum.

**Method.** The analysis uses a projected electron-hole formulation, calculating total energy by minimizing the sum of kinetic and Coulomb interaction energies, while isolating the phase contribution to the binding energy.

**Model / system.** The study uses a prototypical Yin–Yang kagome lattice to model excitons (bound electron-hole pairs) in a setting exhibiting flat Chern bands and spin-orbit coupling.

**Key observables.** Exciton topology (classified by winding number relative to Chern-number difference), total energy $E(\eta)$, and circularly polarized optical absorption spectra $\epsilon_{\sigma}(E_\gamma)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin-orbit coupling ($\lambda_{soc}$), the lattice structure (Yin–Yang kagome), and the exciton wavefunction phase winding.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the Bloch-overlap geometry is relevant for binding, and later sections test the failure of local approximations to capture the full nonlocal momentum-pair contributions to the energy ordering.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the kagome lattice realization, the exciton energy spectrum $E(\eta)$ vs. $\lambda_{soc}$ showing winding sectors, and comparisons between native eigenstates and amplitude-controlled trials showing phase vortex structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical model and the role of Coulomb phase matching in stabilizing vortex excitons. It then uses topological indices to track competing states and demonstrates the reordering via energy minimization. Finally, it connects this reordering to measurable optical selection rules.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optically exciting a typical low-dimensional semiconductor produces a nodeless \(s\)-type exciton as its lowest-energy bound state. Here, we explain how Coulomb phase matching stabilises a quantum-vortex exciton as the lowest bound state in flat Chern bands. Using a prototypical Yin--Yang kagome lattice, we demonstrate that increasing the spin--orbit coupling drives a transition of the lowest exciton from a quantum-vortex state to a zero-winding state, while leaving the band Chern numbers unchanged. We trace this behaviour to a gauge-invariant combination of exciton phase differences and Bloch-overlap phases that can reduce the Coulomb energy. By comparing states with identical wavefunction amplitudes, we isolate this phase contribution and show that it is sufficient to drive the reversal in exciton ordering. We classify exciton topology by the momentum-space wavefunction winding relative to the conduction--valence Chern-number difference,connecting the competing states to circular-polarisation selection rules. We then show that the resulting reordering can be tracked directly in the optical absorption spectrum, through the transition from a dark vortex exciton to a bright zero-winding exciton. These findings establish a microscopic link between exciton topology, Coulomb binding, and optical response that extends beyond the information contained in the underlying electronic band Chern numbers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the problem of quantum causality by defining 'causal confusion'—when measurement data cannot distinguish between a shared common cause and a sequential direct cause. The authors prove that this confusion occurs if and only if the underlying quantum channel possesses the PPT property, providing a new operational meaning for PPT channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous operational definition for PPT channels by linking them to a fundamental concept in quantum causality, which is crucial for understanding the limits of quantum information processing and measurement theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates 'causal confusion,' which is the situation where joint expectation values measured in a spatial (common-cause) quantum scenario coincide with those from a temporal (direct-cause) quantum scenario.

**Main result.** It establishes that for general systems, a quantum channel exhibits causal confusion for some input state if and only if it is a Positive-Partial-Transpose (PPT) channel, and for qubits, this condition is equivalent to the channel being entanglement-breaking.

**Method.** The authors compare joint expectation values calculated under two distinct causal models—a shared bipartite state (spatial) versus sequential measurement through a quantum channel (temporal)—using Pauli observables.

**Model / system.** The study uses general finite-dimensional quantum systems and focuses on local measurements performed by two parties (Alice and Bob) on quantum states, analyzing the structure of quantum channels connecting these measurements.

**Key observables.** Joint expectation values of Pauli observables ($\sigma_j \otimes \sigma_k$), which are used to distinguish between the spatial and temporal correlation structures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on the properties of the quantum channel $E$ (e.g., PPT, entanglement-breaking) and the choice of input state $ho$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to 'light-touch observables' and uses the Lüders' update rule for the temporal scenario in qubit examples.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a Table I summarizing key results relating bipartite states, PPT/CPTP processes, and EB channels to causal confusion, and a $4 	imes 4$ table structure for qubits comparing spatial and temporal expectation values.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the spatial and temporal measurement scenarios, demonstrating the conditions for causal confusion using qubit examples, and culminating in general structural theorems linking confusion to PPT and entanglement-breaking properties.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The joint expectation values of quantum observables measured by two parties typically depend on the causal structure of the measurement protocol. The two causal structures we investigate here are the direct-cause scenario, in which a single quantum state is measured twice in series with a general quantum channel between measurements, and the common-cause scenario, in which a shared bipartite quantum state is measured in parallel. Through a variety of examples of qubit channels with tunable parameters, we identify the full set of input states where the direct-cause joint expectation values of Pauli observables coincide with those obtained from a common cause, a situation that we call causal confusion. More generally, for arbitrary finite-dimensional quantum systems and light-touch observables, we prove that a channel exhibits causal confusion for some input state if and only if it is a positive-partial-transpose (PPT) channel, thereby endowing PPT channels with a new operational meaning in terms of causal confusion. Using this result, we characterize the set of qubit channels for which there exists an input state leading to causal confusion as the set of entanglement-breaking channels.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of training Quantum Neural Networks on noisy, limited quantum hardware by proposing a 'divide-et-impera' training protocol. This method decomposes large quantum circuits into smaller, manageable sub-circuits whose outputs are combined classically. The approach is demonstrated on real-world tasks, suggesting a scalable design principle for future hybrid quantum computing architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on resource-constrained quantum hardware and developing scalable training protocols directly addresses the engineering challenges of implementing quantum algorithms, which is a key concern for quantum information theorists.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models that are feasible to train on current, noisy, and qubit-limited quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The proposed divide-et-impera protocol provides a general design principle for QML algorithms, showing feasibility on real-world tasks like predicting EV charging station states.

**Method.** The authors propose splitting large Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) into smaller, manageable sub-circuits, whose concatenated embeddings are then processed by a classical neural network.

**Model / system.** The model uses Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) trained via a variational quantum circuit (VQC) structure, which is decomposed into multiple smaller quantum processing unit (QPU) calls.

**Key observables.** Prediction scores (e.g., L1 norm for charging stations), Mean Absolute Error (for Air Quality), and the vanishing/concentration of gradients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits, number of trainable parameters, and the error rates from simulated noise channels (Bit-flip, Depolarizing).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the feature space can be partitioned or that information loss from circuit decomposition can be mitigated by a reconstructing network.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the data re-uploading scheme, the decomposition structure (VQC $ightarrow$ Concatenation $ightarrow$ MLP), and heatmaps showing performance degradation under various noise models.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of deep QNNs (Barren Plateaus), proposes the divide-et-impera training protocol, benchmarks this approach on two real-world datasets (EV charging stations and Air Quality Index), and analyzes performance under noise.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum machine learning is rapidly emerging as a promising and potentially more sustainable approach that can complement traditional, energy-hungry HPC, AI, and GPU resources-particularly for the demanding global challenges the world urgently needs to address. In recent years, the first prototypes of quantum processors have reached the market, are sometimes available via the cloud, and distributed quantum computing is beginning to move from a largely theoretical concept to a demonstrated reality. However, these devices are still noisy and qubit-limited. Therefore, it is desirable to design quantum machine learning models with a reduced quantum register and a limited number of quantum gates, avoiding overly deep and wide quantum circuits. Here, we explore this idea by proposing a novel divide-et-impera-based training protocol that splits quantum neural networks into smaller components and better governs their optimization. We benchmark our approach on real data and real-world prediction tasks in intertwined energy and environmental domains, such as the status of charging stations for electric vehicles and the global air quality index. Our results suggest a design principle for quantum machine learning algorithms across a wide range of applications, which can be more feasibly tested on state-of-the-art quantum processors or within modular quantum architectures being developed for future hybrid infrastructures interconnected via high-speed communication links.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel Dual Spin-Sector Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) to accurately calculate excited states in frustrated spin systems like the $J_1$-$J_2$ model. By optimizing across multiple spin sectors and using a penalty cost function, the method resolves ambiguities that plague standard VQE approaches. It successfully reproduces key quantum phases and demonstrates high resilience to noise, advancing the feasibility of quantum simulation for complex magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, advanced quantum algorithm tailored to overcome fundamental limitations (state-ordering inversions) in simulating strongly correlated quantum magnets, which is a major bottleneck for quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) techniques fail to accurately determine excited states and spectral gaps in frustrated spin systems due to state-ordering inversions.

**Main result.** The proposed Dual Spin-Sector VQE framework successfully isolates the true Excited Singlet state, achieving convergence to machine precision and accurately capturing known quantum phases.

**Method.** The authors developed a Dual Spin-Sector VQE by optimizing across distinct magnetization sectors ($S_z=0$ and $S_z=1$) and using a dual-sector penalty cost function to resolve ambiguities.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the spin-1/2 $J_1$-$J_2$ Heisenberg model, applicable to 1D spin chains and 2D rectangular lattices, characterized by frustrated magnetic interactions.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy ($E_0$), Excited Singlet energy ($E_S$), spectral gaps ($\Delta_{SS}, \Delta_{ST}$), and quantum subspace fidelity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $J_2/J_1$ is critical, with key points identified at $J_2/J_1 \approx 0.2411$ (BKT point) and $J_2/J_1 = 0.50$ (Majumdar-Ghosh point).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the exact commutation relation $[H, S^-] = 0$ to map the Triplet state, and it assumes the feasibility of implementing the dual-sector penalty cost function on near-term quantum hardware.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dual spin-sector quantum circuit pipeline, the NISQ overlap circuit for penalty terms, and comparisons showing the failure of standard VQD versus the resilience of the Dual VQE under finite measurement shot noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the failure mode of standard VQE methods in frustrated magnets. It then introduces and details the Dual Spin-Sector VQE framework, showing its application to 1D and 2D lattices, benchmarking its performance against known physical points, and finally demonstrating its resource efficiency for near-term quantum hardware.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Finding excited states and excitation gaps in frustrated spin systems using Variational Quantum Eigensolvers (VQE) is complicated by state-ordering inversions. In the frustrated spin-1/2 $J_1$-$J_2$ Heisenberg model, standard single-sector algorithms like Variational Quantum Deflation (VQD) in $S_z=0$ fail when $J_2/J_1 < 0.25$. In this regime, the lowest-lying excitation in $S_z=0$ is a Triplet ($S=1$), which lies below the Excited Singlet ($S=0$). Standard deflation projects out the ground singlet and converges onto the Triplet state, misidentifying it as the Excited Singlet. Here, we present a Dual Spin-Sector VQE framework that resolves these state-ordering inversions across 1D spin chains and 2D rectangular lattices. By first optimizing the ground Triplet state in the $S_z=1$ sector (where singlet states cannot exist), mapping it into $S_z=0$ via the total spin-lowering operator $S^- = \sum_i S_i^-$, and evaluating a dual-sector penalty cost function, we isolate the Excited Singlet ($E_S$) without variational ambiguity. Numerical state-vector simulations confirm exact convergence to machine precision with unit overlap fidelity. The method captures key physical features, including the gapless phase, the BKT critical point at $J_2/J_1 \approx 0.2411$, the Majumdar-Ghosh dimer point ($J_2/J_1 = 0.50$), and the 2D non-magnetic frustrated regime ($J_2/J_1 \approx 0.40 - 0.60$). Finite-shot sampling simulations and gate-scaling analytics up to $N=100$ qubits demonstrate the resource efficiency and shot resilience of the algorithm for near-term quantum execution.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors analyze the energy spectra of ultracold erbium atoms to characterize their degree of quantum chaos. By employing multi-scale statistical diagnostics, they find the spectra exhibit intermediate statistics, best described by semi-Poisson behavior rather than purely chaotic or integrable limits. This suggests the underlying collision dynamics involve a complex, hierarchical mixing of phase space regions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a quantitative spectral fingerprint for intermediate quantum chaos in a highly controllable quantum gas system, offering diagnostic tools applicable to other complex quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the spectral statistics of quantum systems exhibiting intermediate quantum chaos, bridging the gap between integrable and fully chaotic limits.

**Main result.** The spectral statistics of ultracold erbium atoms are consistently described by semi-Poisson statistics, indicating intermediate quantum chaos that is scale-dependent.

**Method.** A multi-scale statistical analysis is employed using higher-order spacing ratios, accumulated power spectral density (PSD) analysis, and spacing increment distributions.

**Model / system.** The study uses the resonance spectra of ultracold erbium atoms ($^{166}	ext{Er}$ and $^{168}	ext{Er}$) obtained via Fano-Feshbach resonances, which model a system with mixed phase space dynamics.

**Key observables.** Higher-order spacing ratios ($r^{(k)}_n$), accumulated power spectral density exponent ($\alpha$), and spacing increment distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Intermediate quantum chaos regime; crossover parameter $T$; spectral rigidity exponent $\alpha \approx 1.5$; semi-Poisson statistics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating resonance positions as sharp eigenvalues and comparing empirical data against theoretical models like the ATAS surmise and stochastic crossover frameworks.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare empirical distributions of higher-order ratios against Wigner-Dyson and semi-Poisson predictions, and show the scale-dependent behavior of the accumulated PSD.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes spectral correlations using multiple diagnostics (ratios, PSD, increments) to build a consistent picture of intermediate chaos, linking these statistics to a stochastic crossover model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Characterizing intermediate quantum chaos in systems with mixed phase spaces requires probing spectral correlations across multiple scales. Here, we present a multi-scale statistical analysis of the resonance spectra of ultracold 166Er and 168Er atoms using higher-order spacing ratios, spacing increments, and the accumulated power spectral density of decimated level sequences. Our analysis reveals that while the spectra exhibit clear level repulsion, they are consistently better described by semi-Poisson than by standard Wigner-Dyson ensembles, with this intermediate character persisting across higher-order correlations. The accumulated power spectrum reveals an intermediate 1/f^alpha regime, with alpha approximately 1.5 at full spectral resolution and a tendency toward the integrable random-walk limit alpha = 2 at intermediate decimation scales, indicating a scale-dependent loss of spectral rigidity. Furthermore, the spacing-increment distributions independently reveal statistics intermediate between Laplace and Gaussian limits. Together, these complementary diagnostics show that the semi-Poisson character reflects persistent non-Wigner-Dyson correlations beyond the nearest-neighbor scale, providing a consistent picture of intermediate quantum chaos and a possible connection to hierarchical trapping in the underlying collision dynamics.

</details>

### [Quantum Optimization of the Color-of-Money Problem on IBM Quantum Hardware: A Proof-of-Concept Study](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09798v1)

**Authors:** Rahul Rana, Suman Kumar Roy, M Girish Chandra  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09798v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `QC/QI experiment` **3/5**

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a proof-of-concept quantum solution for the complex Color-of-Money financial optimization problem. It reformulates the problem into a QUBO and uses a Variational Quantum Algorithm (VQA) with Pauli Correlation Encoding (PCE) on IBM Quantum hardware. The results confirm the quantum method's ability to match classical optimal solutions, establishing a foundation for quantum optimization in finance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is finance-related, the core methodology—mapping combinatorial optimization to QUBO and solving it via VQA-PCE on real hardware—is a direct demonstration of quantum algorithm implementation for constrained optimization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Color-of-Money (CoM) problem, a complex financial optimization challenge involving resource allocation under multiple constraints.

**Main result.** The quantum approach successfully reproduced the optimal solution found by a classical exact solver (SCIP) on a prototype instance, validating the methodology's feasibility.

**Method.** It uses a Variational Quantum Algorithm (VQA) combined with Pauli Correlation Encoding (PCE) to map the problem into a QUBO format executable on quantum hardware.

**Model / system.** The problem is modeled as a constrained Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) problem, transformed into a QUBO objective function, and implemented on IBM Quantum hardware.

**Key observables.** The objective function value (e.g., 151.00) and the expectation values of Pauli correlation observables $\langle P_i angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Penalty coefficients ($\lambda_1$ to $\lambda_4$), the scaling parameter $\alpha$, and the variational circuit parameters $	heta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methodology assumes that the quantum approach can correctly map and solve the optimization problem, although current hardware noise limits outperformances on small instances.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the entire workflow: MILP $ightarrow$ QUBO $ightarrow$ VQA-PCE Training (Simulator) $ightarrow$ Hardware Execution (with error mitigation) $ightarrow$ Solution.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure moving from problem definition (MILP/CoM) to quantum formulation (QUBO/VQA-PCE), detailing the hybrid quantum-classical workflow, and concluding with hardware execution and benchmarking against classical solvers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Color-of-Money (CoM) problem is a financial optimization challenge in which enterprises must determine how to allocate and route funds across multiple accounts, currencies, and time periods while satisfying regulatory, operational, and liquidity constraints. As the number of assets, obligations, and planning periods increases, the decision space grows rapidly, making large-scale optimization increasingly challenging for classical approaches. In this work, we present a proof-of-concept quantum implementation of the CoM problem using IBM Quantum hardware. The original optimization problem is formulated as a constrained Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) problem and transformed into a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) representation suitable for quantum execution. We employ a Variational Quantum Algorithm (VQA) with Pauli Correlation Encoding (PCE), a qubit efficient encoding technique and apply error-mitigation technique to improve solution fidelity on noisy quantum hardware. A prototype instance consisting of 3 assets, 3 obligations, and 2 time periods was executed on real IBM quantum hardware and benchmarked against classical exact solver SCIP. The quantum approach successfully reproduced the same optimal solution obtained classically, validating the correctness and feasibility of the proposed methodology. While current noisy quantum hardware does not outperform classical optimization on the evaluated small instance, the results demonstrate the feasibility of executing treasury optimization workloads on current quantum hardware and provide a foundation for future investigations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and tests advanced quantum algorithms for calculating the anharmonic vibrational spectra of molecules using quantum hardware. It compares several unitary-cluster-Jastrow ansätze, finding that hardware noise limits performance, but demonstrating that structured quantum sampling outperforms random guessing. The work successfully calculates fundamental vibrational frequencies on IBM QPUs, advancing the field's practical application to molecular physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the application of quantum computation to molecular structure and dynamics, a core area of AMO physics, by benchmarking advanced quantum algorithms against real-world hardware noise.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses extending sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) to accurately calculate anharmonic vibrational spectra of molecules using current quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The novel VLUCJ ansatz proved to be the most hardware-robust circuit structure, while the study successfully demonstrated calculating fundamental vibrational frequencies on IBM QPUs.

**Method.** The authors employ SQD, warm-starting the process with vibrational coupled-cluster (VCC) amplitudes, and comparing multiple unitary-cluster-Jastrow ansätze on superconducting quantum processors.

**Model / system.** The system models are the vibrational spectra of small molecules ($	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$, $	ext{CH}_2	ext{O}$, $	ext{CH}_2	ext{ClF}$), governed by a quartic force-field Hamiltonian derived from DFT calculations.

**Key observables.** Ground-state and excited-state energies, fundamental vibrational frequencies, and the fraction of physically valid one-hot outcomes (retention).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count up to 72, comparison between harmonic-oscillator and vibrational self-consistent-field (VSCF) reference bases, and the anharmonic coupling terms up to quartic order.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The accuracy relies on the underlying potential energy surface approximation and the comparison highlights a trade-off between ansatz expressivity and hardware robustness.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy gaps and circuit performance (CZ count, depth) across different ansätze and sampling methods (random vs. structured quantum sample) on IBM hardware.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the theoretical framework (Hamiltonian, SQD), details the comparison of five ansätze, benchmarks performance on IBM QPUs by comparing results against random sampling and classical VSCF targets, and concludes with the calculation of representative vibrational frequencies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We extend sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) to anharmonic vibrational spectra and use it to benchmark how vibrational ansätze scale on present-day hardware under a direct one-hot encoding (up to 72 qubits). Five particle-conserving circuits are warm-started through vibrational coupled-cluster (VCC) t1, t2 amplitudes: UVCCSD, the compact heuristic circuit (CHC), and three unitary-cluster-Jastrow ansätze introduced here, VLUCJ, Vg-uCJ, and VIm-uCJ. A self-consistent configuration-recovery loop reconstructs correlated states from hardware samples in the low-retention regime where naïve post-selection breaks down, enabling the calculation of ground-state energies for H$_2$O, CH$_2$O, and CH$_2$ClF (3-9 modes). We compare harmonic-oscillator and vibrational self-consistent-field (VSCF, "modal") reference bases. In the small-modal-basis regime relevant for near-term devices, the modal basis is typically more accurate than the harmonic-oscillator representation; empirically, it also yields substantially better-converged hardware sampling for deep circuits at these qubit counts. Across ansätze, we find a clear retention/accuracy tradeoff when scaling: the new VLUCJ circuit is the most hardware-robust, retaining the largest fraction of physically valid one-hot outcomes, whereas the more expressive uCJ variants and UVCCSD can recover more correlation when retention is sufficient. Finally, we extend the recovery protocol to excited states by targeting a chosen per-mode VSCF occupation and warm-starting it with a state-specific VCC calculation, and we demonstrate the calculation of one representative fundamental vibrational frequency per molecule on IBM QPUs.

</details>

C1: All sys- tems, showing an approximate power law of N 2, C2: Seg- regated by mechanisms: balanced scales with N, while left- moving walls (which grow in abundance...</sub>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2610.09879_figures/2610.09879_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 4: Behavior of the system under noise. A: A plot of the errors propagating in the model with random bit ﬂips. Domain walls form in pairs, and spread with a constant drift. B: The probability for an error to lead to the formation of a domain wall, for each rule. Four out of nine never split into walls. p is estimated as the average. C, D: The steady-state fraction of domain walls as a function of noise, showing √ε scaling (C) and the rescaled fraction of domain walls over time (D), with the rate equation prediction using parameters for p = 0.23 and v = (vL + vR)/2 = 0.2275 estimated from simulations.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper shows that the universal computation power of Rule 110 can be replicated using only three simple, mirror-symmetric rules arranged periodically. The complexity and inherent chirality of the resulting system emerge from this arrangement, not from the rules themselves. The analysis quantitatively models how noise introduces errors, predicting that the resulting domain wall density scales with the square root of the noise strength.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, non-quantum example of how emergent complexity and broken symmetries (chirality) can arise purely from the *arrangement* of simple, local, and symmetric components, which has parallels in designing complex quantum systems from simple local interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates that complex, universal computation (Turing completeness) can emerge from the arrangement of simple, individually trivial, and symmetric local rules, rather than requiring inherent asymmetry in the rules themselves.

**Main result.** Rule 110 can be emulated by alternating three mirror-symmetric rules, and the system's robustness and error dynamics (domain wall scaling) are quantitatively predicted and observed.

**Method.** The authors used the framework of Elementary Cellular Automata (ECA), constructing a complex rule via a periodic mixture of three simple rules and analyzing the resulting domain wall dynamics under random noise.

**Model / system.** The model is an Elementary Cellular Automaton (ECA) lattice. The complexity is achieved by tiling the lattice with three simple, symmetric rules (Rule 36, 37, and 5) in a repeating sequence to mimic the behavior of the complex Rule 110.

**Key observables.** Domain wall density ($n$), system locking time, and the scaling of domain wall density with noise strength ($\sqrt{\epsilon}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($\epsilon$), the ratio of domain wall density to lattice size ($n/L$), and the parameters governing the rate equation ($rac{dn}{dt} = 6p\epsilon - 4v (n/2)^2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a computational-mechanics picture where domain walls move ballistically, and the rate equation provides a good description of the steady state, although the final approach to equilibrium is noted to be slower than predicted.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between the complex target rule (Rule 110) and its simple components, show the time evolution and locking times for random initial conditions, and demonstrate the steady-state domain wall fraction scaling with $\sqrt{\epsilon}$ matching the rate equation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the construction of Rule 110 from simple rules, then analyzes the system's self-organization from random initial conditions, and finally models the error propagation by introducing noise and deriving/testing a rate equation for the domain wall density.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the Turing complete rule 110 in elementary cellular automata can be emulated by alternating three simple symmetric rules in space. As the constituent rules themselves are trivial, this shows that diversity alone can generate emergent phenomena. The rules cannot distinguish left from right, and the chirality of rule 110 is created by their arrangement. Random initial conditions lock on to rule 110 over 90% of the time, explained through the motion of domain walls between desynchronized regions. Adding random noise creates errors that seed domain walls, whose fraction scales with the square root of noise strength, explained through a simple rate equation. This points to a route to complexity through repeated, perturbed units, common in biology, and shows that in cellular automaton models of quantum field theory, broken symmetries such as parity need not be present in the local rules.

</details>

### [Q-PhotoMarket: A Design Space Exploration Framework for Photonic Hybrid Quantum Neural Networks in Financial Market Prediction](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09641v1)

**Authors:** Alberto Marchisio, Hanzalah Mohamed Siraj, Muhammad Kashif, Nouhaila Innan, Muhammad Shafique  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09641v1>

**Summary.** This work introduces Q-PhotoMarket, a systematic framework to explore the vast design space of Photonic Hybrid Quantum Neural Networks (HQNNs) for financial forecasting. By testing thousands of photonic configurations, the authors demonstrate that the specific choice of circuit architecture and measurement strategy critically determines predictive power. The findings confirm that quantum circuit design is a crucial, often overlooked, factor in realizing quantum machine learning applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is finance, the core methodology involves optimizing complex quantum circuit parameters (topology, measurement) using advanced search techniques (BO/TPE), which is highly relevant to quantum algorithm design and resource estimation in quantum information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the lack of systematic investigation into how specific photonic circuit design choices influence the predictive performance of Hybrid Quantum Neural Networks (HQNNs) when applied to financial market prediction.

**Main result.** Systematic exploration of over 5,000 photonic configurations reveals consistent, measurable architectural patterns across different financial markets, demonstrating that the choice of circuit topology and measurement strategy significantly impacts performance relative to classical baselines.

**Method.** A systematic Design Space Exploration (DSE) framework, Q-PhotoMarket, is employed, combining exhaustive search with Bayesian Optimization to test various photonic circuit parameters.

**Model / system.** The model is a Photonic HQNN implemented using linear-optical circuits (beam splitters, phase shifters) simulated via the Merlin framework. The computation is hybrid, coupling a trainable photonic QuantumLayer with a classical MLP head.

**Key observables.** Area Under the Curve (AUC), Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC), F1-score, and the General Performance Score (GPS) are used to evaluate predictive performance across U.S., Indian, and cryptocurrency markets.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The design space includes input photon states (e.g., Alternating, Bunched), circuit architectures (e.g., Clements Mesh, Simple), entangling models (MZI, Bell), and measurement strategies (Fock, Dual-rail).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the shared statistical structure across different financial markets allows for meaningful cross-market comparisons, and the performance is highly sensitive to the specific photonic design choices made.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the distribution of AUC across the design space, show DSE diagnostics for different markets (S&P 500, NIFTY 50, Crypto) across various thresholds, and compare performance against classical ML baselines.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the Q-PhotoMarket DSE framework, describes the multi-dimensional search space, presents results showing architectural dependencies, and concludes with comparisons to classical methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic quantum computing has recently emerged as a promising platform for hybrid quantum machine learning due to its native realization of linear-optical circuits and the computational complexity of boson sampling. However, despite growing interest in quantum methods for finance, the influence of photonic circuit design choices on predictive performance remains largely unexplored. Existing studies typically evaluate a single architecture, leaving the broader photonic design space unexamined. In this work, we present Q-PhotoMarket, a systematic design space exploration (DSE) framework for photonic hybrid quantum neural networks (HQNNs) applied to financial market prediction. We explore over 5,000 valid photonic configurations spanning input photon states, circuit architectures, entangling models, and measurement strategies across their compatible computation spaces, for U.S., Indian, and cryptocurrency markets. To improve search efficiency, the exhaustive exploration is complemented with Bayesian optimization. We further incorporate threshold calibration and prediction-collapse diagnostics to enable reliable evaluation under increasingly imbalanced return thresholds. Experimental results show that systematic exploration of more than 5,000 photonic HQNN configurations reveals consistent architectural patterns across financial markets, identifies robust high-performing designs, and demonstrates competitive performance relative to classical machine learning baselines.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper unifies Quantum Darwinism and Decoherent Histories by proposing the concept of 'past redundancy' as the fundamental requirement for observable records in quantum systems. It argues that this concept is more general than QD, suggesting that redundancy can arise from mechanisms other than those previously assumed. This offers a richer, more realistic framework for investigating quantum objectivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the foundational question of quantum objectivity by providing a broader, less fine-tuned mechanism (past redundancy) for information recording than standard Quantum Darwinism, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to unify two major paradigms for understanding quantum records—Decoherent Histories (DH) and Quantum Darwinism (QD)—by focusing on the concept of 'past redundancy' in isolated quantum systems.

**Main result.** The proposed framework shows that past redundancy is a more general phenomenon than the fine-tuned conditions required for QD, allowing for redundancy caused by mechanisms beyond standard QD assumptions.

**Method.** The authors develop a unified formalism by analyzing the necessary conditions for redundant records across time, employing concepts like quantum mutual information and the Bhattacharyya coefficient.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on isolated quantum systems, often modeled by coupling a system (S) to an environment (E) split into fragments, using generalized CNOT-fan-out models for illustration.

**Key observables.** Quantum Mutual Information (MI), the Bhattacharyya coefficient (BC), and the error probability in hypothesis testing ($	ext{P}_{	ext{error}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of environment fragments ($N$), the size of the system ($n$), and the time evolution parameters ($	au_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial state in specific models (like CNOT-fan-out) is sometimes considered 'fine-tuned,' and the analysis relies on bounding error probabilities using inequalities like Fano's.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the environment fragments and show the characteristic redundancy plateau in quantum mutual information, and one figure plots decoherence decay as a function of fragment size.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the limitations of DH and QD separately, then introducing 'past redundancy' as the unifying concept, and finally deriving bounds on error probabilities that quantify this redundancy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

There exist two different paradigms for records in isolated quantum systems: decoherent histories (which determines which past events leave formal records) and quantum Darwinism (which determines which current events get redundantly recorded). Neither one implies the other, and connections between the two are scarcely investigated. We unify quantum Darwinism and decoherent histories based on the insight that only redundant records about past events matter. The resulting framework of past redundancy encompasses quantum Darwinism when applied to events at the current time, and it connects redundancy to decoherence of histories and the probability sum rule via the Bhattacharyya coefficient (or fidelity). Importantly, various examples show that past redundancy is a more widespread phenomenon than the fine-tuned models necessary for quantum Darwinism suggest. We thus arrive at a richer picture in which redundancy can be caused by mechanisms different from quantum Darwinism (such as macroevents or cascade structures), which emphasizes that redundancy is necessary but not sufficient for objectivity, and which, overall, allows to realistically investigate the question: how generic is redundancy in nature?

</details>
