**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines how an external potential gradient across a p-n junction can induce a Mott gap closing in a Hubbard lattice. Using DMFT on plaquette clusters, the authors find that the transition to a correlated metal is governed by the potential drop matching the plaquette's excitation energy. The results highlight the directional dependence of this transition, suggesting that crystal symmetry plays a crucial role in stabilizing the metallic state.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides detailed insights into how spatial inhomogeneity (the p-n junction) can tune the Mott insulating state, offering a theoretical framework applicable to engineered correlated materials or interfaces.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Mott gap closing mechanism induced by a spatially varying external potential gradient within a p-n junction formed in a Hubbard lattice.

**Main result.** The system transitions to a correlated metallic phase when the potential drop strength becomes comparable to the excitation energy of an open 2x2 plaquette, with the diagonal direction exhibiting enhanced coherence.

**Method.** The research employs Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT) coupled with the analysis of inhomogeneous 2x2 plaquette impurity clusters solved via CTQMC simulations.

**Model / system.** The model is the Hubbard Hamiltonian on a square lattice, specifically analyzing the physics at a p-n junction where the on-site potential varies smoothly across the interface, characterized by directions [10] and [11].

**Key observables.** The primary observables include the imaginary part of the central impurity Green's function at zero Matsubara frequency, and the coherent peak intensity in the correlated metal spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the hopping parameter (t), the on-site repulsion (U), and the external potential drop strength ($\epsilon_{ext}$), with transition thresholds found around $\sim 0.35t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the inhomogeneous problem onto $2	imes2$ plaquette impurity clusters within the DMFT framework, and comparisons between different potential profiles are made.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the potential profiles along [10] and [11], the calculated impurity Green's functions showing the transition, and energy level diagrams for the $2	imes2$ plaquette showing degeneracy changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hubbard model in a p-n junction, comparing the effects of potential gradients along different lattice directions ([10] vs [11]) using DMFT on plaquette clusters to determine the critical field strength for the insulator-to-metal transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mott gap closing induced by an external potential gradient is studied by considering the Hubbard model on a square lattice. Our model consists of hole- and electron-doped domains forming the p-n junction where the on-site potential varies smoothly across the interface. The effect of the potential drop direction, either along square edge or diagonal, is studied by describing the correlation effects of inhomogeneous 2x2 plaquette impurity clusters within a dynamical mean-field theory framework. It is found that the metallic phase is favored over the Mott insulators when the potential drop becomes comparable to the energy eigenvalue difference of an open 2x2 plaquette between the ground state and the first excited state. Given the same associated electric-field strength, the excitation energy is almost independent of the drop direction, which amounts ~0.35t at U=5.8t with the hopping parameter t. Upon insulator-to-metal transition, the correlated metal exhibits a coherent peak at zero energy whose intensity is greater for the drop direction along the square diagonal than along the edge. The main origin is attributed to the preserved degeneracy of two molecular orbitals for the former case, while it undergoes splitting in the energy levels for the latter. Our result is discussed regarding the experimentally measured Mott gap closing thresholds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a specialized cavity message-passing algorithm incorporating a trust region to analyze complex, non-convex energy landscapes. By limiting the information passed locally, the method achieves stable convergence where standard techniques fail. It successfully characterizes three distinct landscape regimes, providing a robust tool for studying local minima in disordered systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core model is classical optimization, the use of message-passing algorithms, cavity methods, and the analysis of complex energy landscapes strongly connects to concepts used in quantum many-body physics, particularly in understanding glassy dynamics and phase transitions in disordered quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the difficulty of analyzing complex, high-dimensional energy landscapes arising from non-convex optimization problems, particularly when standard message-passing algorithms fail to converge.

**Main result.** The proposed trust-region cavity message-passing algorithm successfully reveals the structure of these landscapes by limiting local information transfer, demonstrating robustness across different landscape regimes.

**Method.** A novel cavity message-passing algorithm is introduced that incorporates a trust region, restricting message updates to a local window around a reference point, thereby improving convergence in rugged energy landscapes.

**Model / system.** The study uses a routing coordination problem on a sparse random graph, whose cost function's convexity is controlled by a parameter $\gamma$. The analysis is framed within the context of statistical physics message-passing techniques.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the converged local minima energies, the diversity of low-energy minima, and the stability of the fixed points when the trust region size is progressively increased.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the cost function exponent $\gamma$ (controlling convexity), the sender fraction $f_p$, and the trust region size $M$. Three regimes are identified: maximally rugged, intermediate hierarchical, and smooth.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the goal is to identify locally stable fixed points rather than the global optimum. The method avoids introducing second-order approximations by directly truncating the message domain.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the trust-region cavity field for varying $M$, and show stability tests (e.g., Fig. 3, Fig. 13) confirming that enlarging the trust region does not fundamentally change the identified local minima.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem using a specific graph optimization model, proposes the trust-region cavity algorithm, analyzes the resulting energy landscape structure across three distinct regimes based on $\gamma$, and validates the method's stability through progressive and independent single-edge expansion tests.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Energy landscapes of non-convex optimization problems are high-dimensional surfaces that are difficult to reveal, visualize, or analyze. Message-passing algorithms, or equivalently cavity approaches in statistical physics, may fail to identify local minima as messages do not converge owing to the ruggedness of the energy landscapes. Here we aim to reveal the characteristics of these complex energy landscapes by limiting the amount of information passed in local messages, improving the convergence of messages for local minima. By studying a routing optimization problem with its convexity governed by a single parameter, we introduce a cavity message-passing algorithm with a trust region, and sample ensembles of converged local minima of rugged energy landscapes across many independent realizations of the same problem instances. We observe three regimes with different characteristics of the energy landscapes: (1) a maximally rugged regime with a peak in the diversity of low-energy minima; (2) an intermediate regime with a hierarchical multi-cluster organization of low-energy solutions; and (3) a smooth regime dominated by a single minimum. Additional tests show that the converged energies are largely insensitive to the size of the trust region if the size is small or moderate, demonstrating the robustness of the proposed approach in identifying characteristics of rugged energy landscapes.

</details>

P contains problems that can be solved “quickly” (i.e. in polynomial time), while problems in NP can have their solutions verified “quickly” (see Fig. 2.2). Both P and NP contain decision problems. NP-hard contains problems that need not be themselves decision problems nor verifiable “quickly”. For example, they might be decision problems that are ‘undecidable’, like the Halting Problem [82, 83]; thus NP-hard is a strict superset of NP. Yet, any NP problem can be mapped to an NP-hard problem “quickly”. In that sense, NP-hard is at least as hard as NP: if a solution to an NP-hard problem...</sub>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2610.09855_figures/2610.09855_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 2.2: P vs NP: an illustration. A decision problem L is in P if its solution can be found (i.e. it can be decided) by a deterministic Turing machine M in polynomial time (polynomially many “ticks”). A decision problem L is in NP if, provided a witness W supplies us with a proof y that our problem has a solution, M can verify the solution in polynomially many ticks. The proof y must be “written in polynomially many pages” in terms of the size |x| of the input-problem.</sub>

</details>

Due to the differential rotation of S2, the grey point on the z = 0 slice rotates faster than that on the z = z0 slice, leading to the blue points after the map τS2 is applied which are now further apart. On the other hand, the isometric diffeomorphism πS2 rotates all points at the same rate and preserves the original grey distance between the points.</sub>

Each affine diagram (at right) may be obtained by adding a single “extending root” to the cor- responding finite crystallographic diagram (at left); or, alternatively, by removing a single extending node (any of the black nodes) from any affine diagram (at right) we obtain the corresponding finite crystallographic diagram (at left).</sub>

**Summary.** This paper bridges quantum gravity, computational complexity, and geometry by examining spacetime quasicrystals. It argues that semiclassical gravity implies computational power beyond classical limits, suggesting quantum gravity. Furthermore, it develops the theory of Lorentzian quasicrystals and uses N-V tests to confirm their unique, hyperuniform structure in spacetime.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly focused on AMO systems, the study of non-linear dynamics (qubits in curved spacetime) and the use of advanced mathematical structures (quasicrystals) to model fundamental physics are highly relevant to theoretical quantum information and quantum gravity research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper explores the deep connections between solving computationally hard problems, the structure of spacetime geometry via quasicrystals, and the fundamental principles of quantum gravity.

**Main result.** The authors show that semiclassical gravity dynamics can violate the Physical Extended Church–Turing Thesis, suggesting quantum gravity, and they establish that spacetime quasicrystals exhibit novel properties like spacetime hyperuniformity.

**Method.** The work employs two main theoretical approaches: analyzing the weak-field dynamics of qubits coupled to classical gravity, and performing Number-Volume (N-V) tests on constructed Lorentzian quasicrystals in Minkowski spacetime.

**Model / system.** The models include the dynamics of a massive qubit under semiclassical Einstein field equations, and the construction/analysis of Lorentzian quasicrystals, which are generalizations of Penrose tilings to spacetime.

**Key observables.** Computational complexity (NP-completeness), violation of the Physical Extended Church–Turing Thesis, Number-Volume counts ($\langle Nangle$), and the standard deviation of point counts ($\sigma_{N\diamond}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Planck scale ($M_{m Pl}$), vacuum energy scale ($M_{m vac}$), electroweak scale ($M_{m EW}$), and the dimensionality of spacetime (e.g., (3+1)D).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis in Part I assumes the gravitational field is classical and couples semiclassically to quantum fields. Part II relies on constructing highly symmetric quasi-crystalline causal sets.

**Figures summary.** The figures present extensive N-V test data for various lattices (Poisson, integer, Fibonacci) in (2+1)D and (3+1)D, illustrating how the standard deviation of point counts ($\sigma_{N\diamond}$) varies with the boost parameter, demonstrating structural differences.

**Paper structure.** The paper is divided into two parts: Part I addresses the computational power derived from semiclassical gravity, leading to a violation of the Church–Turing Thesis. Part II generalizes quasicrystals to Minkowski spacetime, proposing connections to string theory compactifications and testing their hyperuniformity via N-V tests.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Part I: Semiclassical Gravity Efficiently Solves $\mathsf{NP}$-Complete Problems   Assuming the gravitational field is classical and that it couples to quantum fields via the semiclassical Einstein field equations (EFEs), we show that the weak-field dynamics of a massive and non-relativistic qubit can in principle be used to solve an $\mathsf{NP}$-complete problem in polynomial time. We attribute this vast computational power to the non-linear qubit dynamics afforded by the semiclassical EFEs. Consequently, the above two assumptions entail a violation of the Physical Extended Church--Turing Thesis, which we regard as evidence for the quantization of gravity.   Part II: Spacetime Quasicrystals   We generalize self-similar quasicrystals, such as the Penrose tilings, from Euclidean space to Minkowski spacetime. We construct the first examples of Lorentzian quasicrystals and identify their key novel features. We then explore possible connections to fundamental physics. First, we propose a speculative scenario in which our $(3+1)$D universe is embedded in a particularly symmetric $(9+1)$D torus $\mathbb{T}^{9,1}$, previously identified as the most symmetric toroidal compactification of the superstring. We suggest that this construction may shed light on the seesaw relation $M_{\rm Pl}M_{\rm vac} \approx M_{\rm EW}^{2}$ between the Planck, vacuum-energy, and electroweak scales. Second, we replace the random causal sets of causal set theory with ordered, highly symmetric quasi-crystalline causal sets. We find suggestive evidence that our quasicrystals outperform Poisson random sets in the number-volume correspondence in both $(1+1)$D and $(3+1)$D, where such behaviour has been conjectured to be impossible. Finally, extending hyperuniformity from space to spacetime, we show that our $(1+1)$D quasicrystal is the first demonstrated example of a spacetime-hyperuniform structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs a family of quantum field theories that exhibit superfluidity at every temperature by controlling the symmetry breaking mechanism through the renormalization group flow. By analyzing $eta$-functions in the large-$N$ limit with finite-$N$ corrections, the authors prove the existence of an infinite family of such theories, which also manifest superconductivity when coupled to electromagnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous construction of a superfluid state at all temperatures in a controlled QFT framework is highly relevant to understanding exotic phases of matter, such as those found in condensed matter systems or in the context of quantum field theory renormalization group flow.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to construct unitary, local, 3+1-dimensional conformal field theories whose thermal state spontaneously breaks a continuous global symmetry at every temperature.

**Main result.** They constructed an infinite family of such theories where the superfluid nature of the thermal state is under parametric perturbative control, and weakly gauging the symmetry yields a superconductor over an exponential temperature range.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating and analyzing $eta$-functions up to two loops, comparing the planar limit ($N 	o \infty$) to finite-$N$ corrections ($1/N^2$), and determining fixed points by solving $eta^{(1)}_	heta = 0$.

**Model / system.** The model consists of CFTs defined on $R^3 	imes S^1_eta$ involving Dirac fermions and complex scalars coupled via gauge groups $SU(N_i^a) 	imes SU(N_i^b)$. The system exhibits superfluidity and superconductivity.

**Key observables.** Superfluid stiffness, Meissner mass ($m_M$), thermal free energy density ($\Delta f^*$), and the existence of ordered fixed points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The large-$N$ limit, finite-$N$ corrections ($1/N^2$), and the coupling constants $\lambda^*_i$ which can be driven to arbitrarily weak values.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theories are assumed to be unitary, local, and possess finitely many fields. The analysis relies on systematic perturbative expansions in $1/N^2$ and $ar{\lambda}$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the transition from a planar circle of fixed points to discrete surviving fixed points at finite $N$. Table I summarizes the fixed points found by analyzing one- and two-loop $eta$-functions for various theories.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the $eta$-functions in the large-$N$ limit, identifies the ordered arc of fixed points, incorporates finite-$N$ corrections via a lift, and finally demonstrates the existence of an infinite family of theories exhibiting superfluidity and superconductivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct unitary, local, 3+1-dimensional conformal field theories with finitely many fields whose thermal state breaks a continuous global symmetry at every temperature. They are finite-$N$ versions of the Chaudhuri--Choi--Rabinovici theories, whose planar circle of fixed points has an ordered arc. The circle is lifted at order $1/N^2$, and an infinite family of fixed points on this arc reaches arbitrarily weak coupling, where the superfluid is under parametric control. We also show that weakly gauging the symmetry gives a superconductor over an exponential range of temperatures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced optimal control technique based on geodesic search to mitigate leakage errors in superconducting qubits. By smoothly shaping control pulses and deriving an effective Hamiltonian, the authors demonstrate significant improvements in gate fidelity over existing methods for both single- and two-qubit operations. This advances the practical viability of superconducting quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical hardware limitation (leakage) in superconducting quantum processors by merging advanced concepts from optimal control theory (differential geometry) with solid-state quantum physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to suppress leakage errors in superconducting qubits during quantum gate operations, which arise from population leaking to higher energy states.

**Main result.** The proposed geodesic-based optimal control method significantly improves average gate fidelity and reduces leakage errors for single-qubit rotations compared to standard pulses, while achieving higher fidelity for two-qubit gates at the cost of slightly longer evolution times.

**Method.** The authors adapt an optimal control technique using sub-Riemannian geodesic search on the unitary group, incorporating an envelope function to ensure smooth control pulse turn-on and turn-off.

**Model / system.** The system models two superconducting qubits coupled via a tunable coupler, treated as anharmonic LC oscillators. The dynamics are governed by a full Hamiltonian, which is then simplified using Schrieffer-Wolff and Rotating-Wave Approximations to derive an effective Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Average gate fidelity, leakage error rate, and the resulting unitary operation U(τ).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit frequencies, anharmonicities, coupling strengths, and the specific control pulse parameters (e.g., for $R_x(\pi/2)$ and iSWAP gates).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the coupler remains in its ground state (Schrieffer-Wolff) and applies the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA) to simplify the Hamiltonian.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews the geodesic optimal control formalism and introduces leakage noise mitigation. It then details the physical model for two coupled qubits, deriving the effective Hamiltonian through sequential approximations (SW, RWA). Finally, it presents simulation results comparing the optimal pulses to standard pulses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting qubits are often based on the first two energy levels of a physical anharmonic LC oscillator. Since the anharmonicity of such systems is usually much lower than the involved energy gaps, population can easily leak to higher energy states. This is what characterizes leakage errors, and it can have multiple sources: from coherent error induced by the control platform to incoherent thermal dissipation. In this work, we adapt an optimal control method based on sub-Riemannian geodesic search in the unitary group for a system consisting of two superconducting qubits with leakage levels. We use the $R_x(π/2)$ rotation for single-qubit operation analysis, and the $i$SWAP operation for two-qubit analysis. For the former, we find considerable performance improvement in average gate fidelity as well as in the reduction of leakage errors when comparing the obtained optimal pulses with Gaussian and DRAG pulses, especially for short duration pulses. For the $i$SWAP gate, although leakage error remains roughly the same, we manage to obtain considerably higher fidelities with the cost of requiring a slightly longer time evolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel, unsupervised framework to select quantum kernels for anomaly detection by optimizing two metrics: predictive quality (NPD) and structural separation from classical methods (GD). Using real malware data on quantum hardware, the authors demonstrate a fundamental trade-off, showing that the best performance is achieved by a kernel that balances these two objectives rather than maximizing separation alone.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly tests quantum machine learning kernels on a real-world cybersecurity problem, providing empirical benchmarks for the utility of quantum feature maps in data analysis, which is highly relevant to quantum information applications.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a systematic, unsupervised framework to select optimal quantum kernels for anomaly detection, balancing predictive performance against the degree of geometric separation from classical kernels.

**Main result.** The Pareto-optimal selection process identifies a trade-off, showing that while projected kernels achieve large geometric differences, the best detection performance (AUC 0.840) was found using a fidelity kernel selected via the unsupervised Normalized Pseudo Discrepancy (NPD) criterion.

**Method.** It employs a multi-objective optimization protocol to find the Pareto front by simultaneously maximizing the NPD (a label-free anomaly score) and maximizing the Geometric Difference (GD) between quantum and classical kernels.

**Model / system.** The methods are applied to real malware beaconing data, utilizing one-class Support Vector Machines (OCSVM) with both fidelity and projected quantum kernels. Testing was conducted on both simulators and the IQM 20-qubit Garnet processor.

**Key observables.** AUC (Area Under the Curve), Normalized Pseudo Discrepancy (NPD), and Geometric Difference ($g_{C	o Q}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The trade-off between high expressivity (large GD) and high task performance (high NPD) is central; hardware noise limits the achievable advantage.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the Pareto front guides the optimal model choice, and the NPD score serves as a reliable, label-free proxy for anomaly detection quality.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the unsupervised optimization pipeline searching the Pareto front, and hardware results compare performance metrics (AUC, F1) across different kernel types on the QPU.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the multi-objective framework (NPD + GD), details the application to malware data using fidelity and projected kernels, presents results comparing simulation vs. hardware performance, and concludes by characterizing the performance-separability trade-off.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum kernel methods are leading candidates for a practical quantum advantage in machine learning, but assessing that potential requires two quantities usually reported separately: how well a kernel performs on the task, and how far its geometry departs from the classical kernels available for the same problem. We introduce a fully unsupervised, multi-objective protocol that optimises simultaneously the normalised pseudo discrepancy (NPD), a label-free proxy for anomaly detection quality, and the geometric difference (GD) to a tuned classical reference kernel, selecting models from the resulting Pareto front. We apply it to malware beaconing detection in real network traffic, using a one-class support vector machine with fidelity and projected quantum kernels over four data encodings, on simulators and on IQM's 20-qubit Garnet processor. NPD-guided selection alone finds a fidelity kernel that beats the tuned classical baseline, but with a geometric difference too small to certify the gain as quantum. Projected kernels reach far larger geometric differences; the Pareto-selected one only marginally exceeds the baseline (AUC $0.782$ versus $0.765$, $g_{C\to Q}\approx 89>\sqrt{N}$ relative to that reference kernel), still below the NPD-selected fidelity kernel ($0.840$).

</details>

(a) Each fraudulent conversation is divided into six stages by the boundaries ⌊linspace(0, T, 7)⌋, shown here for T = 14; the labelled critical turn t∗is the first turn of the loss-event stage (turn 9), and detection lateness is measured relative to it. (b) Each stage s carries a fixed base level b(s) i ∈[0, 1] for the four bias channels. (c) The frame channel b(s) f ∈[−1, 1] is zero for the first four stages and non-zero only in the last two, with opposite signs, so every fraudulent conversation contains exactly one RZ ↔RY transition. By the sign convention of Eq. (6), ct,f &gt; 0 is loss framing and drives RZ while ct,f &lt; 0 is recovery framing...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Cognitive-Aware Quantum Machine Learning framework that combines a structured Parameterized Quantum Circuit (PQC) with Reinforcement Learning (RL) to detect financial fraud. The PQC encodes measurable cognitive biases and conversational structure into quantum feature vectors. The RL agent then uses these quantum features to learn the optimal timing for flagging suspicious interactions, treating the detection as an optimal stopping problem.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is in NLP/Fraud Detection, the core mechanism involves using quantum circuits to encode structured, non-linear information (cognitive biases, temporal order) into a feature space for a classical control problem (RL), which is a novel application of quantum feature mapping.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the detection of affinity and romance-investment fraud by modeling the cognitive biases present in manipulative conversations.

**Main result.** The proposed hybrid quantum-classical framework successfully trains an agent to optimally decide when to flag a conversation based on quantum-derived risk features.

**Method.** It employs a joint training scheme where a Parameterized Quantum Circuit (PQC) encodes cognitive biases, and a classical Reinforcement Learning (RL) agent optimizes the stopping decision.

**Model / system.** The model uses an $(n+2)$-qubit quantum register containing qubits for specific cognitive biases, a frame qubit for temporal order, and a narrative qubit for co-occurrence. The quantum circuit parameters are trained via semi-gradient Temporal-Difference (TD) learning.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the continuous, per-turn risk signals derived from the quantum register readout, forming the state vector $\mathbf{s}_t$ used by the RL agent.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the bias weights ($\Theta$), the RL action-value weights ($\mathbf{w}_a$), and regularization parameters like $\lambda$ controlling the trade-off between interpretability and adaptability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that the fraud detection problem can be modeled as an optimal stopping problem solvable by combining quantum feature mapping with RL. It also assumes the synthetic data generation process has no autoregressive structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the end-to-end pipeline from raw text to the flag/wait decision, alongside the detailed quantum encoder. Figure 5 details the synthetic data generation process across six distinct stages of a fraudulent conversation.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the architecture (PQC encoder + RL agent), the mathematical formalism for gradient calculation (parameter shift rule, semi-gradient TD learning), the training stabilization techniques (warm-up, anchoring), and the evaluation using synthetic, stage-structured data.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a hybrid quantum-classical framework that detects affinity and romance-investment fraud by modelling the cognitive biases in a manipulative conversation. In our proposed framework, cognitive biases central to this fraud class are carried by dedicated qubits in a structured parameterized quantum circuit, together with a frame qubit makes the encoding sensitive to the temporal order of manipulative reframing, and a narrative qubit that aggregates co-occurrence through a trainable entanglement layer. The circuit parameters are trained jointly with a classical reinforcement-learning agent that decides, turn by turn, whether to flag the conversation, modeled as an optimal stopping problem. We evaluate the model's performance on synthetic conversations that include hard negatives, legitimate but urgent, and legitimate but pushy sales conversations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs an explicit, large-scale linear system game that serves as a counterexample to Tsirelson's problem in its approximation form. By showing a measurable gap between the achievable quantum correlations ($	ext{Cqa}$) and the correlations from commuting measurements ($	ext{Cqc}$), it proves that these two sets of correlations are distinct. The methodology is highly technical, relying on advanced group theory and formal verification in Lean 4.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep, mathematically rigorous counterexample to a fundamental question in quantum information theory, using tools from geometric group theory, which is highly relevant for understanding the limits of quantum correlation models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses Tsirelson's problem in its approximation form: determining if quantum correlations achievable by commuting measurements ($	ext{Cqc}$) can always be approximated by correlations from finite-dimensional quantum measurements ($	ext{Cqa}$).

**Main result.** The authors construct an explicit binary linear system game that serves as a concrete counterexample, proving that $	ext{Cqa} 
eq 	ext{Cqc}$ by showing $\omega_{	ext{qa}}(G) < 1 = \omega_{	ext{qc}}(G)$.

**Method.** The proof combines advanced group theory (Kazhdan groups, ultraproducts) to construct a group obstruction, which is then mapped to a concrete linear system game structure to establish the gap in correlation values.

**Model / system.** The model is a binary linear system game $G(A, b)$ defined over $\mathbb{F}_2$, whose correlation values ($\omega_{	ext{qa}}, \omega_{	ext{qc}}$) are analyzed. The underlying mathematical machinery involves finitely presented groups and their representations.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the expected payoff values $\omega_{	ext{qa}}(G)$ and $\omega_{	ext{qc}}(G)$, with explicit bounds calculated for the gap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The game parameters are specified by a large matrix size ($m=1,417,152$, $n=1,889,684$), and the gap is quantified by specific rational numbers derived from the construction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies heavily on advanced results from group theory, including the properties of Kazhdan's property (T) and the use of tracial matrix ultraproducts for formalization in Lean 4.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference tables detailing the mapping between mathematical statements and their formal declarations in Lean, and tables summarizing the game-value arguments and matrix data.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first establishing the theoretical framework using group theory and ultraproducts to generate an obstruction, which is then translated into the concrete structure of a linear system game, culminating in the explicit calculation of the gap between the quantum and commuting correlation values.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct an explicit binary linear system game that separates \(C_{qa}\), the closure of the set of finite dimensional quantum correlations, from \(C_{qc}\), the set of commuting operator correlations. The game admits a perfect commuting operator strategy, while every correlation in \(C_{qa}\) has a success probability strictly less than 1. This provides a concrete counterexample to Tsirelson's problem in its approximation form. The defining system has \(1{,}417{,}152\) equations in \(1{,}889{,}684\) variables, with exactly three nonzero coefficients per equation and a single nonzero entry on the right hand side. We also compute the classical value exactly. The complete system is specified in Lean~4, and the separation and exact classical value are formalized using Mathlib.

</details>

### [Collusion-Secure Semi-Quantum Secret Sharing Scheme using a Quantum Third Party](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10176v1)

**Authors:** Santanu Majhi, Nirupam Basak  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.10176v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **2/5**

<img src="2610.10176_figures/2610.10176_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a robust Semi-Quantum Secret Sharing (SQSS) protocol designed to eliminate critical security vulnerabilities present in prior schemes. By incorporating decoy states and restricting operational authority, the authors achieve information-theoretic security against sophisticated collusion attacks. This advances the practical application of quantum key distribution concepts to secure multi-party computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the security foundations of quantum information protocols, which is central to quantum computing and quantum cryptography research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The security of Semi-Quantum Secret Sharing (SQSS) protocols is vulnerable to undetected collusion attacks by dishonest participants, even when assisted by a quantum third party.

**Main result.** The authors propose a novel SQSS protocol that achieves information-theoretic security against collusion attacks and is resilient against various external and internal eavesdropping strategies.

**Method.** The work involves designing a multi-phase quantum protocol incorporating decoy states, restricting operational authority, and using error rate checks (QBER) to detect malicious behavior.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum communication protocol involving classical participants with limited quantum capabilities (Z-basis measurements) assisted by a quantum third party, utilizing entangled pairs and decoy states.

**Key observables.** Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER), detection probability of attacks (e.g., DCNA), and the final reconstructed secret key.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit efficiency ($\eta$), the number of decoy particles ($m_X$), and the threshold for QBER.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The security proofs rely on the assumption that the protocol structure restricts the authority of the third party and that operational modes are announced only after all measurements are complete.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a comparison table (Table 1) summarizing the proposed protocol's advantages over existing schemes regarding security and efficiency, and a trace table (Table 2) detailing an attack simulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying vulnerabilities in existing SQSS protocols, then proposing an enhanced, multi-phase protocol, followed by rigorous security proofs against specific attacks (DCNA, EM, DSSA), and finally demonstrating the recovery of the secret under a universal Z-sweep attack.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The security of a secret sharing protocol is compromised if one or more dishonest participants can reconstruct the secret by deviating from the prescribed protocol, particularly when such malicious behavior remains undetected. Semi-Quantum Secret Sharing (SQSS) is a variant of secret sharing in which the participants possess only classical capabilities, such as preparing and measuring qubits in the computational ($Z$) basis, while a quantum-capable third party assists the dealer in generating and distributing quantum shares of a classical secret. The limited quantum capabilities of the participants in SQSS protocols may introduce vulnerabilities to collusion attacks, enabling an assisting quantum third party, in collaboration with a dishonest classical participant, to jointly recover the secret even without detection. In this work, we propose a novel SQSS protocol that eliminates this vulnerability and achieves information-theoretic security against collusion attacks. We further prove that the proposed protocol is secure against a broad class of external and internal attacks. Finally, a comparative analysis demonstrates that our construction advances existing SQSS protocols by simultaneously optimizing qubit efficiency, involves a classical dealer, and resilience against DCNA attacks using basic Bell states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Non-Orthogonal Amplitude Amplification (NOAA) for quantum processors combining continuous and discrete variables. It addresses fidelity limitations arising from non-orthogonal state overlaps and energy measurement errors. By proposing a High-Quality Spectral Projector (HQSP) filter, the authors show that their method outperforms standard techniques like RUS, particularly for preparing states with small initial amplitudes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles practical limitations (non-orthogonality, imperfect knowledge) in implementing quantum search algorithms on complex, hybrid quantum hardware architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses developing a Non-Orthogonal Amplitude Amplification (NOAA) method for hybrid Continuous Variable-Discrete Variable (CV-DV) quantum processors when the available projector is not perfectly selective for the target state.

**Main result.** The proposed protocol, utilizing a squeezed CV ancilla and a HQSP filter, achieves higher fidelity and efficiency than the Repeat-Until-Success (RUS) method for small initial target amplitudes, even in the presence of non-orthogonality.

**Method.** The authors generalize Amplitude Amplification (AA) to the NOAA framework and mitigate performance degradation using a High-Quality Spectral Projector (HQSP) filter to suppress errors from imperfect state discrimination and energy mismatch.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a hybrid CV-DV quantum processor, combining continuous variables (oscillators) with discrete variables (qubits). The protocol relies on spectral-transformation oracles acting on this coupled system.

**Key observables.** Target-state fidelity ($	ext{F}_{	ext{NOAA}}$), success probability ($P_s$), and the degree of fidelity degradation due to state non-orthogonality or energy mismatch.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial target-state amplitude ($\psi_0$), squeezing parameter ($r$), and the degree of energy mismatch ($\delta\alpha_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation is that the CV-assisted reflection is non-selective due to non-orthogonal CV states associated with different DV eigenstates, and the target eigenenergy may only be known approximately.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention analyzing oscillator wavepackets and filter responses, showing how the HQSP filter keeps the target wavepacket within the passband, which is crucial for maintaining high fidelity.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of non-orthogonality in CV-DV systems, derives the fidelity bounds, and then proposes the HQSP-assisted NOAA protocol. It compares this protocol's performance against the standard Repeat-Until-Success (RUS) method across different regimes of initial amplitude.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a non-orthogonal amplitude amplification (NOAA) method for hybrid continuous-variable- discrete-variable (CV-DV) quantum processors, where the available CV-assisted projector is not perfectly se- lective for the target state. We establish a bound on the achievable target-state fidelity, which is determined by the initial target-state amplitude and the selectivity of the accessible reflection operator. This bound limits the performance of NOAA in hybrid CV-DV systems. To mitigate this limitation, we propose using a squeezed CV ancilla to reduce the overlap between relevant CV states and a hybrid quantum signal processing (HQSP) filter to suppress errors arising from imperfect knowledge of the target eigenenergy. Numerical simulations show that, for small initial target-state amplitudes, our protocol achieves higher fidelity and efficiency than the repeat-until-success (RUS) method despite the presence of non-orthogonality. For large initial amplitudes, RUS is already efficient, and amplitude amplification provides little additional advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel, optimized phase-space characterization for qubit stabilizer states by adapting the discrete Wigner function to the specific state. It constructs a non-stabilizer witness based on this optimization, which allows for the rigorous, operational detection of non-stabilizer quantum states. The results restore a meaningful geometric understanding of stabilizer structure in the context of quantum information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, operational diagnostic tool (the witness) to distinguish stabilizer states from general quantum states using phase-space geometry, which is fundamental to understanding quantum resource theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the lack of a fundamental, state-independent phase-space description for qubit stabilizer states when using the discrete Wigner function.

**Main result.** By introducing a state-adapted optimization procedure, the authors prove that stabilizer states admit an exact delta-function Wigner representation, leading to a non-stabilizer witness that certifies non-stabilizerness.

**Method.** The methodology involves optimizing the discrete Wigner map based on measurement statistics across mutually unbiased bases (MUBs) and analyzing the resulting witness quantity under random Clifford transformations.

**Model / system.** The system under study is the $n$-qubit quantum system, analyzed using the discrete phase space defined by Pauli operators and displacement operators. The structure relies heavily on the properties of stabilizer states and complete MUB partitions.

**Key observables.** The non-stabilizer witness $W_{	ext{opt}}(0)$ (optimized Wigner function at the origin), and the covariance $	ext{Cov}_C(X_\lambda(C), X_\mu(C))$ of observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), the dimension ($d=2^n$), and the effective number of statistically distinct phase patterns ($N_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a complete stabilizer MUB partition and relies on asymptotic limits (large-qubit limit) for the stability of the witness.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the discrete phase space partition ($\pi$) for qubits, showing how different choices of cocycle phases lead to different arrangements.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the limitation of fixed Wigner maps, then introducing the state-adapted optimization, proving the delta-function representation for stabilizers, and finally developing advanced tools (covariance estimation, large deviation theory) to diagnose non-stabilizerness robustly.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The apparent absence of a stabilizer-speci?c phase-space description for qubit systems is not fundamental, but arises from ?xing a state-independent discrete Wigner map. Allowing the phase-space representation to adapt to the state through a simple optimization procedure, we prove that every qubit stabilizer state admits an exact delta-function discrete Wigner representation for any ?xed phase-space partition. This optimization can be implemented directly at the level of measurement statistics by selecting maximal probabilities across mutually unbiased bases forming the partition. This construction naturally leads to a non-stabilizer witness de?ned by the optimized value of the Wigner function at the origin. Stabilizer states saturate the universal lower bound of this witness for pure states, whereas a value strictly larger than the bound certi?es non-stabilizerness. Although the witness is not a ?nite-size resource monotone, it becomes sharply concentrated and e?ectively invariant under random Cli?ord transformations in the large-qubit limit, suggesting an asymptotically linear growth law for broad families of non-stabilizer states. Our results restore a meaningful geometric phase-space characterization of qubit stabilizer structure and provide an operational diagnostic of non-stabilizerness.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced experimental technique using a $^3	ext{He}$ neutron spin filter and a $	ext{2D}$ detector to measure polarization-dependent neutron scattering in three dimensions. By applying this method to $	ext{Mn}_3	ext{WO}_6$, the authors successfully analyzed out-of-plane incommensurate magnetic reflections. This development overcomes the limitations of older setups, enabling detailed studies of complex magnetic structures like multiferroics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant advancement in experimental techniques for probing magnetic order, providing a powerful tool to map magnetic structure in $3	ext{D}$ reciprocal space, which is crucial for understanding emergent phenomena in correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Studying complex, noncollinear, and incommensurate magnetic orders in condensed matter materials requires measurements that extend beyond the traditional horizontal scattering plane.

**Main result.** The developed technique successfully measured polarization-dependent intensities of out-of-plane incommensurate magnetic reflections in $	ext{Mn}_3	ext{WO}_6$, confirming the suitability of the method for complex magnetic structure analysis.

**Method.** The authors employed polarized neutron diffraction using an *ex-situ* $^3	ext{He}$ neutron spin filter ($	ext{}^3	ext{He-NSF}$) coupled with a two-dimensional ($	ext{2D}$) detector to analyze out-of-plane scattering polarization.

**Model / system.** The experiment was performed on the multiferroic single crystal $	ext{Mn}_3	ext{WO}_6$. The methodology involves complex data reduction procedures to convert $	ext{2D}$ intensity maps into full $3	ext{D}$ reciprocal space distributions while correcting for time-dependent neutron polarization and transmission.

**Key observables.** Polarization-dependent intensities of magnetic reflections, specifically the asymmetry value derived from non-spin-flip and spin-flip scattering intensities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is sensitive to the magnetic modulation wavevectors ($	ext{q-vectors}$) running in various directions, and the polarization analysis relies on measuring the asymmetry $	ext{Asymmetry} = (I^{++} - I^{+-}) / (I^{++} + I^{+-})$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include treating the polarizer transmission as constant and using time-dependent correction procedures based on the $	ext{}^3	ext{He}$ polarization measured via NMR.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between conventional in-plane measurements and the new out-of-plane capability; schematics detail the $	ext{}^3	ext{He-NSF}$/$	ext{2D}$ detector setup and the complex data reduction workflow.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of conventional setups, then details the novel experimental setup combining the $	ext{}^3	ext{He-NSF}$ and $	ext{2D}$ detector. It rigorously derives the time-dependent data reduction procedure, applies this method to $	ext{Mn}_3	ext{WO}_6$, and finally uses the measured polarization asymmetries to constrain the magnetic structure model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Polarized neutron scattering has been widely used for studying magnetic orders in condensed matter. A typical setup for this technique is a triple-axis spectrometer with a ferromagnetic single-crystal monochromator and analyzer, which is suited for point-by-point measurements within the horizontal scattering plane. However, this setup cannot fully meet the demands of recent studies on complex magnetic orders with emergent cross-correlated phenomena. For instance, multiferroics, magnetic skyrmions, and other topologically nontrivial magnetic orders tend to have incommensurate magnetic modulation vectors running along various directions in reciprocal space, which cannot be captured by measurements restricted to the horizontal scattering plane. To overcome these limitations, we have developed an experimental setup combining a $^3$He neutron spin filter ($^3$He-NSF) and a two-dimensional (2D) detector, which enables us to analyze the polarization of neutrons scattered away from the horizontal plane. We also establish a data reduction procedure to convert the 2D intensity maps into three-dimensional intensity distributions in reciprocal space, correcting for the effect of imperfect $^3$He polarization. This method is applied to single-crystal diffraction measurements on the multiferroic material Mn$_3$WO$_6$, which exhibits complex incommensurate magnetic orders. We have successfully observed polarization-dependent intensities of out-of-plane incommensurate magnetic reflections, demonstrating the effectiveness of the present method for analyzing complex magnetic structures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive framework to characterize a hierarchy of probability distributions arising from a specific mean-reverting Stochastic Differential Equation. It introduces two methods—transforming the SDE versus transforming the stationary solutions—and proves that these two approaches yield different generalized distributions, highlighting a fundamental non-commutativity in the mathematical procedure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on classical stochastic processes, the mathematical machinery of deriving complex, multi-parameter distributions from underlying dynamics (SDEs) is highly relevant to modeling non-equilibrium steady states in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the stationary distributions generated by a mean-reverting Stochastic Differential Equation (SDE) and explores how these distributions can be generalized.

**Main result.** The authors show that generalizing the hierarchy via power transformations yields two distinct, non-commuting routes (dynamical vs. distributional), meaning the generalized hierarchy is not unique.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving stationary probability density functions (PDFs) from a master SDE and comparing results obtained by transforming the SDE versus transforming the resulting stationary PDFs.

**Model / system.** The model is a mean-reverting SDE for a positively defined variable, which generates a hierarchy of distributions related to the Generalized Beta family. The analysis involves parameter reductions ($\kappa=0, \kappa_1=0, \kappa_2=0$) to map to known distributions.

**Key observables.** Probability density functions (PDFs) and cumulative distribution functions (CDFs) for the modified-Beta (mB) and generalized mGB families.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mean-reversion rate ($\gamma$), mean-reversion scale ($	heta$), and stochastic coefficients ($\kappa, \kappa_1, \kappa_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is the non-commutativity of nonlinear transformation and stationary-state reduction, implying the generalized hierarchy is route-dependent.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the flow of the hierarchy in the Beta sector and the generalized sector via the two distinct transformation routes, while tables provide the explicit mathematical forms (PDFs/CDFs) for both generalized families.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the base hierarchy from the master SDE, then details two procedures for generalization (dynamical vs. distributional), and finally provides extensive mathematical tables comparing the resulting functional forms and parameters for both generalized families.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a mean-reverting stochastic differential equation with a three-component stochastic term and show that it generates a hierarchy of steady-state (stationary) distributions. At the top level, the hierarchy is described by a modified-Beta distribution, while one- and two-parameter reductions produce compact-support, power-law-tailed, and exponential-type limiting families within a single stochastic framework.   We then construct two generalized extensions of this hierarchy. In the first, the power transformation is applied directly at the level of the stochastic differential equation; in the second, the same transformation is applied only after the stationary modified-Beta hierarchy has been obtained. While these two procedures agree on important lower branches and limiting cases they generally differ at the top level. The generalized hierarchy is therefore not unique: nonlinear transformation and stationary-state reduction do not commute.   For both routes, we derive the probability density and cumulative distribution functions, express their parameters in terms of the underlying stochastic dynamics, clarify the relations among their limiting cases, and compare the resulting families with the traditional Generalized Beta framework.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how physical reservoir computing can be optimized for filtering and predicting complex, noisy signals by precisely matching the timescales of the input noise, the hardware memory, and the required forecast window. Using a niobium oxide device, the authors demonstrate that understanding these timescale relationships allows for the development of new physical architectures capable of analyzing stochastic components across different temporal scales.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on signal processing, the use of a physical, non-linear, time-dependent system (the reservoir) to process complex, multi-timescale data echoes concepts in open quantum systems where environmental coupling dictates observable dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of filtering and forecasting correlated noise signals by establishing a relationship between the signal, noise, and hardware timescales.

**Main result.** Timescale matching is crucial for physical reservoir computing, determining whether the noise is averaged out or if its temporal structure is sufficient for accurate algorithmic forecasting.

**Method.** The authors use a physical reservoir computing setup, testing reconstruction and prediction tasks on synthetic colored noise and real cryptocurrency volatility data.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a nanoporous niobium oxide layer acting as a reservoir. The input signal is modeled as a sum of deterministic harmonics and colored noise, $\xi(t)$.

**Key observables.** Normalized Root Mean Square Error (NRMSE), Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR), Reservoir Memory Horizon, and Forecasting Regime Index.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise correlation time ($	au$), reservoir memory, and forecast horizon are the key interacting timescales.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The performance of the physical reservoir is hypothesized to be governed by the matching of the three relevant timescales.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the reservoir signal processing overview, SNR vs. noise level, and performance metrics (NRMSE) for reconstruction and prediction across varying noise correlation times.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the problem (timescale matching) to detailing the physical setup and signal model, presenting results on reconstruction and prediction using synthetic and real-world data, and concluding with design principles based on operating regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Physical reservoir computing exploits the nonlinear dynamics of physical systems to process time-dependent data with greater energy efficiency than conventional machine learning approaches. However, physical reservoirs have fixed intrinsic response timescales, whereas real-world signals combine deterministic and stochastic components across multiple timescales. Here we show, using a nanoporous niobium oxide reservoir, synthetic noisy signals and cryptocurrency-price volatility, that the relationship among noise correlation time, reservoir memory and forecast horizon determines whether correlated noise is filtered or predicted. Noise varying faster than the relevant reservoir memory and forecast horizon is averaged by the reservoir, whereas the temporal structure of slower-varying noise is sufficient for algorithmic forecasting. We introduce the reservoir memory horizon and forecasting regime index to distinguish these operating regimes. These contributions demonstrate that timescale matching can guide the encoding of input time series and development of physical reservoir architectures that filter, analyse and predict stochastic signal components across distinct temporal scales.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the phase diagram of a semiflexible magnetic polymer, modeling systems like DNA complexes. By combining Monte Carlo simulations and variational theory, the authors map out how chain stiffness and magnetic interactions drive multiple first-order transitions between coil, globule, and crystal states. The results highlight the complex interplay between conformational entropy and magnetic ordering in soft matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The coupling between magnetic degrees of freedom and polymer conformation provides a rich model for studying coupled order parameters and phase transitions, relevant to many-body systems in condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To characterize the zero-field phase diagram of a semiflexible magnetic polymer as a function of bending stiffness and temperature.

**Main result.** The phase diagram exhibits multiple first-order transitions, including coil-to-globule and globule-to-crystal transitions, demonstrating how stiffness and magnetic coupling reorganize the polymer's conformational landscape.

**Method.** The study employs Monte Carlo simulations on an FCC lattice, complemented by a variational free-energy upper-bound approach to analyze the thermodynamic limits.

**Model / system.** The model is a semiflexible magnetic polymer chain where each monomer has a two-state spin, coupling conformational energy (bending penalty $\kappa$) with magnetic interactions ($J$). It serves as a minimal representation of nucleoprotein complexes.

**Key observables.** Contact Density ($ho_c$), Magnetization ($\langle|m|angle$), Nematic Order Parameter ($S$), and the overall phase diagram structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bending stiffness ($\kappa$), Temperature ($T$), and the ratio $K = \kappa/J$. The zero-field limit ($h=0$) is the primary focus.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the zero-field limit ($h=0$) and uses variational upper bounds for the crystalline phase, which assumes an ordered, maximally compact cylindrical bundle structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the polymer, the evolution of ground state structure with stiffness, and the construction of the crystalline upper bound using a cylindrical bundle.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian, first characterizing the zero-field phase diagram using simulations, then deriving analytical upper bounds for the crystalline phase, and finally mapping out the sequence of transitions based on the competition between bending costs and magnetic/contact energies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a semiflexible magnetic polymer in which each monomer carries a two-state spin, giving rise to coupled conformational, stiffness, and magnetic degrees of freedom. The model provides a minimal representation of nucleoprotein complexes in vivo, in which DNA bending rigidity and bridging proteins must be simultaneously accommodated. We employ Monte Carlo simulations on a face-centered-cubic (FCC) lattice, complemented by a variational free-energy upper-bound approach, to characterize the zero-field phase diagram as a function of bending stiffness and temperature. At low stiffness, we identify a first-order transition combining coil-to-globule collapse with a ferromagnetic-to-paramagnetic transition. At lower temperatures, an additional first-order transition separates the disordered globule from an ordered crystal characterized by nematic-like orientational order of the bond tangents. At high stiffness, the globule disappears, resulting in a direct first-order coil-to-crystal transition. These results demonstrate how chain stiffness and magnetic degrees of freedom couple to polymer conformation and reorganize the zero-field phase diagram. Our model connects the two limiting cases of flexible magnetic polymers and semiflexible homopolymers, the latter corresponding to the strong-field limit in which all spins are aligned.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a novel, unbiased theoretical method to predict magnetic order by analyzing the leading eigenmodes of the magnetization response within the paramagnetic state. By applying this technique to materials like $	ext{KV}_2	ext{Se}_2	ext{O}$ and $	ext{NiI}_2$, the authors successfully reproduce complex magnetic structures without needing to assume the final ordered state beforehand.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the use of linear response theory to probe instabilities from a non-interacting (paramagnetic) reference state is a powerful, generalizable technique for identifying emergent collective behavior, which has parallels in open quantum system dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Traditional methods for predicting magnetic order are limited to predefined spin configurations and become computationally intractable for large systems.

**Main result.** The magnetic order can be predicted unbiasedly by analyzing the leading eigenmodes of the self-consistent magnetization response calculated from the paramagnetic state.

**Method.** The method involves solving a linearized eigenvalue problem derived from the self-consistent magnetization response function, incorporating spin-orbit coupling.

**Model / system.** The study applies this method to various magnetic materials, including $	ext{KV}_2	ext{Se}_2	ext{O}$, $	ext{Cu}_2	ext{OSeO}_3$, and monolayer $	ext{NiI}_2$, which are characterized by complex magnetic interactions.

**Key observables.** The largest eigenvalue ($\lambda_{\mathbf{q}}$) and its corresponding eigenvector ($oldsymbol{\phi}_{\mathbf{q}}$) determine the magnetic instability, wavevector ($\mathbf{q}$), and pattern of magnetic moments.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC), wavevector ($\mathbf{q}$), and the instability threshold ($\lambda_{\mathbf{q}} = 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the leading eigenmodes of the paramagnetic state response are sufficient to capture the true magnetic ground state, overcoming the limitations of pre-selected configurations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the leading response eigenvectors for $	ext{KV}_2	ext{Se}_2	ext{O}$ for both C-type and G-type stacking, visualizing the magnetization components.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of prior methods, details the theoretical framework using linear response theory on the paramagnetic state, and then applies this framework to calculate and distinguish between known magnetic orders in several distinct materials.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The magnetic order of a material is usually predicted by comparing the energies of trial spin configurations, a strategy that cannot reach orders outside the chosen set and rapidly becomes intractable as the number of magnetic sites grows. We show that the order can instead be identified directly from the paramagnetic state: the leading eigenmodes of its self-consistent magnetization response, obtained with spin--orbit coupling at arbitrary wavevector within the primitive cell, determine both the ordering wavevector and the pattern of moments, with no candidate configurations. With no prior assumption about the order, the method correctly reproduces the G-type stacking with out-of-plane moments in the altermagnet candidate KV$_2$Se$_2$O, the $3{:}1$ ferrimagnetic tetrahedra of Cu$_2$OSeO$_3$, and the incommensurate spiral instability of monolayer NiI$_2$. Our results establish the response of the paramagnetic state as a predictive and unbiased route to assessing magnetic order, enabling systematic searches for complex magnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes percolation in a multiplex network composed of two interdependent 1D long-range lattices. It determines the critical coupling strength required for the existence of a mutual giant component. A key finding is that the transition's nature (first-order vs. continuous) is fundamentally controlled by the decay exponent $\sigma$ of the long-range interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical percolation, the concept of network robustness and phase transitions in coupled systems shares mathematical parallels with critical phenomena studied in quantum many-body systems, particularly concerning connectivity and failure modes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence and critical conditions for a mutual giant component in a multiplex network formed by two interdependent one-dimensional long-range networks.

**Main result.** The multiplex network is significantly less stable than a single layer, and the order of the percolation transition depends critically on the decay exponent $\sigma$, changing from first-order to continuous as $\sigma$ increases.

**Method.** The authors employ theoretical analysis, deriving rigorous lower bounds, and utilizing a tree approximation to estimate the critical percolation threshold ($C_c$).

**Model / system.** The model is a multiplex network of two layers on $\mathbb{Z}$, where intra-layer links decay algebraically as $p_r \propto r^{-(1+\sigma)}$. Interdependence is established via links connecting corresponding nodes in both layers.

**Key observables.** Mutual Giant Component (MGC) existence, the critical thresholds ($C_{	ext{single}}^c$ and $C_{	ext{mul}}^c$), and the order of the phase transition (first-order vs. continuous).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The decay exponent $\sigma$ ($0 < \sigma < 1$) and the coupling strength parameter $C$ are crucial. The transition order changes around $\sigma \approx 0.35$ to $0.4$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a tree approximation, which is noted to be highly accurate (within 0.6%) for $\sigma \le 0.5$ but fails to capture the transition order for larger $\sigma$.

**Figures summary.** The figures illustrate the schematic of the two-layer network, comparing the critical thresholds derived from the single-layer and multiplex tree approximations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the multiplex model, deriving theoretical bounds for the critical threshold, applying a tree approximation to estimate the threshold, and finally analyzing how the transition order changes as a function of the decay exponent $\sigma$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the emergence of a mutual giant component in a multiplex network comprised of two one-dimensional long-range networks on a lattice. The probability that two nodes on one lattice, with a distance $r$ between them, are connected with an edge falls algebraically with distance as $Cr^{-(1+σ)}$, where $0 < σ< 1$ and $0 < C \leq 1$. A mutual giant component exists if $C > C_c^{\mathrm{mul}}$, where $C_c^{\mathrm{mul}}(σ)$ is a critical value of $C$ that is dependent on $σ$. We find rigorous lower bounds on $C_c^{\mathrm{mul}}$, and a tree approximation whose estimate of $C_c^{\mathrm{mul}}$ agrees with simulations within 0.6% for $σ\leq 0.5$. The multiplex network is significantly less stable than the single network case, as is evident by the larger value of $C_c^{\mathrm{mul}}$, which is up to 2.4 times the single-layer value. Our simulations indicate that the order of the transition depends on $σ$. For $σ\leq 0.35$ the mutual giant component collapses abruptly, in a first-order phase transition, while for $σ\geq 0.4$ the transition is continuous. When the dependency links connect random pairs of nodes, the transition is first order for all $σ$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work pioneers the use of AI-accelerated simulations to predict the atomic structure of amorphous polymers like pDXL by simulating their synthesis. By analyzing the resulting ensemble microstructures, the authors successfully deconvolute the measured diffraction signal into distinct contributions from the monomer and the growing polymer chain. This provides a powerful, first-principles method for validating polymer structure against experimental scattering data.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of ML-force fields trained on quantum mechanical data (DFT) to simulate complex, disordered polymer growth processes is highly relevant to ML-physics, providing a predictive tool for materials structure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is to determine the atomistic microstructure of amorphous Poly(1,3-dioxolane) (pDXL) and predict its experimentally measurable scattering signals.

**Main result.** The primary diffraction peak is revealed to be a sum of two counteracting modes: a decaying monomer contribution ($\approx 1.5 	ext{ Å}^{-1}$) and a growing polymer contribution ($\approx 1.6 	ext{ Å}^{-1}$).

**Method.** The authors employed an AI-accelerated *ab initio* bottom-up polymer structure prediction (AI2-BPSP) framework to simulate the living cationic ring-opening polymerization.

**Model / system.** The system is amorphous pDXL undergoing polymerization. The simulation uses a Machine-Learning Force Field trained on DFT data to model bond breaking and formation during the polymerization process.

**Key observables.** X-ray and neutron interference functions ($F_X(q)$, $F_N(q)$), partial radial distribution functions ($g_{\alphaeta}(r)$), and partial structure factors ($S_{\alphaeta}(q)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chain length ($n$, up to 100-mer), temperature (300 K), and pressure (1 bar).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The MLFF is assumed transferable from short oligomers to the macromolecular limit, and the ensemble is not assumed to be at true equilibrium due to finite simulation time.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the polymerization steps, partial radial distribution functions at various chain lengths, statistical convergence of structural metrics, and the predicted interference functions showing the separation of monomer and polymer contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the AI2-BPSP workflow, simulating polymerization to generate ensembles of microstructures. It then analyzes the resulting structures by calculating partial structure factors and predicting the total X-ray and neutron scattering functions, emphasizing the separation of contributions from the monomer and the growing polymer backbone.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Poly(1,3-dioxolane) (pDXL) is a chemically recyclable polyether that can be synthesized by living cationic ring-opening polymerization with precise control of chain length into the ultra-high-molecular-weight (UHMW) regime, where it acquires enhanced mechanical properties. Like most polymers, however, it has amorphous condensed-phase structure, and diffraction measurements alone cannot resolve the atomistic microstructure that underlies them. In this work, we predict the ensemble of the pDXL microstructures by applying a recently developed AI-accelerated ab initio bottom-up polymer structure prediction (AI$^{2}$-BPSP) framework that simulates the living polymerization of pDXL under experimental synthetic conditions with machine-learning force fields trained within van der Waals-corrected hybrid density functional theory. Partitioning the predicted X-ray and neutron diffraction signal between the growing chain and the surrounding monomer resolves its dominant feature into two counteracting modes: a monomer contribution near $q \approx 1.5$ Å$^{-1}$ that decays as monomer is consumed, and a polymer contribution near $q \approx 1.6$ Å$^{-1}$ that is absent in oligomers and grows with chain length. This work paves the way to direct accuracy validation of these statistically significant, first-principles, and chain-length resolved diffraction predictions against experiments.

</details>
