**Summary.** This paper examines the stability of quantum systems containing 'ghost' states, which arise from higher-derivative or gravity-related field theories. Using a simplified coupled oscillator model, the authors demonstrate that the initial state does not decay, suggesting a mechanism for stabilizing Hamiltonians that are mathematically unbounded from below. This offers theoretical evidence for the stability of such exotic quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that a system with an unbounded Hamiltonian can maintain a stable, oscillating state challenges standard expectations of vacuum instability in quantum field theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the physical stability of quantum systems that inherently possess negative kinetic energy, often termed 'ghost' states, which arise in advanced QFT models.

**Main result.** Contrary to expectations based on the unbounded nature of the Hamiltonian, the 'would-be ground state' was shown to remain dynamically stable over long times, exhibiting oscillations rather than decaying.

**Method.** The authors analyzed the time evolution of a simplified, coupled quantum mechanical model using numerical methods and compared quantum dynamics with classical trajectories.

**Model / system.** The model consists of two coupled quantum harmonic oscillators, where one oscillator is treated as a 'ghost' system (possessing a negative kinetic energy term in the Hamiltonian).

**Key observables.** Survival probability of the would-be ground state, $P_v(t)$; energy exchange between the normal and ghost subsystems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constant ($\lambda$); oscillator frequencies ($\omega_1, \omega_2$); time ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the time evolution of the initial, decoupled 'would-be ground state' and is limited by numerical computation time.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate classical trajectories in the energy plane, the survival probability $P(t)$ showing oscillatory behavior, and probability current analysis in the $x-y$ plane.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the toy model, analyzing the decoupled spectrum (including density of states calculations), and then analyzing the time evolution of the coupled system, comparing quantum results to classical expectations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many Quantum Field Theory models, either designed to improve the ultraviolet behavior via higher derivative terms or aimed at providing unified descriptions of Gravity, inevitably introduce negative-energy 'ghosts' states. These states raise fundamental questions regarding the stability of the theory. In this study, we investigate the time evolution of simplified quantum mechanical models consisting of two coupled normal and ghost subsystems. Although the full spectrum of these models is unbounded from below and, therefore, lacks a true ground state, we focus the time evolution of a 'would-be ground state': a zero-energy eigenstate of the decoupled system. By analyzing its metastability under interactions, we gain insights into the dynamics of ghost-like states. Contrary to the standard paradigm, our results demonstrate that, across a broad range of couplings, the would-be ground state does not decay and remains dynamically stable over long times. This provides evidence towards resolving the stability problems of Hamiltonians that are unbounded from below. We also discuss the connections with the corresponding classical and semiclassical dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a CV-QKD protocol using noisy coherent states and a displacement receiver, demonstrating its potential to outperform standard homodyne detection. The authors provide a full security analysis against collective attacks and derive a method to estimate channel parameters using realistic photon-number-resolving detection. This establishes the displacement receiver as a promising, robust alternative for quantum key distribution implementations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, experimentally relevant alternative to standard homodyne detection for CV-QKD, offering a tunable receiver strategy that improves performance in realistic noisy environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops and analyzes a Continuous-Variable Quantum Key Distribution (CV-QKD) protocol utilizing a binary alphabet of noisy coherent states and displacement receivers.

**Main result.** The displacement receiver can outperform conventional homodyne detection in experimentally relevant regimes, and the protocol remains secure even with noisy state preparation and imperfect detection.

**Method.** The security analysis is performed using the Devetak-Winter formula against entangling-cloner attacks, while parameter estimation is achieved by analyzing ratios of conditional probabilities derived from PNR measurements.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum communication over a thermal-loss channel, using noisy coherent states (displaced thermal states) as the encoding basis and a displacement receiver for measurement.

**Key observables.** Secret key rate, discrimination error probability, channel transmittance ($\sqrt{\eta}$), and mutual information $I(A; B)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transmissivity ($\eta$), noise parameters ($ar{n}_A, ar{n}_E$), detector efficiency ($\gamma$), and displacement parameter ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The security analysis assumes a Gaussian channel model for Eve's information and that Alice's devices are trusted; reliable parameter estimation requires Photon-Number-Resolving (PNR) detection.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the prepare and measure scheme under an entangling-cloner collective attack, and Table I provides reference values for Eve's thermal mode mean photon number.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and the advantage of the displacement receiver for state discrimination. It then details the security analysis using the Devetak-Winter formula for key rate calculation. Finally, it develops methods for parameter estimation, deriving $\sqrt{\eta}$ by analyzing ratios of conditional probabilities from PNR measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Near-optimal binary coherent state discrimination can be achieved by performing a displacement operation followed by photon detection. This displacement serves as a control parameter that can be tuned to optimize a chosen figure of merit, which makes such receivers attractive beyond state discrimination. Here, we turn this discrimination strategy into a complete continuous-variable quantum key distribution (CV-QKD) protocol based on a binary alphabet of noisy coherent states (displaced thermal states) and displacement receivers. We develop a strategy for estimating the channel parameters with the same displacement receiver, since standard quadrature-based methods do not apply. While a key map can be obtained with on/off detection, we demonstrate that reliable parameter estimation requires photon-number-resolving detection with sufficient resolution. Finite detector resolution underestimates the observed noise and constitutes a potential security loophole, which we quantify and show to be mitigable with modest resolution. We analyze the protocol's security against entangling-cloner collective attacks in the asymptotic regime, for both direct and reverse reconciliation, and show that it can generate secret keys even under noisy state preparation and imperfect detection. Interestingly, we find that the displacement that minimizes the discrimination error probability also performs well for key generation, thus providing a useful and analytically tractable tuning strategy for the CV-QKD receiver. We evaluate the protocol's performance over a range of transmittances and noise parameters, showing that the displacement receiver can outperform conventional homodyne detection in experimentally relevant regimes. Together, our results point to displacement receivers as a promising alternative for CV-QKD implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced Ring-DMRG algorithm to efficiently simulate periodic quantum systems using Tensor Ring decompositions. It overcomes the inherent difficulties of optimizing TRs by introducing robust two-site truncation schemes and advanced solvers. The work demonstrates that this method maintains the favorable scaling of DMRG while significantly reducing the required computational resources for critical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of robust, scalable tensor network algorithms like Ring-DMRG is crucial for simulating strongly correlated materials where periodic boundary conditions are natural, allowing access to regimes previously inaccessible due to computational cost.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently optimizing Tensor Ring (TR) decompositions for periodic quantum many-body systems, which are difficult due to the closed geometry leading to ill-conditioned environments.

**Main result.** The proposed Ring-DMRG algorithm retains the favorable cubic scaling ($\mathcal{O}(\chi^3)$) with bond dimension of standard DMRG while achieving this at a substantially smaller bond dimension compared to other methods.

**Method.** It extends DMRG to a two-site Ring-DMRG algorithm, improving robustness with gauge transformations and using generalized Davidson solvers for the local variational problem.

**Model / system.** The methods are applied to periodic quantum many-body systems, using the spin-1/2 Heisenberg chain as a primary example to benchmark performance.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy error, bond dimension ($\chi$), and environment rank ($p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), environment rank ($p$), system size ($N$), and the ratio $J_2/J_1$ for the Heisenberg chain.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the low-rank structure of the environment allows for efficient, systematic approximation, generalizing belief propagation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares TT (open) vs. TR (periodic) representations. Figure 2 shows energy convergence for the Heisenberg chain, illustrating the scaling advantage of TRs over TTs ($\chi_t \propto \chi_{r}^{1.5}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the TR representation for periodic systems, details the Ring-DMRG methodology (including two-site truncation and alternating optimization), and analyzes its computational scaling and convergence properties against established methods like TT.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tensor-ring (TR) decompositions provide a natural representation of periodic systems but are difficult to optimize because the closed geometry prevents a global canonical form and leads to costly, ill conditioned environments. Existing periodic DMRG methods alleviate this difficulty by compressing long environments to a low-rank representation, reducing local operations to $\mathcal{O}(pχ^3)$, where $p$ is the retained environment rank. Here, we extend this approach to an efficient two-site Ring-DMRG algorithm and improve its numerical robustness through appropriate gauge transformations and a generalized Davidson solver. A central challenge in the two-site formulation is the truncation step, which must account for the surrounding environment. When this environment is sufficiently separable, a suitable change of frame reduces the truncation to an ordinary SVD. When it is not separable, we instead use an alternating optimization that retains the full environment.   The computational advantage of tensor rings over tensor trains depends on the scaling of the required environment rank $p$ with bond dimension and system size. For critical systems, the divergent correlation length makes long-range observables remain sensitive to the periodic geometry as the thermodynamic limit is approached. In this regime, the environment rank required to reach a fixed accuracy in the observable does not grow with the system size. Ring-DMRG therefore retains the same cubic scaling with bond dimension as standard DMRG, but at a substantially smaller bond dimension. The low-rank environment construction also provides a systematic generalization of belief propagation (BP), with standard BP recovered in the rank-one limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes percolation on complex, self-similar hierarchical lattices defined by EIGS. It rigorously proves that the critical behavior (universality class) cannot be determined solely by geometric dimensions or by requiring commensurability of underlying scales. The work significantly advances the mathematical understanding of critical phenomena by showing the limitations of dimension-based classification.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical percolation, the rigorous mathematical framework developed for classifying universality classes based on geometric invariants (dimensions) and the explicit disproof of simple dimensional criteria provides powerful, generalizable mathematical tools applicable to critical phenomena in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To rigorously investigate the universality classes of Bernoulli percolation on complex, self-similar hierarchical lattices generated by Edge Iterated Graph Systems (EIGS).

**Main result.** The study proves that universality cannot be determined by any finite set of substitution-multiplicative dimensions, and furthermore, it disproves the conjecture that critical-exponent universality requires commensurate renormalization scales.

**Method.** The authors use renormalization group techniques, analyzing various critical exponents ($eta, 
u, \delta, \eta_{av}$) and relating them to geometric dimensions ($\dim_H, \dim_M, \dim_P$) derived from the EIGS structure.

**Model / system.** The model is Bernoulli bond percolation on hierarchical lattices generated by deterministic or random Edge Iterated Graph Systems (EIGS). These lattices are characterized by specific rules graphs and associated geometric dimensions.

**Key observables.** Critical exponents ($eta, 
u, \delta, \eta_{av}$), ambient dimension ($\dim_H(\Xi)$), pivotal dimension ($\dim_P(C)$), and mass dimension ($\dim_M(C)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The percolation probability $p$ near the critical point $p_c$, and the structure of the underlying EIGS rule graph $R$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of specific geometric dimensions derived from the EIGS structure and the application of theorems related to number theory (e.g., six exponentials theorem).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the Diamond Hierarchical Lattice (DHL) and compare the reliability functions and critical exponents for two different lattices ('Tie' and 'Gem') to demonstrate shared universality despite structural differences.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the EIGS framework, establishing relationships between critical exponents and geometric dimensions, proving the failure of dimension-based universality, and finally constructing counterexamples to the requirement of commensurate renormalization scales.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study Bernoulli percolation and its universality classes on hierarchical lattices generated by edge iterated graph systems (EIGS). By examining eight critical exponents, we prove that no finite family of substitution-multiplicative dimensions completely determines universality, providing a rigorous proof of the failure of dimension-based universality discussed by Hauser and Saxena [Phys. Rev. B 34 (1986), 8193-8195]. Although the six exponentials theorem provides number-theoretic criteria for scale commensurability, we further disprove the conjecture that critical-exponent universality requires commensurate discrete renormalisation scales.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously characterizes when quantum walks on strongly regular graphs achieve Uniform Average Mixing (UAM). It uses moment theory and spectral analysis to reduce the problem to algebraic constraints on the graph's eigenvalues. The work provides a complete classification of such graphs, which is crucial for understanding quantum mixing dynamics on highly symmetric structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between quantum walks, spectral graph theory, and moment problems provides deep insights into the mixing properties of quantum systems on structured lattices, relevant for understanding quantum transport.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the necessary and sufficient conditions for a continuous-time quantum walk on a strongly regular graph (SRG) to exhibit Uniform Average Mixing (UAM) when observed at a random time.

**Main result.** The classification identifies specific infinite families of SRG parameters that admit UAM, and it provides explicit criteria for when the mixing can be realized by at most two observation times.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on moment theory, spectral analysis of the graph's adjacency matrix, and reducing the UAM condition to solving a set of affine moment constraints.

**Model / system.** The model is a continuous-time quantum walk evolving on a connected, non-complete strongly regular graph (SRG). The dynamics are governed by the evolution operator $U(t) = e^{itA}$, where $A$ is the adjacency matrix.

**Key observables.** The averaged transition probability matrix $\int_0^\infty M(t) d\mu(t)$, which must equal $rac{1}{n}J$ for UAM.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph parameters $(n, k, \lambda, \mu)$ defining the SRG, and the eigenvalues of the adjacency matrix.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The graph $G$ is assumed to be connected and not complete. The analysis often assumes the existence of a bounded time density for the observation law $\mu$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the provided notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining UAM and deriving moment constraints, to proving that the average can be realized by at most two observation times, and finally culminates in a comprehensive classification of all admitting SRGs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study continuous-time quantum walks on connected strongly regular graphs that are not complete, observed at a random time drawn from a freely chosen probability law. Uniform average mixing (UAM) asks for a law under which every averaged transition probability equals $1/n$, where $n$ is the number of vertices. On a strongly regular graph this is equivalent to two affine constraints on three cosine moments. We construct a bounded, compactly supported time density for every strongly regular graph with nonintegral eigenvalues. For integral spectra we give an exact finite Toeplitz criterion and its Hankel form. Every averaged mixing matrix of such a graph is realized by at most two observation times. Three elementary inequalities on the moment line, which also give a short proof of Chan's classification of complex Hadamard matrices in the Bose-Mesner algebra, lead to a determination of all strongly regular graphs that admit UAM. Apart from the conference graphs of nonsquare order and the graphs with instantaneous uniform mixing, these are the members of two infinite families of parameter sets and their complements, and for them we give explicit laws with two observation times. The Petersen graph and its complement are the smallest members. A strongly regular graph with UAM admits a bounded time density exactly when it has no instantaneous uniform mixing. We also correct the classification of instantaneous uniform mixing on strongly regular graphs by Godsil, Mullin and Roy. Its sign condition excludes the halved $5$-cube, which mixes uniformly at time $π/4$. With the order $4θ^2$ read literally, its parity condition also excludes the Clebsch graph and includes the parameters $(36,14,4,6)$, for which no time law gives uniform average mixing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QuSema, an autonomous testing agent designed to find 'silent bugs' in quantum computing libraries. It overcomes the limitations of traditional testing by using quantum semantics and documentation as a source-level oracle. By reasoning about intended quantum behavior, QuSema can uncover subtle, non-crashing defects critical for reliable quantum algorithm development.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on software, the underlying challenge of verifying complex, non-linear, and high-dimensional physical simulations (like those in quantum computing) mirrors the need for robust verification techniques in AMO physics simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is detecting 'silent bugs' in quantum libraries, where incorrect results are produced without triggering explicit runtime failures.

**Main result.** The proposed agent, QuSema, successfully discovered 40 previously unknown bugs, including 30 silent bugs, surpassing existing testing methods on a benchmark.

**Method.** QuSema is an autonomous agent that uses quantum semantics and API documentation as a source-level semantic oracle to reason about intended behavior and generate executable tests.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum computing libraries like Qiskit and PennyLane, which operate on qubits and quantum states manipulated by unitary operators.

**Key observables.** Semantic deviations, incorrect output values from valid inputs, and discrepancies between documented API specifications and actual code execution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity of quantum circuits, gate parameters (e.g., angles $	heta, \varphi$), and the structure of quantum operations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the intended behavior of the quantum library can be inferred and constrained using quantum semantics and documentation, allowing for testing beyond simple failure modes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the limitations of existing black-box testing methods, such as the inability to establish expected output relations or trigger bugs under specific semantic conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of existing failure-based and comparison-based testing for quantum libraries. It then introduces QuSema, detailing its agentic loop that uses quantum semantics to derive source-level oracles, followed by empirical results on bug detection.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum libraries are now critical infrastructure for quantum algorithm development, yet their correctness remains difficult to test. Existing testing techniques mainly rely on failure-based or comparison-based oracles, exposing bugs only when executions fail, violate runtime checks, or disagree with another implementation. Their applicability is limited when suitable execution-based oracles are unavailable, leaving some silent bugs undetected. Such missed bugs can produce incorrect results that propagate into experimental conclusions, simulation studies, and algorithmic designs. Here we present QuSema, an autonomous testing agent for finding silent bugs in quantum libraries. QuSema uses constraints from quantum semantics and documentation as a source-level semantic oracle to assess whether implementation logic can produce invalid outputs from valid inputs. It operates through an agentic loop that repeatedly inspects library API documentation and source code, reasons about the intended behavior of quantum operations, identifies potential semantic deviations, and validates them by generating executable tests through library APIs. Guided by quantum-domain reasoning, QuSema turns high-level behavioral mismatches into concrete, user-triggerable bug reports, enabling it to uncover non-crash defects. We implement QuSema for Qiskit and PennyLane. On a benchmark of 20 historical silent bugs, QuSema achieves higher mean bug relocation counts than Claude Code and Codex, with the DeepSeek configuration costing less than Claude Code. QuSema also discovers 40 previously unknown bugs confirmed by the developers, including 30 silent bugs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how self-propelled swarms can achieve global order using only sparse, random local information sampling. The authors demonstrate that this sparse sampling is highly efficient, showing that even reducing the sampled subset size can accelerate alignment. The findings establish a theoretical and experimental framework for information-efficient collective coordination.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of using inherent estimation noise (fluctuations) as the driving force for spontaneous symmetry breaking and collective order is a general principle applicable to non-equilibrium statistical physics and complex systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving global coordination among self-propelled agents without relying on a central controller or dense global communication.

**Main result.** Sparse, random sampling is shown to be an information-efficient mechanism that reliably drives disordered populations into a globally aligned, nematic state, with noise playing a constructive role in symmetry breaking.

**Method.** The authors combine analytical derivations of predictive criteria with extensive numerical simulations and physical experiments using differential-drive robots.

**Model / system.** The model consists of N point particles (or robots) in 2D, where each agent periodically reorients its motion toward the centroid of a small, randomly sampled subset of its peers.

**Key observables.** Nematic Order Parameter (S(t)), Circular Variance, and Trend Formation Time (t_f).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sampling size (n), sampling frequency, and the reorientation threshold ($R_T = \sqrt{N}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are modeled as collisionless point-particle motion, and the experimental setup maps this idealized model onto physical robots using non-overlapping offsets.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the evolution of alignment (nematic order) and the time required to reach alignment ($t_f$) when using different sampling sizes ($n=2$ vs. $n=[\sqrt{N}]$), demonstrating that smaller samples accelerate ordering.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the minimal model dynamics, to analyzing the role of sampling size on ordering metrics, deriving analytical criteria, and finally validating these findings through physical robot experiments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Achieving global coordination without a central controller or dense global communication is a defining challenge for both biological collectives and engineered swarms. We introduce and analyze a minimal model in which self-propelled agents in a bounded domain periodically reorient their motions toward the centroid of a small, randomly chosen subset of their peers, with no direct sensing of any individual neighbor's position or heading. We show that this sparse, non-local sampling rule reliably drives an initially disordered population to a globally aligned, nematic state, and that shrinking the sampled subset -- down to the minimum of two agents -- accelerates ordering rather than impeding it: the resulting estimation noise, filtered through a geometric turning rule, is itself the engine of symmetry breaking. We derive an analytical criterion, in quantitative agreement with simulation, that predicts when this ordering succeeds as a function of the sampling size and sampling frequency. We confirm the mechanism experimentally in a swarm of up to fifteen differential-drive robots. These results identify sparse random sampling as an information-efficient route to collective coordination, with implications for understanding animal collectives and for designing communication-limited robotic swarms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on the ambipolar metal-insulator transition in bilayer MoSe2 using advanced contact-free radio-frequency reflectometry. By measuring both electron and hole transport, the authors provide strong evidence that the MIT is governed by strong electron-electron interactions, leading to Wigner crystallization. This opens a quantitative transport window for studying correlated states in high-quality 2D semiconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides quantitative transport measurements in a clean, tunable 2D system, offering experimental validation for theoretical models describing strongly correlated phases like Wigner crystals.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the metal-insulator transition (MIT) in two dimensions, specifically resolving the long-standing debate over whether Coulomb interactions or disorder primarily drive this transition.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates interaction-driven MITs in bilayer MoSe2 for both electrons and holes, evidenced by comparable Wigner-Seitz radii at transition, and maps the transition using contact-free RF reflectometry.

**Method.** The authors employed contact-free radio-frequency (RF) reflectometry to measure sheet resistance and magnetotransport properties in the bilayer semiconductor.

**Model / system.** The physical system is bilayer MoSe2, a semiconductor used to study ambipolar transport. The measurements probe the transition between a correlated liquid and a Wigner solid state.

**Key observables.** Sheet resistance (R) vs. carrier density, Magnetoresistance (MR) peaks, and resonance frequency ($f_0$) shifts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wigner-Seitz radii ($r_s$), critical densities ($n_c$), and the ratio of Coulomb interaction energy to kinetic energy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The RF readout is non-perturbative, and the analysis compares results to theoretical benchmarks derived from disorder-free, symmetric gate geometries.

**Figures summary.** Figures show RF spectra, extracted sheet resistance vs. density (for both electron and hole doping), and magnetotransport ratios mapping the MIT.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents the experimental setup, characterizes the ambipolar MIT using RF measurements, analyzes the temperature and magnetic field dependence of the transition, and compares findings to theoretical models of correlated electron systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

When Coulomb interactions dominate kinetic energy, dilute two-dimensional carriers form a Wigner solid. Both its melting and disorder-driven localization can drive density-tuned metal-insulator transitions, but their relative roles and interplay have been debated for decades. Atomically thin semiconductors offer a new test because transport and scanning tunneling microscopy can probe the same material, yet poor contacts impede transport where correlations are strongest. Here we introduce contact-free radio-frequency reflectometry that senses sheet resistance through capacitive gate coupling. It resolves the electron and previously inaccessible hole transitions in the same bilayer MoSe2 device, whose carriers share disorder but not mass. Their critical densities differ fourfold yet correspond to comparable Coulomb-to-kinetic-energy ratios, where tunneling microscopy finds solid-liquid coexistence. Magnetoresistance peaks sharply at each transition, reflecting a Pomeranchuk-like, field-driven expansion of the Wigner solid against the correlated liquid. Our results demonstrate interaction-driven transitions in high-quality two-dimensional semiconductors, opening quantitative transport in contact-limited systems.

</details>

### [Efficient Estimation of Logical Sensitivities Through Fault-Counting](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10531v1)

**Authors:** Winston Fu, J. Wilson Staples, Jeff D. Thompson  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.10531v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper introduces a differentiable estimator to overcome the shot-budget limitations of traditional finite difference methods when calculating logical sensitivities in quantum error correction. By estimating the full gradient from a single simulation run, it drastically reduces the required computational resources. This efficiency allows researchers to accurately map out critical performance boundaries, such as noise thresholds, in complex, multi-parameter noise models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, statistically efficient tool for characterizing the robustness of quantum codes, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum computers by precisely mapping out the boundaries of reliable operation in complex noise landscapes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for an efficient method to estimate logical sensitivities ($
u_i = \partial p_L / \partial p_i$), which quantify how the logical error rate ($p_L$) responds to individual physical noise parameters ($p_i$) in quantum error correction.

**Main result.** A novel differentiable estimator can calculate all components of the gradient $
abla_{\mathbf p}p_L$ simultaneously from a single Monte Carlo data set, achieving a significant reduction (one to two orders of magnitude) in required simulation shots compared to standard finite difference methods.

**Method.** The core method is a differentiable estimator that leverages information about underlying fault configurations to estimate partial derivatives of the logical error rate. This is combined with Newton root finding to trace threshold contours in multi-dimensional noise models.

**Model / system.** The primary model is quantum error-correcting circuits, specifically using the surface code subjected to circuit-level noise. The analysis is performed on the logical error rate $p_L$ as a function of various physical noise parameters $p_i$.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate ($p_L$), Logical Sensitivities ($
u_i = \partial p_L / \partial p_i$), Threshold Contours ($\Delta(p) = 0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical noise parameters ($p_i$), circuit distance ($d$), shot budget ($N$), and the step size ($h$) used in finite difference methods.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The differentiable estimator relies on accessing underlying fault configurations in stabilizer tableau simulations. The analysis often uses linear approximations for small error rates and assumes the ability to use local sensitivity information to guide root finding.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the variance and estimates of sensitivities ($
u_i$) between the proposed differentiable method and standard finite differences. Other figures illustrate the tracing of threshold contours in multi-parameter noise spaces.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of sensitivity estimation, contrasts the standard finite difference approach with the proposed differentiable estimator, demonstrates the superior variance scaling of the new method, and finally applies this technique to locate and trace threshold contours in complex noise models using Newton root finding.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error-correcting circuits are affected by multiple physical noise mechanisms, whose contributions to logical failure must be understood to evaluate code performance and guide improvements in hardware. The individual error budget contributions for an error type $i$ can be characterized by its logical sensitivity $ν_i = \frac{\partial p_L}{\partial p_i}$, which measures the response of the logical error rate $p_L$ to each physical noise parameter $p_i$. Normally, $ν_i$ is measured using linear fits such as finite differences, where $p_L$ is measured at two or more values of $p_i$ to calculate partial derivatives. In this paper, we develop a differentiable estimator to obtain all components of $\nabla_{\mathbf p}p_L$ simultaneously from a single Monte Carlo data set at one noise configuration, using information about the underlying fault configurations. In surface code simulations with circuit-level noise, the estimator agrees with conventional finite differences while requiring one to two orders of magnitude fewer shots to achieve the same variance. We apply this technique to quantify error budgets, resolve sensitivities at the individual qubit level, and infer effective code distance. Finally, we incorporate the sensitivities into Newton root finding to locate and trace threshold contours in multidimensional noise models. These results provide an efficient method for extracting and applying logical sensitivity information from standard quantum error correction simulations.

</details>

### [Efficiently computable bounds on the energy-constrained quantum reading capacity](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08945v1)

**Authors:** Vishal Singh, Zixin Huang, Mark M. Wilde  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08945v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

</details>

<img src="2610.08945_figures/2610.08945_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Upper and lower bounds on the energy constrained reading capacity of a pair of loss–dephasing channels plotted against the average photon number per channel probe. In this example, the first channel has the parameters η1 = 0.95, γ1 = 0.01, and the second channel has the parameters η2 = 0.85, γ2 = 0.01. The output dimension of the channel is truncated to four.</sub>

**Summary.** This work establishes theoretical upper and lower bounds for the energy-constrained quantum reading capacity. It tackles the problem by utilizing advanced quantum information measures like relative entropies within semidefinite programming frameworks. The results provide a quantitative understanding of how energy limitations restrict the information rate achievable in quantum communication channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of capacity bounds under physical constraints like energy is crucial for designing realistic quantum communication protocols, bridging abstract theory with physical implementability.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the energy-constrained quantum reading capacity of a finite family of quantum channels.

**Main result.** Upper and lower bounds for this capacity are derived using advanced information-theoretic tools, showing how the capacity relates to channel relative entropies under an energy constraint.

**Method.** The authors use semidefinite programming (SDP) techniques, involving approximations of relative entropies (Belavkin-Staszewski and Umegaki) and alternating optimization to establish rigorous bounds.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum reading, where classical messages are encoded into sequences of quantum channels, and the reader probes these channels while respecting an average energy constraint on the probes.

**Key observables.** Quantum reading capacity ($R_{H,E}(N)$), Holevo information, Belavkin–Staszewski relative entropy, and Umegaki relative entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The average probing energy constraint ($E$), the set of quantum channels ($\{N_x\}$), and the number of uses ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The upper bound relies on SDP approximations using Gauss-Legendre quadrature, and the lower bound is derived via bilinear programming, both of which involve approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares upper and lower bounds for unconstrained capacity for unitary channels, showing overlap. Figure 2 plots upper and lower bounds for loss-dephasing channels against the average photon number.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the converse bound using relative entropy techniques, establishes the upper bound via SDP (Appendix A), and derives the lower bound using bilinear programming (Appendix B), culminating in comparisons and numerical examples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum reading, classical messages are encoded in sequences of quantum channels and recovered by probing the channels and processing their outputs. We study the reading capacity of a finite family of finite-dimensional channels under an average constraint on the probing energy, allowing arbitrary adaptive operations between channel uses. Using an input-dependent chain rule for the Belavkin-Staszewski relative entropy, we derive a converse bound expressed as an optimization involving channel Choi operators and the operator relative entropy. This formulation admits semidefinite approximations and reduces, without an energy constraint, to a channel information radius. To obtain achievable rates, we construct bilinear semidefinite lower approximations to a standard non-adaptive reading bound. Alternating optimization produces feasible probe states and encoding distributions, whose Holevo information gives a directly evaluable achievable rate. For jointly classical-quantum channels, we derive a separate converse based on the Umegaki relative entropy. Without an energy constraint, this converse matches a non-adaptive achievable rate, recovering a recent result of Pascual Abraldes and Winter: non-adaptive protocols suffice to achieve the unconstrained reading capacity of jointly classical-quantum channels.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies TAO-DFT to study ladder-type phenylenes, revealing that their electronic properties are not monotonic but exhibit a distinct damped periodic oscillation with a period of three. This periodicity dictates a transition between closed-shell and polyradical states, offering fundamental guidelines for the rational design of these carbon-based nanomaterials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a detailed, quantitative understanding of how structural periodicity dictates electronic correlation in extended $\pi$-systems, which is crucial for designing next-generation organic electronic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Accurately predicting the electronic properties of extended pi-conjugated systems containing radical character, which is a major theoretical challenge due to strong static correlation effects.

**Main result.** The electronic properties of ladder-type phenylenes exhibit a unique damped periodic oscillation, with highly correlated hotspots appearing at intervals $n = 3k - 1$. This periodicity governs the transition from nonradical to polyradical character.

**Method.** The study employs Thermally-Assisted-Occupation Density Functional Theory (TAO-DFT) to efficiently capture static correlation effects in these large systems.

**Model / system.** The physical system is ladder-type phenylenes ($n$-LPs), which are extended $\pi$-conjugated systems formed by $n$ fused four-membered carbon rings, studied for $n=1$ to $40$.

**Key observables.** Singlet-Triplet Energy Gap (EST), radical character, Symmetrized von Neumann Entropy (SvN), Vertical Ionization Potential ($I_v$), Electron Affinity ($E_a$), and Fundamental Gap ($E_g$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The periodicity of 3 in the electronic structure, and the specific structural localization of active orbitals along the central ladder framework.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is the use of TAO-DFT, which is assumed to accurately model static correlation effects, overcoming limitations of standard KS-DFT.

**Figures summary.** Figures systematically present calculated properties (gap, IP, EA, etc.) and real-space orbital distributions for small $n$ (up to $n=5$), demonstrating the evolution of electronic structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the electronic structure evolution of $n$-LPs using TAO-DFT, focusing on how key observables like the S-T gap and radical character oscillate with chain length $n$, contrasting this periodicity with monotonic trends seen in other nanographenes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accurate prediction of the electronic properties of extended $π$-conjugated systems with radical character remains a major theoretical challenge. To overcome this challenge, thermally-assisted-occupation density functional theory (TAO-DFT) has been developed in recent years. In this study, we employ TAO-DFT to systematically explore the electronic properties of ladder-type phenylenes ($n$-LPs), containing $n$ fused four-membered carbon rings. Our calculations reveal that all investigated $n$-LPs (with $n$ = 1 ~ 40) possess singlet ground states. With increasing $n$-LP size, we observe a transition from the nonradical character of smaller $n$-LPs to the polyradical character of larger $n$-LPs. Notably, we identify a distinct damped periodic oscillation in the radical character of $n$-LPs, featuring highly correlated hotspots at specific intervals ($n = 3k - 1$, where $k$ are positive integers). The real-space representation of active orbitals confirms this global electronic behavior is governed by the structural localization of active orbitals along the central ladder framework. These findings provide fundamental insights into the size-dependent electronic properties of $n$-LPs, guiding their rational design for future nanomaterial applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work performs a detailed finite-size scaling analysis on 3D Z(2) and O(2) spin models in an external field. By determining the leading finite-size correction term, the authors provide a robust method to eliminate systematic errors in calculating critical parameters. This methodology is particularly valuable for studying quantum phase transitions, such as those found in QCD.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics models, the rigorous application of finite-size scaling techniques and the connection to QCD phase transitions provide valuable methodological insights applicable to quantum critical points in many-body quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the leading finite-size scaling corrections to universal functions for 3D Z(2) and O(2) spin models when an external symmetry breaking field is present.

**Main result.** The leading finite-size correction is found to be proportional to $e_{z_L}^2 \sim 1/(H L^{1/
u_c})^2$, allowing for the precise elimination of systematic errors in critical temperature determinations, such as in (2+1)-flavor QCD.

**Method.** The study employs detailed finite-size scaling (FSS) analysis using high statistics Monte Carlo simulations, fitting scaled observables to scaling ansatze derived from the Renormalization Group approach.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the 3-dimensional Z(2) and O(2) spin models, studied under the influence of a non-vanishing external magnetic field H. The analysis uses concepts from lattice field theory and critical phenomena.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the singular parts of thermodynamic quantities, including the order parameter (M) and susceptibility ($\chi_M$), analyzed via scaling functions $f_{\chi}(z, z_L)$ and $f_G(z, z_L)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the external field H, lattice size L, and the scaling variable $e_{z_L} = 1/(H L^{1/
u_c})$. Critical exponents ($\delta, eta, 
u_c$) are also determined.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the leading order finite-size correction is controlled by the parameter $e_{z_L}^2$, and it compares results across different scaling variables to estimate systematic uncertainties.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows scaled observables ($\chi_M/M$, etc.) plotted against $e_{z_L}$ and $e_{z_L}^2$ for both Z(2) and O(2) models. Figure 6 compares the free energy density $f_G$ for both models at zero and non-zero fields. Figure 7 shows the ratio $H\chi M/M$ versus the coupling $z$ for the Z(2) model.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the scaling functions for the spin models, first establishing the appropriate scaling variable ($e_{z_L}^2$), then fitting data to extract leading and next-to-leading order coefficients, and finally applying these results to fix systematic errors in physical observables like the chiral transition temperature.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present results from a detailed finite-size scaling analysis of the $3$-$d$, $Z(2)$ and $O(2)$ spin models in an external field $H$. Using high statistics Monte Carlo data, we obtain the leading finite-size scaling correction to the infinite volume scaling functions. We show that these corrections are proportional to $\widetilde{z}_L^2=1/(H L^{3/(1+1/δ))})^{2}$. This provides the parametric form for finite-size corrections to bulk thermodynamic observables as well as the pseudo-critical temperatures determined at non-vanishing $H$. In particular, it allows to eliminate systematic errors in the analysis of the chiral phase transition temperature in (2+1)-flavor QCD, that arose from the previously not well-controlled ansatz for infinite volume extrapolations. We also establish the validity range of this leading order correction and point out that there are significant differences between the $3$-$d$, $O(2)$ and $Z(2)$ universality classes.

</details>

### [Inequivalent Quantum Resources from Multipartite State Discrimination](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10143v1)

**Authors:** Srijani Das, Tuhin Paul, Manasi Patra, Atanu Bhunia, Ramij Rahaman  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.10143v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.10143_figures/2610.10143_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Circuit for nondestructive GHZ-state discrimination using an ancillary Bell state |Φ+⟩A′ jA′ k shared between nodes A j(j ∈s) and Ak(k ∈¯s) (pink). Controlled-NOT gates, with the system qubits as controls, are applied from Aj to A′ j and from Ak to A′ k. The ancilla remains in |Φ+⟩for even parity of the two system qubits and trans- forms to |Ψ+⟩for odd parity. Computational-basis measurements of the ancillas therefore reveal the parity without disturbing the input GHZ state, which is recovered unchanged at the output.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the resource cost associated with distinguishing orthogonal multipartite quantum states using LOCC. It proves that the required quantum resource is highly dependent on the geometric separation (Hamming distance) between the states. The results establish a clear operational asymmetry, showing that non-zero distance allows for entanglement-preserving discrimination using minimal auxiliary resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide a fine-grained understanding of multipartite nonlocality by quantifying how geometric properties (Hamming distance) dictate the minimum quantum resources needed for state discrimination, which is crucial for quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the operational resource cost required for locally distinguishing between orthogonal multipartite quantum states, specifically addressing whether this cost is state-dependent.

**Main result.** The resource cost of LOCC discrimination is state-dependent, showing that two orthogonal GHZ states with nonzero relative Hamming distance can be distinguished using only a single ancillary Bell pair, while zero-distance pairs require no net quantum resource extraction.

**Method.** The authors employ a resource theory framework based on Local Operations and Classical Communication (LOCC), utilizing entanglement monotones and the Hamming distance to characterize distinguishability.

**Model / system.** The focus is on multipartite quantum systems, particularly $n$-qubit Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states, and the analysis extends to larger ensembles of such states.

**Key observables.** Quantum resources (e.g., entanglement), operational cost of LOCC discrimination, and the relative Hamming distance ($d_H$) between the states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Hamming distance ($d_H$) between the states dictates the required auxiliary resource; the number of qubits ($n$) and the size of the ensemble ($k$) are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes the ability to perform non-destructive discrimination, which requires the preservation of the target state's intrinsic multipartite entanglement.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the quantum circuit used for nondestructive GHZ-state discrimination, involving an ancillary Bell state shared between two parties and subsequent measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the bipartite case to the general multipartite case, establishing theorems regarding distinguishability based on the Hamming distance. It contrasts resource consumption for zero vs. non-zero distance pairs, culminating in protocols for entanglement-preserving discrimination.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Any two orthogonal quantum states can be perfectly distinguished via local operations and classical communication (LOCC), yet whether all orthogonal multipartite pairs incur an identical operational cost remains an open question. Here, we address the resource consumption required for local state discrimination across multipartite pair of states. We demonstrate that distinct pairs of orthogonal multipartite maximally entangled states require unequal amounts of quantum resources to achieve local distinguishability. Extending our framework beyond individual pairs, we analyze larger sets of both locally distinguishable and indistinguishable multipartite orthogonal states, revealing a nontrivial hierarchy in their entanglement consumption. These results show that the resource cost of LOCC discrimination is state-dependent, providing insight into the fine-grained structure of multipartite nonlocality.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies advanced statistical mechanics tools, particularly the Wealth Thermalization Hypothesis, to analyze the biomass distribution of wild mammals. It finds the distribution exhibits extreme inequality, which is accurately modeled by an extended Rayleigh-Jeans spectral distribution. This suggests that the concentration of biomass among large species follows a universal statistical law, analogous to wealth concentration in human societies.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject is biology/economics, the methodology relies heavily on applying concepts from statistical mechanics (RJ distribution, thermalization hypotheses) to model complex, non-equilibrium distributions, which is a general theoretical tool.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper statistically analyzes the global biomass distribution of wild terrestrial mammals, drawing parallels with tools used to characterize wealth inequality.

**Main result.** The biomass distribution exhibits extreme inequality, which is successfully modeled by an extended Rayleigh-Jeans (RJ) spectral model, suggesting a universal thermalization principle.

**Method.** The analysis employs economic tools like the Lorenz curve and Gini coefficient, applying concepts from statistical mechanics, specifically the Wealth Thermalization Hypothesis (WTH) using RJ distributions.

**Model / system.** The system is the biomass distribution of 4795 wild mammal species. The model uses the RJ steady-state distribution, comparing the data to theoretical curves derived from both RJS and RJE spectral models.

**Key observables.** Gini coefficient (G=0.944), Lorenz curve, and Pareto curve describing biomass concentration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The model parameters include the temperature T and chemical potential $\mu$, leading to optimized parameters like $\epsilon_{	ext{RJE}} = 7.57 	imes 10^{-4}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is the analogy between biomass/energy and wealth/energy, suggesting that the observed distribution is a robust, mechanism-independent thermalization outcome.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the real Lorenz curve against theoretical curves derived from RJS and RJE models, demonstrating the fit across multiple orders of magnitude of biomass variation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the statistical analysis using economic tools, then details the WTH framework and the RJ modeling, compares the theoretical models to the real mammal data, and finally discusses the implications for ecological mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using the recently published global census of the biomass of wild terrestrial mammals, we perform a detailed mathematical statistical analysis of its distribution over $N_s=4795$ species, drawing on tools developed in economics to characterize wealth inequality. We show that the Lorenz curve of the mammal biomass distribution is characterized by a large Gini coefficient $G=0.944$ exceeding the inequality reported for wealth distribution among world countries. This distribution is compared to the predictions of the Wealth Thermalization Hypothesis (WTH), in which species biomass values are treated as energy levels populated according to a Rayleigh-Jeans (RJ) steady-state distribution. We show that an RJ extended spectral model reproduces the real Lorenz and Pareto curves over four orders of magnitude of biomass variation, capturing the strong condensation of biomass among the rare, heavy-bodied species and its near-absence among the vast majority of light-bodied ones. These results extend the WTH framework, previously validated on distributions of human economic origin, to a biological distribution shaped by ecological and evolutionary constraints, and place the extreme rarity of large-bodied mammal species within the same statistical mathematical description as the oligarchic concentration of wealth in human societies. We finally discuss possible ecological mechanisms that may contribute to the observed distribution, including interspecific interactions, food availability, and competition.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the security of Rabin Oblivious Transfer by comparing classical, pure-state quantum, and novel mixed-state quantum protocols. The authors demonstrate that incorporating mixed quantum states generally enhances the security bounds of the OT protocol compared to using pure states alone. This advances the understanding of information-theoretically secure quantum primitives.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cryptography, the core techniques involve analyzing the robustness of quantum information protocols against eavesdropping and cheating, which relates to fundamental concepts in quantum channel capacity and security proofs.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether using mixed quantum states can improve the security and performance of Rabin Oblivious Transfer (OT) protocols compared to classical or pure-state quantum protocols.

**Main result.** Mixed-state protocols are shown to generally outperform pure-state protocols across various parameters, achieving superior security compared to classical methods, especially for higher success probabilities.

**Method.** The authors derive and compare cheating probabilities ($B_{OT}, A_{OT}$) for multiple protocols (pure-state, two-qubit mixed-state, single-qubit mixed-state) using quantum information theory tools.

**Model / system.** The model is the Rabin Oblivious Transfer (OT) primitive, where a sender transmits a bit to a receiver who learns it probabilistically, while the sender remains ignorant of the outcome.

**Key observables.** Bob's cheating probability ($B_{OT}$) and Alice's cheating probability ($A_{OT}$), plotted against the success probability ($p_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Success probability ($p_s$), angle parameters ($\phi$), and measurement parameters ($r, s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes Alice has perfect memory; the comparison is based on monitoring probabilities rather than full-testing regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 7 compares Bob's cheating probabilities for three protocols (pure-state, Protocol 4, Protocol 5) across varying success probabilities ($p_s$), showing performance crossovers between the protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the security bounds of classical, pure-state, and novel mixed-state protocols for Rabin OT, deriving specific cheating probability formulas for each case and comparing them graphically.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Oblivious transfer (OT) is a fundamental cryptographic primitive that is useful for realizing any two-party computation. Since information-theoretically secure perfect OT is impossible classically and quantumly, research has focused on imperfect protocols, exploring how close to perfect one can get against unbounded dishonest parties. While current approaches often make use of pure quantum states, recent work suggests that mixed quantum states may improve performance. In this paper, we investigate the use of mixed quantum states in Rabin oblivious transfer, where a sender holds one bit and a receiver learns its value with a certain probability while the sender remains ignorant of whether the receiver learned it. We present three mixed-state protocols constructed with two distinct methods, extending an existing pure-state scheme and employing a probabilistic approach, and demonstrate improved performance compared to classical and quantum pure-state protocols across varying parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a hybrid quantum-classical machine learning framework to perform anomaly detection on scarce, time-series data from solar power plants. By integrating a Transformer autoencoder with a Quantum Neural Network, the model aims to overcome the limitations of classical methods in handling data imbalance and model complexity. The results show that this hybrid approach maintains high accuracy while drastically reducing the model's trainable parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is in energy systems, the core methodology involves mapping classical time-series features into a quantum state space for classification, which touches upon quantum feature mapping techniques relevant to quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of anomaly detection on small, highly unbalanced datasets, particularly in time-series data from critical infrastructure.

**Main result.** The proposed two-step hybrid classical-quantum architecture achieves competitive prediction accuracy while demonstrating significant parameter efficiency compared to purely classical or fully quantum approaches.

**Method.** A novel hybrid model is proposed, combining a classical Transformer-based autoencoder for feature extraction with a Quantum Neural Network (QNN) for final classification.

**Model / system.** The system is simulated using data from a large-scale photovoltaic (PV) power plant undergoing various artificially induced hardware failures (e.g., short circuit, degradation).

**Key observables.** Balanced Accuracy (Acc_b), F1 Score, and the number of trainable parameters (#Parameters) are key metrics used for comparison.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The simulation spans 16 days with a 1 Hz sampling rate, and the quantum circuit depth (R) and connectivity are important design choices.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that quantum machine learning can provide a more sustainable and interpretable alternative to classical deep learning models for scarce data.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PV plant's current levels over time, the feature distribution of different fault types, and the comparison of performance metrics ($	ext{Acc}_b$) across different hybrid ansatz constructions.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of identifying the AD challenge, proposing the hybrid architecture (AE $	o$ QNN), detailing the training process (MSE loss followed by weighted cross-entropy loss), and finally benchmarking performance against classical baselines.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Anomaly detection on small and unbalanced datasets remains very challenging in machine learning, although this scenario is common in several domains, including healthcare, cybersecurity, finance, and energy. Data augmentation and generative AI may mitigate training-data scarcity, but they often fall short because anomalies are, by definition, unpredictable, rare, and highly diverse events compared to high-probability normal data. Overfitting to pseudo-anomalies, model collapse, high-dimensional data, uninterpretable black-box models, and validation challenges are typical issues limiting their practical applicability. In this context, quantum machine learning may provide a promising and more sustainable avenue because it can enable more interpretable models with far fewer trainable parameters and smaller datasets, implementable on energy-efficient quantum hardware. Here, we propose a novel two-step hybrid classical--quantum architecture for sequential data and test it on a realistic scenario in the global energy-transition domain, i.e., automated anomaly detection in large-scale photovoltaic plants. The achieved generalization capability and competitive prediction accuracy may pave the way for new hybrid learning models able to exploit the continuously increasing power of cloud-available and more sustainable quantum accelerators integrated with more traditional energy-hungry High Performance Computing resources.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers developed a method using magnons in a $	ext{CrSBr}$ sensor to remotely measure the phase stiffness of superconducting cuprates ($	ext{BSCCO}$). By observing the enhancement of the magnon group velocity upon coupling to the superconductor, they quantitatively mapped the superconducting phase stiffness and detected the universal jump characteristic of the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel, non-invasive optical probe for measuring fundamental superconducting parameters like phase stiffness, linking magnon physics directly to high-temperature superconductivity phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a local, contactless, and all-optical measurement technique for superconducting phase stiffness ($ho_s$) and to study the evolution of superconducting phase coherence, particularly observing the Berezinskii–Kosterlitz–Thouless (BKT) transition in cuprates.

**Main result.** The study successfully identified a parallel-field-induced Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition in bulk cuprates by observing the universal phase-stiffness jump using remote magnon sensing.

**Method.** The technique uses propagating antiferromagnetic magnons in a $	ext{CrSBr}$ sensor interfaced with a superconductor ($	ext{BSCCO}$) to remotely probe superconducting electrodynamics by measuring the magnon group velocity enhancement.

**Model / system.** The system involves a $	ext{CrSBr}/	ext{BSCCO}$ heterostructure where the magnon group velocity is enhanced due to the induction of local Meissner screening currents in the superconductor. The phase stiffness ($ho_s$) is quantitatively extracted from this enhanced magnon group velocity.

**Key observables.** Magnon group velocity ($\langle v_g angle$), Superfluid stiffness ($ho_s$), BKT transition jump.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Underdoped $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Sr}_2	ext{CaCu}_2	ext{O}_{8+\delta}$, magnetic field ($B_\parallel$), temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The enhancement of $\langle v_g angle$ is directly proportional to the superconducting phase stiffness ($ho_s$) via the London penetration depth ($\lambda_L$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the enhanced magnon group velocity in $	ext{CrSBr}/	ext{BSCCO}$ compared to bare $	ext{CrSBr}$. Figure 2 illustrates the temperature dependence of this enhancement, used to map out $ho_s(T)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical link between magnon dynamics and superconducting electrodynamics, details the all-optical measurement protocol using magnon wavepackets, and applies this method to observe the field-induced BKT transition in cuprates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phase stiffness governs macroscopic quantum coherence in superfluids and superconductors, providing a sensitive probe of condensate depletion, fluctuations, and phase transitions. However quantitative and spatially resolved measurements remain challenging. Here we report a contactless, local, all-optical measurement of superconducting phase stiffness using propagating antiferromagnetic magnons as remote sensors. In CrSBr, strong exciton-magnon coupling enables optical excitation and detection of magnon wavepackets and precise determination of their group velocity. When CrSBr is interfaced with underdoped Bi$_2$Sr$_2$CaCu$_2$O$_{8+δ}$, we find that the magnon group velocity is substantially enhanced below the superconducting transition and quantitatively encodes the phase stiffness through Meissner screening currents. Using this capability, we identify a parallel-field-induced Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition through direct observation of the universal phase-stiffness jump, revealing a field-controlled route to expose intrinsic two-dimensional phase-ordering physics in a bulk cuprate. Our work establishes propagating magnons as local probes of superconducting electrodynamics and phase-coherent phenomena in low-dimensional quantum materials.

</details>

### [Singlet sector of the vector model with angular potential](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09815v1)

**Authors:** Srijan Kumar  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09815v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.09815_figures/2610.09815_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1. Large-N free-theory Gross–Witten–Wadia phase boundary at fixed Tr and N(1 −ω2). Here ρ(α) is the holonomy eigenvalue density. The curve, defined by ρ(π) = 0, uses the leading term of equation (1.3) with the sum truncated at n = 5000. The regions above and below the curve are ungapped and gapped, respectively. The figure is used as an illustration of the large N result and should not be interpreted as a finite-N calculation.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the phase transitions of a large-N O(N) vector model on a sphere with an added angular potential. Using advanced techniques like the large-N limit and saddle-point approximation, the authors determine the critical conditions for the Gross-Witten-Wadia transition. The work shows how the potential tunes this transition and reveals non-trivial features in the free energy near the critical points.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of phase transitions in complex field theories, especially those involving geometric constraints (like the singlet sector) and external potentials, provides deep insights into critical phenomena relevant to condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the singlet sector of the large-N, O(N) vector model subjected to an angular potential $\Omega$. The core goal is to analyze the phase transitions occurring in this system.

**Main result.** The angular potential modifies the Gross-Witten-Wadia transition, allowing it to occur even at fixed temperature. The free energy exhibits a double pole in the ungapped phase, suggesting non-local physics.

**Method.** The analysis employs the large-N limit, utilizing Hubbard-Stratonovich transformations and saddle-point approximations to evaluate the free energy and determine critical lines.

**Model / system.** The model is the O(N) vector model defined on $S^1 	imes S^2_r$, restricted to its singlet sector by averaging over the O(N) holonomy. The Hamiltonian includes a term proportional to the angular potential $\Omega$.

**Key observables.** Free energy ($\log Z_{	ext{sing}}$), critical temperature ($T_{	ext{cr}}$), and the holonomy eigenvalue distribution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), angular potential ($\Omega$), radius of $S^2$ ($r$), and the large-N limit ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on the large-N limit and the saddle-point approximation for evaluating the partition function integral.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the large-N free-theory GW-W phase boundary in the $T_r - N(\dots)$ plane.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the system and implementing the singlet projection. It then calculates the free energy at both fixed points, derives the critical transition condition, and analyzes the effect of the angular potential and subleading corrections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the singlet sector of large-$N$, $O(N)$ vector model on $S^1_β\times S^2_r$ in presence of an angular potential $Ω$. We compute the free energy of the model both at the free fixed point as well as the non-trivial fixed point. The model undergoes a phase transition when the temperature $T$ and angular potential attain the relation $T^2r^2\sim N (1-ω^2)$, where $ω=rΩ$ and $r$ is the radius of $S^2$, with $Tr\gg1$, at both the fixed points, with different numerical coefficients. The transition was studied earlier at zero angular potential at $Tr\sim \sqrt{N}$. The holonomy eigenvalue distribution, having support on the full circle below the transition, develops a gap above it, called the Gross-Witten-Wadia transition. The angular potential introduces an additional parameter to such a transition, tuning it, the transition can occur even at a fixed temperature. We show that for the free model transition can occur as $1-ω^2\sim 1/N$ while $Tr\sim O(1)$. For the interacting model the computation is performed at a limit $Tr\gg1$, thus we show the same at a large but fixed temperature which does not scale as $\sqrt{N}$. In the ungapped phase, the free energy exhibits a double pole near $ω^2=1$ for both the fixed points. This is possibly due to the fact that the theory projected onto the singlets is non-local and therefore need not obey the leading simple pole scaling of a local thermal effective action.

</details>

### [Spurious zero-dispersion continua in full $(2,q)$-positivity: A linear hierarchy-depth lower bound from an exactly solvable counting operator](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09698v1)

**Authors:** Maho Nakata  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09698v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
