**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the mathematical constraints imposed on many-fermion systems when using approximate reduced density matrices. It shows that imposing positivity constraints up to a fixed level $q$ can incorrectly predict the existence of noninteger eigenvalues (spurious continua) for certain operators. The work provides an exact threshold for when these approximations fail, guiding the development of more robust theoretical methods for strongly correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical treatment of N-representability constraints and the identification of systematic failure modes (spurious continua) are crucial for developing reliable theoretical tools for strongly correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the spurious zero-dispersion continua that can arise when approximating the spectrum of many-fermion systems using variational reduced density matrices (RDMs) subject to semidefinite constraints.

**Main result.** It determines the exact set of accepted eigenvalues for a solvable counting operator at every level of the full hierarchy of particle-hole constraints, showing that false noninteger eigenvalues appear unless the constraint level grows linearly with particle number.

**Method.** The analysis employs the 'dispersion test' on an exactly solvable counting operator, constructing pseudo reduced density matrices by analytically continuing occupation probabilities to noninteger particle numbers.

**Model / system.** The study concerns fermionic many-body systems, using the framework of reduced density matrices and imposing constraints derived from full $(2, q)$-positivity conditions.

**Key observables.** The set of accepted eigenvalues for the counting operator, the minimized dispersion, and the occupation-correlation matrices.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The hierarchy level $q$ (positivity order), the particle number $N$, and the orbital basis size $r$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of the full hierarchy of semidefinite constraints and relies on constructing pseudo-reduced density matrices via signed interpolation.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the constraints imposed by full $(2, q)$-positivity, establishing equivalences between polar and full formulations, and then applies this framework to a solvable counting model to pinpoint the exact spectrum and the conditions under which spurious eigenvalues appear.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational two-particle reduced-density-matrix methods impose hierarchies of semidefinite constraints. We ask whether a fixed level of such a hierarchy can recover even an elementary many-fermion spectrum, using   the dispersion test, in which a value is accepted as an eigenvalue when the minimized dispersion vanishes. For an exactly solvable operator that counts fermions in one half of the spin orbitals, whose spectrum   consists of integers, we determine the complete set of accepted values at every level of the full hierarchy of particle-hole semidefinite constraints. Besides the integers, this set contains an entire interval of   noninteger values unless the hierarchy level grows linearly with the particle number, and we give the exact threshold. The proof constructs explicit pseudo reduced density matrices that are diagonal in the   occupation basis, obtained by continuing the occupation probabilities of a fixed-particle-number state to a noninteger particle number. Classical semidefinite constraints on occupation correlations, supplemented by   nonnegative occupation-pattern probabilities, suffice to establish fermionic positivity. For this counting model the two relaxations have the same dispersion zero set, not necessarily the same feasible states, and a   converse argument shows that no other false eigenvalues occur. The limitation is specific to the hierarchy: two-body inequalities that encode the integer spectrum would remove every false value but are not generated   at a fixed level. We also verify that the full semidefinite constraints and their complete projection onto two-particle reduced-density-matrix space are equivalent in finite dimensions, so that the result applies   equally to the polar formulation.

</details>

### [Sufficient quantum provenance: retained fields and certified recording precision](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09931v1)

**Authors:** George Kourousias, Sergio Carrato, Roberto Pugliese, Aljoša Hafner, Francesco Guzzi, Matteo Billè, Fulvio Billè, Panagiotis Dimitrakis  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09931v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

(b) QAOA-1 gate-noise bin widths increase with G under equal intervals in arcsin √</sub>

</details>

<img src="2610.09931_figures/2610.09931_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

Each panel shows the achieved record-cell diameter RΩ(S) for the reference-mixture rule, singleton-threshold rule, and exhaustive minimum-cost sufficient set over the five declared tolerances. The shaded region marks RΩ(S) ≤ε; points above it violate their nominal tolerance. The annotated QAOA-1 case at ε = 0.05 is the compositional failure discussed in the text: the singleton rule attains 0.0514 although routing and angle bias each pass the one-field omission check separately. **Summary.** This paper develops a formal framework to quantify 'sufficient quantum provenance,' determining the minimum amount of execution data needed to guarantee that two quantum computations yield results within a specified error tolerance. By using metrics like the Hellinger distance, the authors provide concrete bit-level requirements for recording noise and rotation details. This work is vital for establishing trust and comparability in the emerging field of quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantifiable framework for certifying quantum computation results, which is crucial for building trustworthy quantum algorithms and comparing results across different hardware platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how to determine the minimal set of execution details (provenance fields) and the required recording precision necessary to certify that two quantum computations yield outcome laws close enough within a specified tolerance.

**Main result.** It derives a joint stochastic–coherent certificate that bounds the Hellinger distance, showing that for a specific QAOA instance, a 13-bit record guarantees the required precision.

**Method.** The authors use information-theoretic techniques, framing the problem as finding a minimum-cost sufficient set of fields to bound the worst-case Hellinger distance between outcome laws.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes quantum computations, specifically using examples like QAOA, executed on quantum channels. The comparison is quantified using the Hellinger distance between outcome probability distributions.

**Key observables.** Hellinger distance ($H(p, q)$), latent noise flags, conditional quantum fidelity, and the required number of bits for the comparison record.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tolerance ($\epsilon$, e.g., 0.05), circuit depth, number of qubits (e.g., four-qubit QAOA), and the cost associated with retaining different provenance fields.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The hardware conclusions are conditional on stationary independent shots and are distinct from the analytic channel certificate. The record only guarantees agreement within tolerance, not absolute equivalence.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention tables comparing analytically sufficient precision (record bits) for various QAOA and HEA instances across different certificate types, and a figure illustrating constructive recording budgets.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the general problem of sufficient provenance to deriving a joint certificate for continuous precision, providing concrete results for QAOA, and concluding with hardware-specific minimum label counts for finite context classes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Which execution details must a quantum computation retain, and to what precision, to support a declared comparison? We define a sufficient record by the largest Hellinger distance between outcome laws that share it. Our main result is a circuit-derived certificate for continuous recording precision: independent channel-mixture probabilities enter through the affinity of latent noise flags, while coherent rotation differences enter through conditional quantum fidelity. Their joint composition bounds whole record cells without simulating their outcome distributions. For four-qubit QAOA at depth one, a 13-bit comparison record guarantees distance below 0.05 throughout a declared continuous noise box, uniformly over programmed angles when comparisons hold the logical task fixed. A construction with observable noise flags attains the general bound. Exact tensorization of Hellinger affinity connects recording precision to the length of a future measurement transcript. On finite context classes, separation witnesses certify minimum-cost field retention; a four-field record is the unique minimum for 28 archived QAOA instances. Fresh six-qubit processor measurements establish different minimum label counts at one common tolerance: two labels are necessary and sufficient for raw laws, while one suffices after a fixed decoder for a synthetic image-segmentation task. These hardware conclusions are conditional on stationary independent shots and remain distinct from the analytic channel certificate. Shared task definitions and evidential archives are retained separately. The framework specifies which execution distinctions matter, how precisely to record them, and what evidence supports the resulting agreement guarantee.

</details>

<a id="paper-2610.09485"></a>
### [The Geometry of Lee-Yang Tensors](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09485v1)

**Authors:** Jake Hofgard  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09485v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.09485_figures/2610.09485_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: The roots of the univariate polynomial Fexp(−βH)(s, . . . , s) for a k-local Hamiltonian of the form H = I −|ψ⟩⟨ψ|, where |ψ⟩= |0⟩⊗k. For k = 2, the resulting Hamiltonian is Suzuki-Fisher and hence its roots avoid the interior of the unit disk, but for all k ≥3, roots shatter symmetrically from s = i for sufficiently small β &gt; 0, ensuring that at least one enters the interior of the disk.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a geometric understanding of Lee-Yang tensors to analyze the spectral properties of quantum Hamiltonians. It establishes a local-to-global principle for bounding spectral gaps in specific Hamiltonians, most notably the Suzuki-Fisher class. The research provides a rigorous characterization of these Hamiltonians based on the persistence of their Lee-Yang radius across all inverse temperatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between spectral properties (gaps) and analytic properties (Lee-Yang radius) provides a powerful, non-standard diagnostic tool for understanding quantum phase transitions and the stability of ground states in interacting quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a geometric description of Lee-Yang tensors and use this framework to establish powerful local-to-global principles for bounding spectral gaps in quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** It proves a local-to-global principle for bounding spectral gaps of Suzuki-Fisher Hamiltonians using only the Lee-Yang radius of local operators, and characterizes these Hamiltonians based on their Gibbs state properties.

**Method.** The methodology combines complex Perron-Frobenius theory with the analysis of generating polynomials associated with the Lee-Yang radius, leading to asymptotic and perturbative constraints on the Hamiltonian structure.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum many-body systems described by $k$-local Hamiltonians, particularly the Suzuki-Fisher family of 2-local Hamiltonians, and their associated Gibbs states $\exp(-eta H)$.

**Key observables.** Lee-Yang radius ($r$), Spectral Gap, Spectral Ratio, and the generating polynomial $F_H(z, w)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), the locality $k$ of the Hamiltonian, and the radius $r$ of the Lee-Yang tensor.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis heavily relies on generalizing results from complex Perron-Frobenius theory and assumes the applicability of the local-to-global principle derived from the Lee-Yang radius.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by generalizing spectral ratio bounds using complex Perron-Frobenius theory, deriving a local-to-global principle for spectral gaps, and then using perturbative techniques to characterize the class of Hamiltonians (Suzuki-Fisher) whose Gibbs states maintain a large Lee-Yang radius for all $eta \ge 0$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We provide a geometric description of Lee-Yang tensors, building upon several recent results of Bravyi, Gosset, Liu, and Wong. By applying tools from complex Perron-Frobenius theory, we generalize one of their key lemmas to bound the spectral ratio of non-normal operators with Lee-Yang radius greater than one. As a direct consequence, we provide a local-to-global principle for bounding spectral gaps of Suzuki-Fisher Hamiltonians, a family of $2$-local Hamiltonians, using only the Lee-Yang radius of each local operator to bound the gap from below. On the other hand, we develop a simple perturbative technique to show that the Gibbs states of $k$-local Hamiltonians, $k \geq 3$, cannot have Lee-Yang radius at least one for all inverse temperatures $β\geq 0$. We apply this same method to conclude that Suzuki-Fisher Hamiltonians are precisely those Hamiltonians whose Gibbs states are Lee-Yang at all inverse temperatures $β\geq 0$.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how valley transitions degrade quantum information during spin shuttling in Si/SiGe quantum dots. The authors find that operating at high, specific shuttling velocities allows for coherent information transfer, suppressing dephasing. They propose advanced control protocols based on the Partial Landau-Zener model to maximize this shuttling fidelity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fault-tolerance in solid-state quantum computing by providing quantitative criteria and control mechanisms (PLZ model) to maintain quantum coherence during qubit movement.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is mitigating valley transitions during coherent spin shuttling in Si/SiGe heterostructures, which threatens the fidelity of quantum information transfer.

**Main result.** The authors identify a fast shuttling velocity regime (tens of m/s) where quantum information is preserved despite valley excitations, enabling coherent spin shuttling independent of distance.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical modeling, analyzing the time evolution of a two-level system representing the valley states by solving the time-dependent Schrödinger equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum dot in Si/SiGe heterostructures, modeled as a two-level system governed by a Hamiltonian involving intervalley coupling ($\Delta$) and valley splitting ($E_{VS}$).

**Key observables.** Valley shuttling fidelity ($F_V$), valley splitting ($E_{VS}$), and the complex evolution of the intervalley coupling ($\Delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Shuttling velocity ($v$), shuttling distance, and the comparison between Landau-Zener (LZ) and Partial Landau-Zener (PLZ) models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The electron is assumed to start in the valley ground state, and the analysis relies on modeling the system as a two-level approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of valley splitting on location, the complex evolution of the coupling $\Delta$, and the fidelity $F_V$ versus shuttling distance for various velocity regimes and control strategies.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical challenge and Hamiltonian, analyzing different shuttling velocity regimes (DVL, FVT, CVS), and finally proposing and comparing advanced control strategies like PLZ-based adapted velocity modulation against standard methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Valley excitations are considered one of the biggest challenges for a coherent spin shuttling in Si/SiGe heterostructures. In this work we theoretically investigate the valley dynamics during spin shuttling in a Si/SiGe heterostructure by considering a two-level system given by the two low-lying valley states of the mobile quantum dot. We assume that the electron is initially in the valley ground state and analyze the evolution of this state during shuttling. We find that, depending on the shuttling velocity, the system can be in three distinct regimes. In one of these regimes, the quantum information is preserved even in the presence of the valley excitations, allowing for coherent spin shuttling independently of the shuttling distance. This regime is obtained for a shuttling velocity of tens of m/s, a desired regime where dephasing mechanisms, such as spin-valley hotspots and spatially varying spin splitting, can be suppressed. This result is in accordance with recent experimental work that obtained a high spin shuttling fidelity after a fast and long shuttling. We also propose strategies based on the Partial Landau-Zener model to enhance the valley shuttling fidelity, which was found to be much more efficient than previously proposed strategies based on the standard Landau-Zener model.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously audits the comparison of variational quantum optimizers by demonstrating that the reported performance metrics are heavily biased by the chosen evaluation convention. By implementing a standardized cost accounting that charges every step, the authors show that the apparent superiority of one optimizer over another can vanish when reporting conventions are changed. It serves as a methodological guide for future quantum algorithm benchmarking.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a critical methodological framework for evaluating quantum machine learning algorithms, which is crucial for researchers building practical quantum computation benchmarks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the bias in comparing variational quantum optimizers by analyzing how different reporting conventions (e.g., success-only runs vs. full budget accounting) affect performance metrics.

**Main result.** The reported performance of quantum optimizers, particularly Quantum Natural Gradient (QNG), is highly dependent on the chosen evaluation convention and target setting, suggesting that single-metric comparisons are misleading.

**Method.** The authors conduct a rigorous comparison of optimizers (QNG, Adam, SPSA) by implementing a standardized cost accounting system that prices every step in circuit evaluations.

**Model / system.** The model involves variational quantum circuits used to prepare quantum states on $n$ qubits, evaluated using global and local cost functions.

**Key observables.** Time-to-target ($	au$), total circuit evaluations required, ratio of medians (SPSA vs. Adam), and the number of initializations where one optimizer reaches the target first (QNG vs. Adam).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit widths (3 to 6 qubits), target loss thresholds ($	au=0.20$ or $	au=0.01$), and fixed computational budgets ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that a fair comparison requires charging every run that misses the target the full computational budget and using an exactly computed metric for QNG.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the impact of different reporting choices (success-only vs. budget-capped) on time-to-target comparisons, and tables quantify the performance differences between optimizers under various conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically critiques existing benchmarking practices, presents a standardized methodology involving fixed budgets and step pricing, and then compares QNG against classical optimizers across multiple cost families and target strictness levels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Several published comparisons of variational quantum optimizers time only runs that reach a target loss, or read the verdict at a single target. Either convention could decide whether an optimizer's costlier steps pay off. We measure how much each convention changes verdicts among Adam, simultaneous perturbation stochastic approximation (SPSA) and quantum natural gradient (QNG), on initializations held out from the selection of settings. We compute the exact metric that preconditions QNG, price every step in circuit evaluations and give every method the same budget. In a median pooled over circuit widths, cost families and a sweep of settings with common misses, SPSA needs more than twice Adam's evaluations to reach a loose target. Dropping the censored runs that miss the target hides this gap. On the global-cost family we hold fixed the settings selected for a strict target. QNG then usually reaches the loose target after Adam but the strict target first. QNG's strict-target lead disappears when the metric's simulator price, linear in the number of parameters, is replaced by an assumed hardware count quadratic in that number. We recommend charging every miss the budget and reporting verdicts across targets.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a generalized framework to describe zero modes at defects in FQH-SC heterostructures, extending previous results to non-Abelian topological orders. By distinguishing between different physical processes—tunneling versus direct transfer—the authors derive unique signatures that characterize the underlying topological structure, notably confirming the Fibonacci nature of the Read-Rezayi state.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, measurable diagnostic (distinct amplitude ratios) to distinguish between different types of topological order (e.g., Fibonacci vs. Abelian) in superconducting quantum materials, which is highly relevant for realizing topological quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of finding a general local operator description for zero modes bound to charge-conjugation defects in non-Abelian Fractional Quantum Hall-Superconductor (FQH-SC) heterostructures.

**Main result.** The authors successfully construct a generalized zero mode algebra and predict distinct universal amplitude ratios for tunneling versus direct zero mode anyon transfer, providing a signature for the underlying topological order.

**Method.** The methodology involves generalizing the bulk-to-boundary construction, mapping bulk Wilson operators to localized zero mode operators, and analyzing the resulting fusion-channel dependence.

**Model / system.** The system studied is FQH-SC heterostructures built from Abelian and non-Abelian FQH states, where the superconducting proximity effect creates a charge-conjugation defect line.

**Key observables.** Zero mode anyons, the fusion-channel dependence of tunneling amplitudes, and the comparison between tunneling-probed bulk monodromy and direct transfer-probed $G$-crossed associativity data.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fractions $
u=1/m$ (Laughlin), $
u=1/m$ (Moore-Read), and $
u=3/(3M+2)$ (Read-Rezayi); the underlying topological order (Abelian vs. non-Abelian).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the superconducting proximity effect gaps the counter-propagating edges and that the defect structure is governed by $G$-crossed braided tensor categories.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference figures illustrating the physical setup (FQH edges coupled by superconductors), the mapping of Wilson operators, and the superconducting segment acting as a charge-conjugation defect line.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for a generalized zero mode construction, detailing the mapping from bulk operators, and then applying this framework to specific, highly correlated FQH states (Laughlin, MR, RR) to derive concrete algebraic structures and measurable signatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study zero modes bound to charge-conjugation defects in fractional quantum Hall-superconductor (FQH-SC) heterostructures built from Abelian and non-Abelian FQH states. Although the associated defects are well described by $G$-crossed braided tensor categories, a local operator description of their zero modes is not generally known. We generalize the bulk-to-boundary construction of localized zero mode operators to FQH-SCs with non-Abelian parent topological orders, mapping bulk Wilson operators in the folded theory to localized zero mode operators in the one-dimensional FQH-SC theory. This construction distinguishes zero mode anyons from the local zero mode operators that act on the defect Hilbert space and yields a generalized zero mode algebra. It also determines the fusion-channel dependence of tunneling: tunneling of zero mode operators probes bulk monodromy, while direct zero mode anyon transfer probes $G$-crossed associativity data. Applying this framework, we recover the $\mathbb{Z}_{2m}$ parafermion zero mode structure of fermionic Laughlin FQH-SCs at filling $ν=1/m$, obtain a $\mathbb{Z}_2^{(n)}\times\mathbb{Z}_N$ structure for generalized Moore-Read states at filling $ν=1/m$, with $N=2m$ for fermionic states and $N=m$ for bosonic states, and find a $\mathrm{Fib}\times\mathbb{Z}_N$ structure for the $k=3$ Read-Rezayi state at filling $ν=3/(3M+2)$, with $N=2(3M+2)$ for fermionic states and $N=(3M+2)/2$ for bosonic states. In the Read-Rezayi case, zero mode operator tunneling and direct zero mode anyon transfer yield distinct universal amplitude ratios between the neutral fusion channels, providing a characteristic signature of the underlying Fibonacci defect structure.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09747v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates extending the complex scalar algebra of quantum mechanics to a minimal bicomplex algebra. It establishes that this extension introduces a distinct, ideal-valued sector alongside the standard complex sector. The work is purely algebraic, suggesting a mathematically richer framework for quantum theory while leaving the physical interpretation of the new sector open.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the exploration of alternative scalar algebras directly challenges the foundational mathematical assumptions underpinning quantum mechanics, which is a core theoretical concern for quantum physicists.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper questions whether the complex numbers ($\mathbb{C}$) used as the scalar algebra in standard quantum mechanics are fundamentally necessary, or if they merely form an embedded sector within a larger, commutative, and associative scalar algebra.

**Main result.** The minimal extension satisfying these algebraic constraints is the bicomplex algebra, which introduces a distinct, ideal-valued sector alongside the standard complex sector.

**Method.** The authors use abstract algebra to analyze the required structure, imposing conditions of commutativity, associativity, and the existence of a proper ideal sector.

**Model / system.** The model is the scalar algebra itself, which is extended from $\mathbb{C}$ to the bicomplex algebra $\mathbb{B}$. This extension is analyzed by decomposing $\mathbb{B}$ into orthogonal idempotent ideals.

**Key observables.** Ideal-valued eigenvalues, algebraic phase relations between sectors, and the structure of entangled states within the extended algebra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The minimal dimension of the extension is four real dimensions, realized by the bicomplex algebra.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the complex sector must remain intact while admitting a stable, complex-like ideal sector, leading to the bicomplex structure.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a multiplication table (Table 1) for the bicomplex algebra in a basis adapted to an idempotent element, which helps define the algebraic structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the algebraic necessity of the extension, deriving the bicomplex structure, analyzing its two distinct decompositions (canonical vs. physical), and finally examining how quantum concepts like state space and entanglement map onto this dual scalar architecture.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Standard quantum mechanics takes the complex numbers as its scalar algebra. We ask whether this complex structure is algebraically fundamental or may instead form a distinguished embedded sector of a larger commutative and associative scalar algebra. Requiring the ordinary complex sector to remain intact while admitting an additional stable complex-like analytic sector turns the latter into a proper ideal, makes zero divisors unavoidable in finite dimension, and implies a lower bound of four real dimensions. The bicomplex algebra realizes this minimum, and under an additional compatibility assumption the four-dimensional realization is unique up to isomorphism. The resulting algebra exhibits two distinct decompositions: the canonical decomposition into orthogonal idempotent ideals and a decomposition adapted to the embedded complex sector and the additional ideal-valued sector. The ordinary complex scalars therefore lie diagonally across the canonical idempotent decomposition. The ideal sector supports Taylor series, oscillatory exponentials, Fourier-type kernels, and Schrödinger-type expressions, and carries a formal ideal-valued eigenvalue structure. Restricting the Born rule to the embedded complex sector recovers its standard form. Whether this dual scalar architecture has physical significance beyond the algebraic framework remains an open question.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an automated technique to simplify fault-tolerant quantum circuits by performing fault-equivalent rewrites. By systematically searching for reductions, the authors significantly decrease resource requirements (like CNOT gates) and improve the logical error rate for codes like the Steane code. This work provides a powerful, scalable tool for designing more efficient quantum hardware protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of automated, systematic tools for circuit optimization is crucial for scaling quantum computation, providing concrete blueprints for hardware implementation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for an automated method to reduce the resource cost and improve the logical performance of fault-tolerant quantum circuits.

**Main result.** The automated rewriting method successfully reduced the required ancilla preparations and CNOT gates for syndrome extraction, leading to a demonstrated logical error rate reduction of up to 23% for the [[7, 1, 3]] code.

**Method.** The core method is an automated search procedure utilizing fault-equivalent rewrites, which applies non-reducing enabling rules to expose Bell-pair reductions while preserving fault-tolerance.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on fault-tolerant quantum computation, specifically optimizing syndrome-extraction gadgets for Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECCs) like the [[7, 1, 3]] Steane code.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate, number of ancilla preparations, number of CNOT gates, and circuit depth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical two-qubit error rate ($p$), and noise models including dephasing and depolarizing idle noise.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The search procedure assumes that the composite reduction matcher returns finitely many matches, and performance evaluation relies on computationally expensive Monte Carlo simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare logical error rates versus physical error rate ($p$) or number of cycles ($N$) for various syndrome-extraction protocols, showing performance improvements for the optimized circuits.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the automated search framework using fault-equivalent rewrites, applies this to specific QEC codes (Shor/Steane), and evaluates the resulting circuits by comparing resource counts and simulating logical error rates under various noise models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an automated method for reducing fault-tolerant circuits through fault-equivalent rewrites. Starting from a known fault-tolerant circuit, the search applies non-reducing enabling rules to expose Bell-pair reductions, each of which removes an ancilla preparation and a CNOT gate. Because every search transition preserves fault equivalence, the resulting circuits inherit the fault-tolerance properties of the input circuit. Candidate circuits are evaluated using circuit-level Monte Carlo simulation. For Shor-style syndrome extraction with the $[[7,1,3]]$ code, our method reduces one syndrome-measurement round from 30 to 18 ancilla preparations and from 54 to 42 CNOT gates. At a physical two-qubit error rate of $p = 10^{-3}$, the optimized circuit lowers the logical error rate by approximately 21% for both logical basis states. The reduction ranges from 13% to 23% over a range of $p$ spanning two orders of magnitude. We also apply the method to Steane-based dynamic syndrome extraction constructed from Goto's verified logical-$\lvert 0 \rangle$ preparation, in which the verification qubit serves as a flag. The search selects a circuit using four ancillas and 14 CNOT gates, matching the resource counts of the previous design but with an improved CNOT depth. Under depolarizing idle noise at $3p/10$ the selected circuit lowers the logical error rate by approximately 15% for both logical basis states, and by approximately $10%$ at $p/10$. These results demonstrate that automated fault-equivalent rewriting can identify circuits with lower resource costs or improved logical performance without separately verifying the fault tolerance of every candidate.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a modular benchmarking framework to systematically test optimization strategies for parameterized quantum circuits. By decoupling gradient estimation from parameter updates, the authors evaluate performance across diverse tasks like chemistry and machine learning. The work is vital for understanding how resource constraints and algorithmic choices affect practical quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, resource-aware framework for comparing optimization algorithms in the NISQ era, which is crucial for developing practical quantum machine learning and simulation protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Training parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) is challenging due to the need to balance optimization progress against limited evaluation budgets and the inherent statistical uncertainty of near-term quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The study benchmarks various optimization strategies, showing that performance is highly dependent on the specific workload, resource constraints, and the modular separation of gradient estimation from parameter updates.

**Method.** The authors developed a modular benchmarking framework that independently evaluates quantum search-direction estimators (e.g., OCP, finite-difference) and classical parameter-update rules (e.g., Adam, SGD).

**Model / system.** The benchmarking is applied across four distinct quantum workloads: QAOA for MaxCut, QCNN for Iris/MNIST classification, and VQE for molecular hydrogen ($	ext{H}_2$).

**Key observables.** Solution quality (e.g., Test Accuracy, Energy Error), and resource costs (Objective Evaluations, Logical Circuits, Shots).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite-shot simulation (256 shots/circuit), 156-qubit processor, and various hyperparameter settings for optimizers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The hardware runs are presented as targeted case studies and do not establish a universal optimizer ranking or isolate the causal effect of device noise.

**Figures summary.** Figures provide an overview of the modular optimization loop across the four workloads, comparing solution quality against quantum-evaluation cost.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews background, defines component-level comparisons, details the specific workloads/protocols, presents results comparing various optimizers, and concludes with a discussion of limitations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Training parameterized quantum circuits requires balancing optimization progress with limited evaluation budgets and the statistical uncertainty of near-term quantum hardware. We present a modular benchmarking framework that separates quantum search-direction estimation from classical parameter-update rules, allowing their interactions and sensitivities to be examined independently. We evaluate a suite of gradient-based, stochastic, and derivative-free optimizers across combinatorial optimization using the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA), supervised quantum machine learning using Iris classification and a quantum convolutional neural network (QCNN) for binary MNIST classification, and quantum chemistry using the variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) for molecular hydrogen. By separating objective evaluations from sample-circuit costs under finite-shot simulation and physical execution on a 156-qubit processor, we compare optimizer behavior across workloads with different parameter counts and measurement requirements. Four selected simulator and hardware case studies report terminal and best observed objectives separately, alongside post-training classifier accuracies. The comparisons include selected MaxCut and hydrogen trajectories and illustrate workload-dependent objective changes and evaluation costs, while the simulator benchmark compares both peak and mean performance across three seeds. The hardware runs do not establish an optimizer ranking or isolate a causal effect of device noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a method to construct quantum error-correcting codes capable of implementing a non-Clifford CCZ gate using physical T/T⁽ gates. By combining projective geometry with SAT solving, the authors build codes of increasing length while proving a lower bound on the minimum required code length. This advances the theoretical understanding of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the feasibility of implementing universal quantum gates (like CCZ) in fault-tolerant quantum computation using specific hardware constraints (transversal gates), which is central to quantum computing theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper focuses on constructing Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) quantum error-correcting codes that can implement a non-Clifford logical CCZ gate using transversal physical T/T⁽ gates.

**Main result.** The authors successfully construct such codes for various block lengths (e.g., $n=48$ to $n=496$) and prove that no such code exists for lengths below $n=39$.

**Method.** The construction combines projective geometry to define the stabilizer structure, uses the constraints imposed by the target CCZ gate (leading to Campbell-Howard conditions), and solves the resulting compatibility search as a Boolean Satisfiability (SAT) problem.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum error correction using CSS codes, where the logical operations are realized via transversal physical T/T⁽ gates. The underlying mathematical structure is defined using binary projective geometry $	ext{PG}(s-1, 2)$.

**Key observables.** Code distance parameters ($d_X, d_Z$), the weight of codewords, and the existence of a compatible logical operator.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Block length $n$ (tested from 48 to 496, with a gap $39 \le n \le 46$ remaining open), and the required distance $d_Z \ge 3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the code structure can be derived from projective geometry, and the search for compatible operators is encoded as a SAT problem.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but mention references to equations (6), (10), and a general proof structure for Theorem 4.5.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the problem using projective geometry to establish $d_Z \ge 3$. It then translates the physical constraints into algebraic conditions (CH conditions), which are solved using SAT solvers to find compatible codes. Finally, it systematically proves non-existence for small lengths ($n < 39$) through exhaustive algebraic exclusion.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct CSS codes with three logical qubits whose logical CCZ gate is implemented by transversal physical $T/T^{\dagger}$ gates and whose distance is at least $3$. These codes start with $8$-divisible $X$-stabilizers defined by projective geometry, which guarantees $d_Z\geq 3$. A SAT solver then finds three $X$-logical operators compatible with the transversal CCZ. We build codes at thirteen block lengths from $n=48$ to $n=496$, with $d_Z=3$ or $4$, and illustrate the method with the $[[48,3,3]]$ code $Q_{48}$. Additionally, we prove that no CSS code with three logical qubits, $Z$-distance at least $3$, and a quasi-transversal CCZ gate (transversal $T$ with a diagonal Clifford correction) exists below $n=39$. The $[[47,3,3]]$ code of Jacinto et al. serves as an upper bound, leaving $39\leq n\leq46$ open.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper creates a novel, unified metric to help organizations prioritize post-quantum cryptographic migration by combining classical data breach risks with future quantum computing threats. It mathematically proves how these two distinct risks interact using a competing-risks model. The resulting 'Cost of Delay' score provides an optimal, actionable ordering for security investments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not physics-focused, the rigorous mathematical modeling of competing failure processes and the use of survival analysis techniques are highly relevant to open quantum systems modeling where multiple decoherence or failure channels compete.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the lack of a common metric for prioritizing data migration to post-quantum cryptography, balancing immediate classical security risks against future quantum threats.

**Main result.** It develops a unified 'Cost of Delay' metric that successfully couples classical and quantum risks, demonstrating that the quantum threat can significantly reorder asset prioritization compared to classical-only assessments.

**Method.** The authors model both risks as a 'cost of delay' in currency per year, using a competing-risks framework that combines an annualized classical loss expectancy with a quantum loss rate derived from an arrival law.

**Model / system.** The model is an abstract risk assessment framework applied to digital assets, using parameters like data value, turnover, classical hazard rates, and a Weibull-fitted arrival law for a Cryptographically Relevant Quantum Computer (CRQC).

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the total Cost of Delay (CoD), which dictates the optimal sequence for security upgrades.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the classical hazard rate ($\lambda$), data value ($V$), data lifetime ($L$), and the quantum arrival rate derived from expert anchors.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Major assumptions include that parameters are assumed from public documentation rather than elicited from practitioners, and that the model only covers confidentiality risk.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the race between classical and quantum attackers over time, show the dominance threshold ($\lambda^*$) where quantum risk takes over, and demonstrate the quantum term's ability to reorder assets beyond input uncertainty.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the unified CoD metric, proves mathematical propositions regarding its structure, validates the construction using simulations, and applies the model to several public system case studies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Organisations deciding which assets to migrate to post-quantum cryptography first, and how that work competes with a backlog of classical findings, lack a common unit: post-quantum scores are dimensionless and quantum-only, while classical risk is annualised loss. We express both threats as a cost of delay in currency per year on the same asset. The quantum term is the rate at which deferring migration commits irreversible harvest-now-decrypt-later loss, computed from required confidentiality duration, recordable traffic share and data turnover, using a quantum-arrival law fitted in closed form to published expert anchors. A competing-risks factor couples the two terms so the same loss is not counted twice. We prove that the construction is not a weighted sum of a classical and a quantum score, give a dominance threshold on the classical hazard that is independent of asset value, and show that the induced order is optimal for sequencing work under constant loss rates. We verify the implementation by simulation, adaptive quadrature and a second implementation. On four public systems with parameters assumed from public documentation, the quantum term reorders the asset list beyond input uncertainty for one system only (signal-to-noise 1.36 against 0.14-0.46), moves specific long-lived, quiet, recordable assets decisively, and otherwise changes magnitudes. Asset value and turnover explain 73-91% of the uncertainty in the quantum term; the arrival date explains 3-17%. The case studies are illustrative and are not validated against outcomes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper compares two open-source quantum network simulators, QuISP and SeQUeNCe, by cross-validating basic tasks like entanglement generation and purification. The authors find that while the simulators disagree on the time taken for these tasks, they correctly predict the final resource fidelity under the same noise conditions. This work establishes a vital benchmark for improving the reliability of quantum network simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous benchmarking methodology for quantum network simulators, which is crucial for developing reliable protocols for future quantum internet architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To ensure the consistency and accuracy of simulation outputs when modeling quantum network tasks across different open-source simulators.

**Main result.** The simulators show quantitative differences in task completion time due to connection models, but they agree on the final fidelity of distributed resources under identical error parameters.

**Method.** A comparative analysis (cross-validation) was performed by simulating fundamental quantum networking primitives like entanglement generation, swapping, and purification using two distinct simulators.

**Model / system.** The study models quantum networks, which utilize photonic qubits and quantum memories to distribute entanglement over long distances, simulating first-generation (1G) repeater protocols.

**Key observables.** Latency of resource distribution (time required for tasks) and the fidelity of the distributed entangled resources.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error parameters (e.g., Pauli noise, qubit loss), connection model assumptions, and classical communication timing.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison assumes that the core physics of resource fidelity is correctly modeled, even if the timing mechanics differ between simulators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the three core processes: link-level entanglement generation (MIM), entanglement swapping at a repeater node, and entanglement purification.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a comparative framework by detailing the simulation of basic quantum networking tasks, comparing the results from QuISP and SeQUeNCe, and concluding with a methodology for future cross-validation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a comparative analysis of two open-source quantum network simulators, QuISP and SeQUeNCe, focusing on cross-validation of basic networking tasks to ensure consistency and accuracy in simulation outputs. Although both simulators are designed to study quantum network management policies and their dynamics under complex noise models, their differing underlying assumptions can lead to variations in simulation results. We highlight the discrepancies in how the two simulators handle connections, internal network node processing time, and classical communication, resulting in significant quantitative and qualitative differences in the time required to perform basic network tasks such as entanglement generation, purification, and swapping. We simulate common scenarios between the simulators to compare both the latency of resource distribution and the fidelity of the distributed resources. Our findings indicate that while the simulators differ in the time required to complete network tasks---a constant factor difference attributable to their respective connection models---they agree on the fidelity of the distributed resources under identical error parameters. This work demonstrates a crucial first step towards enhancing the reliability and reproducibility of quantum network simulations, as well as leading to full protocol development. Furthermore, our benchmarking methodology establishes a foundational set of tasks for the cross-validation of simulators to study future quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper studies the anisotropic three-state Potts model on a square lattice to determine how spatial anisotropy affects its critical behavior. Using Monte Carlo simulations and finite-size scaling, the authors show that while anisotropy alters the system's effective geometry, the underlying bulk critical universality class remains robustly that of the isotropic three-state Potts model.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed theoretical understanding of how lattice geometry and anisotropy manifest at criticality, offering a quantitative framework (effective-geometry rescaling) to separate universal physics from non-universal boundary effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate whether spatial anisotropy in the two-dimensional three-state Potts ferromagnet modifies the bulk critical universality class or primarily affects the finite-size critical geometry.

**Main result.** The study concludes that spatial anisotropy changes the effective critical geometry (e.g., directional correlation ratios) but does not alter the bulk critical universality class, which remains consistent with the 2D three-state Potts model.

**Method.** The research employs Wolff single-cluster Monte Carlo simulations and finite-size scaling analysis, supplemented by the analysis of Fisher zeros and directional correlation ratios.

**Model / system.** The system is an anisotropic three-state Potts ferromagnet defined on a square lattice with Hamiltonian $H = -\sum_{\langle ij angle_x} J_x \delta_{\sigma_i, \sigma_j} - \sum_{\langle ij angle_y} J_y \delta_{\sigma_i, \sigma_j}$. The anisotropy is controlled by the ratio $\lambda = J_y/J_x$.

**Key observables.** Overall correlation ratio ($R$), directional correlation ratios ($R_x, R_y$), correlation-length exponent ($
u$), specific-heat exponent ($\alpha$), and the effective aspect ratio ($ho_e$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anisotropy ratio $\lambda$ is varied, with specific studies conducted for $\lambda = 0.5, 0.75, 	ext{ and } 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the effective geometry rescaling can account for directional splitting in finite-size observables, and that the effective aspect ratio can be determined from critical FK wrapping probabilities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram, finite-size scaling results for correlation ratios, and the comparison of directional ratios ($R_x, R_y$) showing how geometric compensation restores directional equivalence.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the critical behavior using multiple diagnostic tools (FSS, Fisher zeros, directional ratios) to distinguish between universal bulk properties and non-universal geometric effects induced by anisotropy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the two-dimensional anisotropic three-state Potts ferromagnet on the square lattice using Wolff single-cluster Monte Carlo simulations and finite-size scaling. For coupling ratios $λ=J_y/J_x=0.5$, $0.75$, and $1$, finite-size scaling of the correlation ratio yields critical behavior consistent with the two-dimensional three-state Potts universality class. The scaling of the leading Fisher zeros gives a correlation-length exponent consistent with $ν=5/6$, while their cumulative density is consistent with the expected specific-heat exponent $α=1/3$. We further characterize the anisotropy at criticality using directional correlation ratios $R_x$ and $R_y$ together with directional FK wrapping probabilities. For $λ=0.5$ on a physically square lattice, $R_x$ and $R_y$ approach distinct critical values while yielding a common correlation-length exponent. From the wrapping probabilities, we independently determine an effective aspect ratio $ρ_e^\square=0.6413(5)$, in close agreement with the theoretical value $ρ_e^{\square,\mathrm{th}}\simeq0.64150030$ obtained from the isoradial representation. Using the theoretical value to set the physical aspect ratio restores directional equivalence, $R_x\simeq R_y$, and brings the overall correlation ratio toward the isotropic-square reference. The results show that spatial anisotropy changes the effective critical geometry without altering the bulk three-state Potts universality class.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a comprehensive Landau theory framework for non-relativistic magnetism, generalizing previous work to handle complex coplanar and non-coplanar magnetic orders. By constructing symmetry-allowed free energies, the authors provide a direct, rigorous link between Landau theory and the spin space group approach. This allows for detailed predictions of magnetic observables across various crystal symmetries.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the rigorous application of symmetry constraints (group theory) to derive effective Hamiltonians and predict phase transitions is a powerful general technique applicable to any strongly correlated system where symmetry breaking is key.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops phenomenological Landau theories to describe magnetic phase transitions in non-relativistic magnetic systems, extending the analysis from simple collinear orders to complex coplanar and non-coplanar arrangements.

**Main result.** The theory predicts two main outcomes: a collapse to a lower-symmetry collinear phase, or a genuinely orthogonal coplanar/non-coplanar order that directly links the Landau theory framework to the spin space group classification.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing symmetry-allowed free energies up to quartic order using group theory, analyzing how the free energy depends on the dimensionality of the order parameter's irreducible representation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on non-relativistic magnetic systems exhibiting zero-wavevector (k=0) magnetic orders, treating the magnetic state via order parameters transforming according to irreducible representations of the crystallographic point group.

**Key observables.** Net magnetization, spin conductivity, multipolar moments, and piezomagnetism.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero-wavevector (k=0) magnetic orders; tetragonal, trigonal/hexagonal, and cubic symmetries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system is near a continuous phase transition, allowing for a low-order polynomial expansion of the free energy, and focuses on single irreducible representations of the point group.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not describe specific figures, but mention the use of symmetry-adapted tensors (Table XI) to constrain the free energy expansion terms.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured logically, starting with the general Landau problems and epikernels, moving to multi-dimensional Landau theories, detailing the orthogonal orders, providing specific examples (Kagomäe, pyrochlore), calculating observables, and finally connecting to related physical systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work we study phenomenological Landau theories of non-relativistic magnetic systems, generalizing work on the collinear case to coplanar and non-coplanar orders. Focusing on zero-wavevector ($\boldsymbol{k}=0$) magnetic orders described by a single irreducible representation of the crystallographic point group, we construct the symmetry-allowed free energies to quartic order for all possible multi-dimensional irreducible ordering channels. We find distinct Landau theories for tetragonal, trigonal/hexagonal, and cubic symmetry. Similar to the case of spin-nematic fluids, we find two natural outcomes: a collapse to a lower-symmetry collinear phase or a genuinely coplanar or non-coplanar order where the order parameters form a mutually orthogonal set in spin space -- reminiscent of the $A$ and $B$ phases in ${}^3$He. We show in general that this symmetry-broken phase realizes the spin space group associated with the spatial representation of the order parameter sharing the same parent space group as the paramagnetic phase. This provides a direct link between the Landau and spin space group approaches. We calculate key observables using this Landau approach, including net magnetization, spin conductivity, multipolar moments, and piezomagnetism, connecting our results to constraints from the underlying spin space group. Examples of coplanar and non-coplanar magnetic orders for each case are worked out in detail. Finally, we discuss the relationship of the lower-symmetry collinear phases to the usual collinear spin groups, as well as connections to atomic altermagnetism, superfluid ${}^3$He and electronic nematic phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses XAS and RIXS spectroscopy to characterize the electronic structure and magnetic excitations in the trilayer nickelate $	ext{Pr}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10}$. By analyzing the magnetic response and fitting to spin-wave models, the authors determine that the magnetic interactions are governed by a balanced interplay between in-plane and interlayer couplings, establishing the multilayer architecture as the primary determinant of magnetic behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed mapping of magnetic interactions using spectroscopic probes like RIXS provides quantitative insight into the underlying quantum magnetic Hamiltonian, which is crucial for understanding strongly correlated electron behavior in cuprate/nickelate superconductors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand how the multilayer nickelate architecture controls electronic and magnetic interactions in $	ext{Pr}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10}$, particularly how rare-earth substitution affects these magnetic couplings.

**Main result.** The magnetic interactions are governed by a balanced network of significant in-plane and interlayer couplings, suggesting the multilayer structure, rather than rare-earth substitution alone, is the key control knob.

**Method.** The research combines advanced experimental techniques (XAS and polarization-dependent RIXS) with theoretical modeling (Spin-Wave Analysis and fitting to various Hamiltonian models).

**Model / system.** The system is the trilayer Ruddlesden-Popper nickelate $	ext{Pr}_4	ext{Ni}_3	ext{O}_{10}$. The analysis models the magnetic interactions using effective exchange couplings ($J_1, J_2, J_3, J_z$) within a spin-wave framework.

**Key observables.** Dispersive magnetic excitations, orbital hybridization channels (planar vs. out-of-plane), and effective exchange couplings ($J_1, J_2, J_3, J_z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exchange couplings are found to be in the $\sim 10-16 	ext{ meV}$ range, indicating a balanced magnetic network.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The conventional Linear Spin-Wave Theory (LSWT) is favored as the most direct quantitative description, although comparisons with entangled-dimer and stripe-dimer models were made.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure and effective magnetic interaction model, while RIXS maps show the momentum and energy dependence of the magnetic response.

**Paper structure.** The paper first uses XAS/RIXS to map the multiorbital electronic structure and anisotropy. It then uses momentum-resolved RIXS to identify dispersive magnetic excitations softening toward the SDW wave vector. Finally, it employs spin-wave analysis and fitting to various Hamiltonian models to quantify the exchange couplings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rare-earth substitution strongly modifies the structure, metallicity, and superconducting behavior of Ruddlesden-Popper (RP) nickelates, but its impact on their magnetic interactions remains unclear. Here, we combine x-ray absorption spectroscopy (XAS) and resonant inelastic x-ray scattering (RIXS) to resolve the multiorbital electronic structure and magnetic excitations of Pr$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10}$. The absence of an overlapping rare-earth resonance provides direct access to the Ni $L_3$ edge, while polarization-dependent measurements at the Ni $L$- and O $K$-edges resolve distinct planar and out-of-plane Ni-O hybridization channels. Momentum-resolved RIXS reveals dispersive magnetic excitations that soften toward the incommensurate spin-density-wave wave vector. An effective spin-wave analysis yields a sizable intra-trilayer exchange coupling, comparable to the leading in-plane interactions and robust against rare-earth substitution. Our results identify the multilayer architecture, rather than rare-earth substitution alone, as a key control knob of the magnetic interactions in these materials.

</details>

**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09947v1>

**Summary.** This paper provides a deep theoretical and experimental analysis of Quantum Data Reuploaders (QDRs), characterizing their achievable frequency spectrum using Fourier analysis. It derives capacity bounds based on this spectrum size and compares QDR performance against classical MLPs on various function approximation and classification tasks. The findings highlight that while QDRs have a theoretically structured capacity, their practical performance is highly sensitive to input scaling and dimensionality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools (Fourier analysis, sumsets) to quantify the limitations of quantum circuit depth in approximating classical functions, which is crucial for understanding the practical utility of variational quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the expressive power and capacity of Quantum Data Reuploaders (QDRs), a specific architecture for Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs). It aims to characterize how the structure of the quantum circuit limits the functions it can approximate.

**Main result.** The accessible frequency spectrum of QDRs grows polynomially with the reuploading depth $R$, leading to a capacity bound proportional to $\sqrt{|KR|/n}$. Experimentally, QDRs perform well on certain tasks but are limited in multivariate regression compared to MLPs.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on Fourier theory, mapping the quantum circuit output to a finite Fourier series whose frequency set $KR$ is characterized using sumsets and word length metrics on an integer lattice.

**Model / system.** The model is the QDR architecture, $U(	heta, x) = W_{R+1}V(x)W_R(	heta) \cdots W_1$, where $V(x)$ encodes classical data $x$. The analysis is performed on the resulting output function $f_	heta(x)$, which is a finite Fourier series.

**Key observables.** Test Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) for regression tasks (e.g., $	anh(x^3/3)$) and classification accuracy for tasks like XOR and spirals.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The reuploading depth $R$, the input scale $\sigma$, the number of qubits $n$, and the number of trainable parameters $P$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the encoding $V(x)$ has a finite phase decomposition, and the theoretical bounds rely on the structure of the frequency set $KR$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the test RMSE of QDRs versus matched MLPs across various targets (ReLU, sigmoid, $	anh(x^3/3)$) and show decision boundaries for classification tasks.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical characterization of the frequency spectrum $KR$ using Fourier analysis. It then derives capacity bounds based on the size of this spectrum. Finally, it presents extensive experiments comparing QDR performance against MLPs on regression and classification tasks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Data reuploading---repeatedly encoding classical inputs at multiple layers of a parameterized quantum circuit---is a central mechanism for enhancing the expressivity of quantum neural networks (QNNs). Building on the Fourier-theoretic framework of Schuld et al.~\cite{schuld2021fourier}, we study the frequency spectrum of the general multivariate, $R$-reupload architecture (QDR). (i) We make explicit that the accessible frequencies form the set $K_R=S^{(R)}-S^{(R)}$, which equals the $R$-fold sumset $T^{(R)}$ of the difference set $T=S-S$ of the encoding spectrum, and we characterise it exactly: $K_R=\{k\in\mathcal L:\ \ell_T(k)\le R\}$, where $\mathcal L=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb Z}(T)$ is an integer lattice and $\ell_T$ is word length in $T$. (ii) We show that the \emph{directions} of the spectrum stabilise already at $R=1$ (the real span of $K_R$ equals that of $\mathcal L$), whereas the spectrum itself never saturates: the maximal frequency grows linearly in $R$ and the number of frequencies grows polynomially, as $Θ(R^{m})$ with $m=\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal L$. (iii) Using the spectrum size, we give a Rademacher-complexity bound on the capacity of QDRs of order $\sqrt{|K_R|/n}$. (iv) We report experiments on function approximation and classification with train/test splits, several seeds and parameter-matched classical baselines. The simulated spectra match the theory exactly. A single-qubit QDR beats a matched MLP on several smooth or periodic univariate targets and on the spiral task, but it is clearly worse on multivariate regression ($d\ge4$), where it is highly sensitive to the input-encoding scale; it beats the MLP only on equations with $d\le3$ inputs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces advanced machine-learned descriptor models designed to predict electronic ground states by embedding the physical constraints of Kohn-Sham DFT directly into the loss functions. By enforcing mathematical rules like orthonormality and manifold membership, the models achieve superior generalization and accuracy, significantly outperforming prior state-of-the-art methods in predicting molecular properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the integration of deep learning into quantum chemistry, providing mathematically rigorous methods to ensure that ML predictions respect fundamental physical laws, which is crucial for reliable simulations in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is accelerating the accurate prediction of electronic ground states for molecules and materials while ensuring the predictions remain physically valid and generalize well beyond the training data.

**Main result.** The proposed descriptor models, incorporating physical constraints like orthonormal loss (ON-Loss) and manifold projection (GROOT/ROCKET), significantly reduce energy and force errors (up to 99.8% reduction) compared to previous state-of-the-art methods.

**Method.** The authors develop and apply specialized machine learning architectures (GSMs) that are explicitly aligned with the governing equations of Kohn-Sham DFT by enforcing physical constraints during training.

**Model / system.** The system is the electronic ground state of molecules/materials, governed by the Kohn-Sham DFT equations. The ML models predict descriptors (like the density matrix or Hamiltonian) that must satisfy physical constraints, such as belonging to the Grassmann manifold.

**Key observables.** Energy (E) and Force (F) Mean Absolute Error (MAE); convergence speed (ERIC); and the ability to maintain accuracy below chemical accuracy ($\sim 0.8$ mHa).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison involves size extrapolation (QM9 to QM40) and testing on out-of-distribution molecules (QMugs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that by enforcing physical constraints derived from the underlying quantum mechanics (e.g., idempotency, orthonormality), ML models can overcome the limitations of standard supervised learning on electronic structure data.

**Figures summary.** The notes indicate comparisons across multiple tables showing MAE reductions for various models (Pbase, GROOTrot, ROCKET) across different generalization tests (size extrapolation, self-consistency fine-tuning).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the limitations of current ML potentials, proposes physically constrained descriptor models (GSMs) using novel loss functions (ON-Loss, GROOT, ROCKET), and validates these methods through rigorous testing on size extrapolation and self-consistency refinement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) excel at in-distribution tasks, accelerating drug and material development, yet they struggle to generalize out-of-distribution. We propose to push the cost-accuracy Pareto frontier by designing observable-agnostic electronic ground-state descriptor models (GSMs) with computational costs situated between MLIPs and Kohn-Sham density functional theory (KS-DFT). We align the learning objectives and architectures of GSMs with the governing equations of KS-DFT by enforcing physical constraints and removing optimization pressure on unphysical or irrelevant degrees of freedom. In our size-extrapolation experiments from QM9 to QM40, our combined contributions OrthoNormal-Loss (ON-Loss) and Grassmann Restricted Occupied-Orbital Training (GROOT) reach a 79.1% energy and 83.4% force mean absolute error (MAE) reduction over previous state-of-the-art density GSMs. For Hamiltonian GSMs, ON-Loss and Residual Optimal-gauge Conditioning-aware KS-Eq. Training (ROCKET) together reduce the energy and force MAEs of the strongest baseline by 99.8% and 95.9%, respectively. Using a self-consistency rejection criterion, we filter out extrapolation errors on QMugs, rejecting fewer than 0.4% of predictions while reaching an energy MAE of 0.07 mHa. Finally, we demonstrate the efficiency of label-free self-consistency fine-tuning, and transfer GSMs to reactive chemistry in Transition1x, reaching energy errors below chemical accuracy.

</details>

### [Quantum Approximation Complexity of Classical Optimization Problems](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09584v1)

**Authors:** Stuart Hadfield  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.09584v1>

At each level, the deterministic class APX, PTAS, or FPTAS is contained in its randomized and quantum counterparts. The deterministic boundaries are not drawn separately. Open markers A and B have deterministic approximation algorithms; filled markers C and D mark conditional quantum–classical ratio separations. Under NP ⊈BQP, Max-E3SAT and MaxCut share marker A in R-APX \ BQ-PTAS, while Planar Vertex Cover occupies marker B in R-PTAS \ BQ-FPTAS [4–6]. Marker C (CMO) lies in BQ-FPTAS \ R-APX if Factor /∈FBPP, and is even...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously defines quantum complexity classes for approximating solutions to classical optimization problems. It establishes a strict hierarchy among these quantum classes, demonstrating that quantum computation can yield provable worst-case improvements over classical algorithms for certain problems, provided that $	ext{NP} 
ot\subseteq 	ext{BQP}$.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO physics, the rigorous framework for quantifying computational advantage (quantum vs. classical) is foundational for understanding the limits of quantum simulation and computation in physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the quantum approximation complexity of classical optimization problems, aiming to determine the best solution quality achievable using quantum computation compared to classical methods.

**Main result.** Under the assumption that NP is not contained in BQP, the quantum complexity classes form a strict hierarchy (BQ-FPTAS $\subsetneq$ BQ-PTAS $\subsetneq$ BQ-APX), and quantum computation can provide worst-case improvements over classical guarantees for certain problems.

**Method.** The authors define quantum counterparts to classical approximation classes (BQ-APX, BQ-PTAS, BQ-FPTAS) and use conditional separations based on the hardness of problems like factoring and discrete logarithms to establish these theoretical gaps.

**Model / system.** The analysis is purely theoretical, focusing on computational complexity classes (APX, PTAS, etc.) and the computational power of quantum algorithms (BQP) applied to classical optimization problems.

**Key observables.** Approximation ratios ($\alpha, ho$), success probability (fixed at $\ge 2/3$), and the relationship between the expected score and the guaranteed output ratio.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity assumptions, notably $	ext{NP} 
ot\subseteq 	ext{BQP}$, and the specific approximation factors (e.g., one-half for DLog-Fit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The entire framework relies on the assumption that $	ext{NP} 
ot\subseteq 	ext{BQP}$, and the analysis for specific problems assumes the hardness of factoring or discrete logarithms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the inclusion relationships between various approximation classes for several example problems (A-D).

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the quantum approximation complexity classes, then establishes the hierarchy relationships, and finally proves conditional separations using specific number-theoretic problems (like factoring and discrete logarithms) to demonstrate quantum advantages.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical approximation complexity asks what solution quality can be guaranteed with polynomial-time computation. The classes APX, PTAS, and FPTAS distinguish a fixed approximation ratio, approximation to any fixed accuracy, and approximation schemes whose running time is also polynomial in inverse accuracy. Their randomized counterparts are R-APX, R-PTAS, and R-FPTAS. We define bounded-error quantum counterparts BQ-APX, BQ-PTAS, and BQ-FPTAS. Membership requires a uniform quantum algorithm that, on every input, returns a feasible classical solution achieving at least the claimed approximation ratio with probability at least 2/3. Scores (objective values) must be efficiently classically computable. Running time includes parameter selection, preparation, measurement, decoding, and repetition. Many quantum optimization methods are used heuristically, and high benchmark scores alone do not establish these guarantees. We further establish a conditional hierarchy for logarithmic, polynomial, and exponential approximation factors.   Assuming NP $\nsubseteq$ BQP, the quantum classes form a strict hierarchy. Problems based on prime factorization and discrete logarithms give conditional quantum-classical separations. Certified Maximum Order has an exact quantum algorithm, while any randomized polynomial-time algorithm guaranteeing at least an inverse-polynomial approximation ratio on every input would yield efficient factoring. Discrete-Logarithm Fitting has an exact quantum algorithm and a deterministic one-half approximation, but any fixed improvement over one half would give a randomized polynomial-time algorithm for the safe-prime discrete logarithm problem. Our results show, under explicit complexity assumptions, that quantum computation can improve worst-case approximation guarantees. A quantum-classical gap for common problems such as MaxCut or MaxSAT remains open.

</details>
