**Summary.** This paper introduces a Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) method to calculate the complex anharmonic vibrational spectra of polyynes. By projecting the massive vibrational Hamiltonian onto a small, sampled subspace, the authors overcome the exponential scaling problem. The results demonstrate that this quantum approach can accurately predict both vibrational energies and sensitive spectroscopic details, like infrared intensities.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the simulation of molecular vibrations, a core topic in AMO physics, by developing a quantum algorithm to circumvent the exponential scaling inherent in full diagonalization of the vibrational Hamiltonian.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is predicting anharmonic vibrational spectra, which is computationally infeasible due to the exponential growth of the Hilbert space with molecular size.

**Main result.** The sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) framework successfully computes vibrational energies and spectroscopic observables for polyynes in subspaces far smaller than the full theoretical space.

**Method.** The authors employ an end-to-end SQD framework combining quantum state preparation (using vib-LUCJ or vib-SqDRIFT ansatze), configuration recovery, and diagonalization projected onto a selected subspace.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on polyyne chains (C2H2 and C4H2) whose anharmonic vibrational Hamiltonian is modeled using a multi-mode expansion. The electronic structure calculations are based on DFT (B3LYP/def2-SVP).

**Key observables.** Vibrational energies, infrared spectra, and transition-dipole moments (which determine intensities and excited-state composition).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system size is quantified by the Hilbert space dimension, which can reach $\approx 5.5 \times 10^{11}$ for C4H2.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitations noted are the need for systematic optimization of sampling parameters and the fact that spectroscopic observables are more sensitive than ground-state energies.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the modal representation and qubit encoding. Other figures compare SQD results for transition dipole moments ($0 \to m$ transitions) against exact diagonalization for C2H2 and C4H2.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the computational bottleneck of anharmonic spectra. It then details the SQD framework, comparing two sampling strategies (vib-LUCJ and vib-SqDRIFT) to calculate energies and, critically, transition-dipole moments, concluding with resource estimates for quantum implementation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Predicting anharmonic vibrational spectra is a complex computational task, which becomes quickly infeasible as molecular size and the number of vibrational basis functions increase, leading to an exponential growth of the underlying Hilbert space. Here, we employ a sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) framework to compute anharmonic vibrational energies and spectra. The approach combines quantum state preparation and sampling with configuration recovery, diagonalization of the Hamiltonian projected in a subspace of basis configurations, and calculation of transition-dipole moments to obtain both vibrational energies and infrared spectra. We investigate two approaches for sampling, namely, vib-LUCJ and vib-SqDRIFT and apply the method to study the polyyne molecules C$_2$H$_2$ and C$_4$H$_2$, reaching representations requiring up to 104 qubits. Beyond energies, transition-dipole calculations reveal that infrared intensities and excited-state composition continue to evolve with basis size through anharmonic mixing and intensity redistribution, even when low-energy eigenvalues appear converged. These results show that sample-based quantum subspace methods can capture spectroscopic information in vibrational spaces far beyond the regime of full diagonalization. Moreover, our simulation identify multi-state recovery and scalable projected-space treatment as central challenges for further scaling sample-based approaches.

</details>

### [Standard estimators cannot represent fault-tolerant workloads at measured error rates: evaluated, evidence-based uncertainty for quantum resource estimation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10490v1)

**Summary.** This paper introduces a robust framework to quantify the uncertainty in quantum resource estimates for fault-tolerant computing. By propagating evidence-based priors through multiple cost models, the authors demonstrate that current single-number estimates are misleading. The results show that using measured hardware error rates drastically increases the required resources and reveals significant structural disagreements between different estimation tools.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, uncertainty-aware methodology for estimating resource requirements, which is crucial for any theoretical work aiming to connect abstract quantum algorithms to realistic hardware constraints.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard estimates of quantum resources for fault-tolerant algorithms are reported as single numbers, failing to account for inherent hardware parameter uncertainties and disagreements among cost models.

**Main result.** Propagating measured error rates significantly widens resource estimates (e.g., 40x wider interval for RSA-2048) compared to optimistic conventions, and structural disagreements between cost models are substantial.

**Method.** The authors developed an open, tool-agnostic framework to propagate evidence-based priors over hardware parameters through multiple surface-code cost models, using advanced sensitivity analysis.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to superconducting two-dimensional nearest-neighbour surface-code architectures, evaluating workloads like RSA-2048, ECC-256, and AES-256.

**Key observables.** Physical-qubit count, resource intervals (e.g., 90% interval), and the fraction of parameter space an estimator can represent.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical two-qubit gate error rate (measured median $\approx 4 \times 10^{-3}$ vs. conventional $10^{-3}$), code distance, and model-specific constants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is limited to the superconducting platform due to available error-rate measurements; the framework relies on propagating priors rather than predicting future performance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dramatic widening of resource intervals due to measured error rates and the structural disagreement factors between different cost estimation models.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the uncertainty framework, applies it to several cryptographic and simulation workloads on superconducting hardware, and presents three coupled findings that undermine the single-number resource estimation convention.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Estimates of the quantum resources needed to run fault-tolerant algorithms are almost always reported as single numbers, even though they rest on uncertain hardware parameters and on cost models that disagree. We present an open, tool-agnostic framework that propagates evidence-based priors over fault-tolerant hardware parameters through five surface-code cost models, adds correlated-input sensitivity analysis, and evaluates the resulting intervals with a pre-registered three-layer protocol. Applied to RSA-2048, ECC-256, AES-256, and a Hubbard-model simulation on a superconducting surface-code architecture, it produces three coupled findings that together undermine the single-number convention. Propagating the error rates hardware has actually demonstrated, whose median across five large-array devices is about 4 x 10^-3 rather than the conventional 10^-3, widens the 90% physical-qubit interval to roughly forty times and lifts its median about 4.6 times above the conventional point estimate. Against the same inputs, five independently structured cost models, including two third-party estimators (the Azure Quantum Resource Estimator and Qualtran) that agree with each other to within about ten percent, disagree by a stable factor of two, a structural uncertainty that no single tool reveals. Most consequentially, both third-party estimators exceed their representable envelope for 80 to 100 percent of the evidence-based parameter space, and the AES-256 gate count overflows one tool's counter outright, so these workloads cannot be represented at measured error rates at all. The optimistic convention conceals every one of these effects. The uncertainty machinery we use is standard. The contribution is its evidence-based application, the pre-registered evaluation, and the finding that standard tools break down precisely where hardware operates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the computational role of fixed anatomical wiring in the fly visual system by creating a connectome-only neural network model. By comparing the actual connectome to randomized versions, the authors show that the specific, measured connectivity is crucial for achieving high computational accuracy under realistic wiring constraints. This suggests that biological structure dictates efficient operating regimes for complex sensory processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biology, the use of graph theory, message passing, and comparing structured vs. random connectivity ensembles mirrors concepts in network science and disordered systems, which can inform models of complex quantum or classical computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is determining how much of the computational efficiency in the visual system is dictated solely by its measured synaptic wiring (connectome).

**Main result.** The biological connectome achieves higher accuracy than randomized ensembles at matched wiring cost, suggesting that the measured connectivity and eye geometry jointly set efficient operating points for visual computation.

**Method.** The authors built a 'connectome-only' message-passing model, fixing the anatomical graph and eye geometry while only learning synaptic gains and thresholds.

**Model / system.** The model uses the full connectome of the *Drosophila* visual system, taking visual input from an anatomically faithful eye model and passing it through the connectome graph to a Kenyon-cell-centered decoder.

**Key observables.** Task accuracy (color, shape, number discrimination), information propagation dynamics, and computational performance across different connectome ensembles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Synaptic gains, neuronal thresholds, and the wiring cost (proxied by synaptic distance and count).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes that the fixed anatomical wiring and eye geometry are the primary determinants of efficiency, and that performance differences reflect structural constraints rather than optimization differences.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the multi-step computational flow for number discrimination. Figure 2 compares various randomized connectome ensembles (e.g., unconstrained vs. biological) based on wiring constraints and associated statistics. Figure 3 compares task accuracy across these different connectome variants.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by modeling the visual system, details the connectome-only message-passing approach, tests performance on multiple vision tasks, and compares the biological connectome against randomized ensembles to draw conclusions about wiring efficiency.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the extent to which measured synaptic wiring determines computation remains a central challenge. Here, we couple the proofread adult Drosophila melanogaster connectome to an anatomically faithful model of its eye. Visual information is inputted in the eye model, then passed to the connectome, and finally read from a Kenyon-cell-centered linear decoder. This creates a connectome-only model in which the anatomical graph and eye geometry are fixed and only scalar synaptic gains and neuronal thresholds may be learned. The model supports multitask vision, including color discrimination, shape classification, and numerical discrimination that follows a ratio-dependent scaling characteristic of approximate number perception. To test whether precise connectivity is consequential under wiring economy, we compare the biological graph to randomized ensembles that increasingly preserve biological synaptic constraints. At matched wiring cost, the biological network consistently yields higher accuracy, whereas less constrained rewiring surpasses it at the cost of inflated wiring. These findings indicate that the measured connectivity and eye geometry jointly set efficient operating points for visual computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models LLM reasoning as pathfinding on a De Bruijn graph, proving that such structured reasoning is highly sample-efficient. Empirically, it shows that while frequent state reporting boosts accuracy, a moderate structure density is necessary to achieve robust performance. The findings suggest that the optimal design of complex AI systems requires balancing local precision with global structural robustness.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily focused on AI/ML, the rigorous mathematical modeling using graph theory (De Bruijn graphs) and the explicit analysis of information flow constraints ($c$) provide a novel, structured framework for understanding complex sequential computation, which has parallels in quantum circuit design or state evolution.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental relationship between pattern recognition and step-by-step reasoning capabilities within Large Language Models (LLMs). It seeks to characterize how the underlying structure of reasoning traces dictates the model's sample efficiency and robustness.

**Main result.** Step-by-step reasoning is highly sample-efficient if the reasoning traces exhibit a De Bruijn graph structure, suggesting that real-world data may possess this underlying mathematical structure. However, the optimal performance requires a balance between high state frequency (small $c$) for accuracy and low state frequency (large $c$) for robustness.

**Method.** The authors combine theoretical proofs on De Bruijn graph path coverage with extensive empirical testing, fine-tuning LLMs (Qwen series) on equation-solving tasks while manipulating context window sizes and introducing controlled perturbations.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Large Language Model (LLM) predicting the next token. The theoretical framework models the reasoning process as paths on a De Bruijn graph, where the context length $c$ determines the graph's structure.

**Key observables.** Final-Answer Accuracy, Derivation Accuracy, $P_{\min}/P_{	ext{tot}}$ ratio, and the model's performance under varying state emission intervals ($k$) and perturbation probabilities ($p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The context length $c$ (determining De Bruijn structure), the state emission interval $k$, and the perturbation probability $p$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that if the next token depends only on $c$ preceding tokens, the reasoning process can be modeled as a path on a De Bruijn DAG. It also assumes that the optimal regime balances accuracy and robustness.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the De Bruijn graph structure, showing that path coverage ratios decrease significantly for structured graphs. Other figures compare accuracy metrics (final vs. derivation) across different state emission intervals and perturbation levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from a theoretical characterization of reasoning as path traversal on a De Bruijn graph, establishing sample efficiency. It then moves to empirical validation using synthetic tasks (equations) to test the impact of state emission frequency and perturbations on accuracy and robustness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We argue that pattern recognition and step-by-step reasoning are two ends of a spectrum. A large language model (LLM) learns to reason step-by-step when data is structured such that the next token depends on a small amount of preceding context. Inference in LLMs resembles pattern recognition when the next token depends on a large amount of preceding context. If the next token depends on only the $c$ most recent tokens, reasoning traces are paths on a De Bruijn graph whose nodes are $c$-length contexts and edges are next-token transitions between contexts. The set of reasoning traces of a task forms a directed acyclic subgraph of the De Bruijn graph. An LLM that has learned all edges of this subgraph can compose them to solve longer, unseen tasks, i.e., it reasons step-by-step. We prove that the number of edges is vanishingly small compared to the number of reasoning traces. Empirically, the number of training samples a transformer needs is a power law in the number of edges, so learning to reason step-by-step is sample efficient. We can induce De Bruijn structure in any task by maintaining a ``state'' that makes future reasoning independent of the past. The frequency of states in the reasoning trace determines $c$. We show, by fine-tuning Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct to solve equations and answer questions about stories, that frequent states (small $c$) result in higher accuracy but greater fragility to perturbations at test time. LLMs trained with a large $c$ are only as good as models that perform pattern recognition without reasoning. A moderate density of states balances accuracy and robustness. We show that real-world data has De Bruijn structure: Qwen3-14B and Qwen3-32B retain over 75% of their accuracy on GSM8K, MATH-500 and GPQA-Diamond when attention is restricted to a sliding window less than 15% as long as the full reasoning trace.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the relationship between Wigner negativity and decoherence time for cubic Gaussian quantum states. It establishes a strict quadratic tradeoff, showing that high negativity inherently leads to rapid decoherence. The findings are crucial for understanding the practical limits of using nonclassical states as resources in quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource quantification in continuous-variable quantum information, showing fundamental physical limitations on how nonclassicality (negativity) can be sustained against environmental decoherence.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental tradeoff between achieving high Wigner negativity (a quantum resource) and maintaining a long decoherence time in specific quantum states.

**Main result.** For cubic Gaussian states, achieving large Wigner negativity ($\mathcal{N}$) necessarily results in a short decoherence time ($	au$), bounded by $	au \lesssim \mathcal{N}^{-2}$.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical derivations involving the Wigner function, characteristic functions, and the calculation of the Quadratic Coherence Scale ($	ext{C}_{2G,\gamma}$) to establish the mathematical bounds.

**Model / system.** The system is a single-mode continuous-variable quantum system described by annihilation/creation operators. The states analyzed are cubic Gaussian states, which are generated by applying a cubic gate ($\hat{\Gamma}_\gamma = \exp(-i\gamma\hat{q}^3)$) to an initial Gaussian state.

**Key observables.** Wigner negativity ($\mathcal{N}$), Decoherence time ($	au$), Quadratic Coherence Scale ($	ext{C}_{2G,\gamma}$), and the Mandel $Q$ parameter.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The cubic gate strength ($\gamma$), the squeezing parameter ($r$), and the negativity ($\mathcal{N}$) are key parameters governing the state properties.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the full six-parameter family of single-mode cubic Gaussian states, though the ideal cubic phase state is noted as unphysical.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Wigner function for cubic Gaussian states, showing the positive/negative regions based on the parameter $\lambda$, and plot the negativity as a function of $\lambda$ compared to Fock state negativities.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the system and key observables (Wigner function, negativity) to deriving the core tradeoff ($	au \propto \mathcal{N}^{-2}$), determining optimal state parameters, and finally comparing these optimal states against Fock states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Wigner negativity has been shown to be a resource in quantum computing, raising the question of how to efficiently produce states with a large Wigner negativity. Cubic Gaussian states, obtained when a cubic gate is applied to a Gaussian state, have been proposed as candidates for this purpose. We show that cubic Gaussian states with a large Wigner negativity $\mathcal N$ necessarily have a short decoherence time $τ$ that is bounded above by $\mathcal N^{-2}$. In other words, a large Wigner negativity comes at a cost: the decoherence time $τ$ of cubic Gaussian states decreases at least quadratically in the negativity $\mathcal N$. Maximizing $τ$ at fixed Wigner negativity $\mathcal N$, we show that this upper bound is reached by optimal cubic Gaussian states. They have the property that for large negativity, the optimal squeezing scales as $\ln\mathcal N$ and the optimal cubicity as $\mathcal N^{-1}$. Optimal cubic Gaussian states with large negativity can therefore be constructed with small cubicity, provided sufficient squeezing is applied. However, their decoherence time decreases with growing negativity. In addition, we show that their preparation is extremely sensitive to thermal noise. Finally, we compare, for each integer $n$, the optimal cubic Gaussian state with the $n$th Fock state that has the same negativity: despite their pronounced differences in photon number distribution and Wigner function, we show that they have similar, be it slightly larger, decoherence times and entanglement generating potential through a beam splitter.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a learning analytics framework to analyze detailed response logs from quantum circuit simulator tasks in an introductory course. By profiling task difficulty and mapping the observed answer space, the authors provide actionable evidence for course designers. This work demonstrates how process data, beyond simple pass/fail scores, can significantly improve quantum education pedagogy.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is educational, the rigorous analysis of systematic errors and solution space diversity in quantum circuit construction provides a model for analyzing complex, open-ended physical problem-solving in quantum contexts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the lack of actionable pedagogical insights derived from detailed response logs generated by interactive quantum circuit simulators used in introductory quantum computing education.

**Main result.** A learning analytics framework was developed that successfully identifies bottleneck tasks, broad solution spaces, and recurring systematic errors not visible from final scores alone, enabling concrete course redesign.

**Method.** The authors implemented a two-branch learning analytics pipeline analyzing submission logs across 21 circuit exercises, focusing on task-level diagnostics like first-attempt accuracy and answer-space profiling.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum circuits and quantum computing concepts, simulated via a drag-and-drop interface in the TIM learning environment. The analysis is performed on submission logs from an introductory course cohort.

**Key observables.** Task-level indicators include participation, completion rates, first-attempt accuracy, valid-attempt counts, and the observed answer-space (plain-text representation of submitted circuits).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis covers 492 learners and 47,533 valid submissions across 21 obligatory exercises.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The learner-level branch is an ongoing research extension, and the analysis is limited to the cohort data from one specific course run.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two-branch pipeline, and ASCII visualizations (e.g., Fig. 2, Fig. 8) show the common correct and incorrect constructions observed in the answer space.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the two-branch learning analytics framework (task-level focus), presents the analysis of 21 exercises using process and outcome difficulty metrics, and concludes by demonstrating how these insights support actionable course redesign.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interactive quantum circuit simulators are increasingly used to support introductory quantum computing education for learners from varied disciplinary backgrounds. These environments can also generate detailed response logs of submitted circuits, but such data are rarely transformed into evidence that instructors can use for course redesign and development.   We present a learning analytics framework for quantum circuit simulator tasks and report its task-level branch on anonymised logs from an introductory quantum computing course delivered in the TIM learning environment. The task branch characterises exercises through participation, completion, first-attempt accuracy, valid-attempt counts, sessionised active time, and observed answer-space reporting of common correct and incorrect constructions.   The dataset covers the 2024-2025 cohort of 492 learners, 47,533 valid submissions, and 21 obligatory quantum circuit exercises. The framework identifies bottleneck tasks, broad solution spaces, and recurring wrong-answer patterns that are not visible from final scores alone. Its most distinctive output is a plain-text representation of the observed answer space, designed to make open-ended circuit submissions directly interpretable to instructors. We show how response logs can reveal actionable task-design issues and support concrete course redesign. A learner-level branch is retained only as an ongoing research extension and is not a central empirical contribution of the present paper.

</details>

### [Vortex screening and the fate of the BKT transition with long-range couplings](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10171v1)

**Authors:** Guido Giachetti  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.10171v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.10171_figures/2610.10171_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Schematic representation of the renormalization procedure. Spin configurations before (left) and after (right) integrating out a vortex–antivortex pair, with positive and negative vortices marked in red and blue. The yellow surface symbolically represents the many-body vortex interaction. Integrating out the pair screens this interaction by decreasing the spin stiffness Jℓ.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2610.10171_figures/2610.10171_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. RG flows and phase structure. (a) Trajectories at σ = 1.85, colored according to their LR (teal), QLRO (ochre), or disordered (gray) basin. (b) Fixed-point locus as σ varies (pink): the LR-marginal endpoint of the Gaussian line, J = [2π(2 −σ)]−1, g = y = 0, for 7/4 &lt; σ &lt; 2, joins the finite-coupling branch for σ &lt; 7/4 at JBKT = 2/π. The nearby saddle flow is schematic. (c) Illustrative numerical RG phase diagram for J0 = 1/T, g0 = 10−4/T, and y0 = e−6/T , with the same color code.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the stability of the BKT phase in 2D XY models with long-range couplings using a real-space renormalization group approach. By analyzing vortex screening, the authors derive a critical condition ($\sigma > 7/4$) for the persistence of QLRO. The findings establish a detailed phase diagram, predicting a crossover to a Wilson-Fisher-like fixed point when the coupling decay is too slow.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous RG treatment of vortex screening in the presence of long-range interactions provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding phase transitions in 2D systems, connecting concepts from Coulomb gases to critical phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the stability of the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) phase, characterized by Quasi-Long-Range Order (QLRO), when the system is subjected to long-range interactions decaying as $r^{-2-\sigma}$.

**Main result.** A stable Gaussian QLRO phase is found to exist only for coupling decay exponents $\sigma > 7/4$; otherwise, the system flows towards a finite-coupling, Wilson-Fisher-like fixed point.

**Method.** The analysis employs a real-space renormalization-group (RG) treatment, incorporating the full many-body vortex interaction and perturbatively including spin-wave contributions.

**Model / system.** The model is the two-dimensional XY model with a ferromagnetic Hamiltonian featuring long-range couplings $J(r) \sim r^{-2-\sigma}$. The physics is analyzed by decomposing the field into smooth spin-wave and discrete vortex components.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the renormalization of the spin stiffness ($J$) and the vortex fugacity ($y$), which determine the stability of the QLRO phase.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical exponent $\sigma$ governing the decay of the long-range coupling, and the RG flow parameters $J_\ell, y_\ell, g_\ell$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis retains the full many-body vortex interaction and assumes that the vortex screening process preserves the Gaussian-average representation of the partition function.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the renormalization procedure, showing how integrating out vortex-antivortex pairs screens the interaction and renormalizes the spin stiffness $J_\ell$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by defining the model and the RG framework. It details the vortex screening mechanism, deriving the stability criterion ($\sigma > 7/4$). Finally, it analyzes the RG flow, predicting the existence and nature of a finite-coupling fixed point for $\sigma < 7/4$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The stability of the Berezinskii--Kosterlitz--Thouless phase against long-range interactions remains an open problem. We address this question in the two-dimensional XY model with couplings decaying as $r^{-2-σ}$, by developing a real-space renormalization-group treatment near the Gaussian manifold of quasi-long-range ordered (QLRO) states. We retain the full many-body vortex interaction and show that, at leading order in the fugacity, screening by small vortex--antivortex pairs preserves its Gaussian-average representation without an expansion in the long-range coupling. Including spin waves perturbatively then yields a local stability criterion: a stable QLRO interval exists only for $σ>7/4$. We also investigate a Wilson--Fisher-like fixed point at finite coupling for $σ<7/4$, for which the retained flow predicts $ν^{-1}\sim\sqrt{7/4-σ}$ as it merges with the Gaussian line.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates how a triad of trapped exciton-polariton condensates can deterministically generate and switch between three distinct topological vortex states. By tuning the pump power and using a control beam, the system achieves on-demand control over the global phase winding, which is unambiguously read out via momentum-space light emission. This establishes the condensate triad as a powerful, compact platform for reconfigurable chiral light sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it uses polariton condensates—a platform bridging quantum optics and condensed matter—to demonstrate robust, deterministic control over topological quantum numbers (OAM/chirality) in a solid-state system.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the deterministic generation and all-optical switching of topologically distinct vortex states in a triad of trapped exciton-polariton condensates.

**Main result.** The system establishes the condensate triad as a compact platform for reconfigurable chiral light generation, enabling on-demand orbital angular momentum control using a ternary logic basis.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements of momentum-space photoluminescence (PL) with theoretical modeling using coupled Stuart-Landau and Gross-Pitaevskii equations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a triad of trapped exciton-polariton condensates in a microcavity. The dynamics are modeled by coupled equations incorporating hopping/Josephson coupling and dissipative terms, reflecting the $C_{3v}$ symmetry.

**Key observables.** Momentum-space photoluminescence profiles, quantized phase winding ($\pm 2\pi$), and the ability to distinguish topological states without interferometry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Non-resonant pump power (controls coupling sign), control beam intensity, and the sign of the dissipative coupling ($D$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system can be effectively described by the coupled Stuart-Landau or Gross-Pitaevskii mean-field equations, and the control beam can deterministically break symmetry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup, spatial excitation patterns, and crucial momentum-space PL profiles comparing zero-winding (ferromagnetic) states against $\pm 2\pi$ winding states, demonstrating the unique signatures in K and K' points.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the basic coupled model (dyad/triad) to demonstrating the control over phase winding via pump tuning, culminating in the unique characterization of chirality and OAM control using momentum-space imaging.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the deterministic generation and all-optical switching of ternary topologically distinct vortex states in a triad of trapped exciton-polariton condensates with direct state readout from momentum-space emission distribution. Tuning the non-resonant pump power controls the sign of the dissipative coupling, selecting between a ferromagnetic in-phase state and two frustrated states with global phase winding. The zero-winding ferromagnetic state has unstructured far-field emission at low momenta and forms vortex-antivortex arrays at the Dirac points, reflecting the C_3v global symmetry. The two frustrated states of +-2pi winding can be activated on-demand by a control beam and produce different triangular emission patterns selectively localized at the K or K' points. Although all three states share identical real-space emission distributions, their momentum-space photoluminescence profiles distinguish them unambiguously, revealing the chirality without the need for interferometry. This enables on-demand orbital angular momentum control using a pump independent of resonant phase or OAM imprinting, establishing condensate triads as a compact platform for reconfigurable chiral light generation operating on a ternary logic basis.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates how to achieve programmable, field-free, and asymmetric magnetization switching in magnetic multilayers using Spin-Orbit Torque. The asymmetry is fundamentally linked to an internal effective magnetic field ($H_y$) that breaks system symmetry. By controlling this asymmetry based on the initial magnetization state, the authors successfully realize a reconfigurable spin logic device.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on spintronics, the concept of symmetry breaking via an internal effective field ($H_y$) to control non-linear switching behavior is analogous to controlling non-equilibrium dynamics in open quantum systems or engineered quantum potentials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to demonstrate and understand programmable, field-free, asymmetric spin-orbit torque (SOT)-induced magnetization switching for realizing reconfigurable spin logic devices.

**Main result.** Asymmetric switching is shown to originate from an in-plane effective field ($H_y$) that breaks system symmetry, enabling the realization of programmable spin logic by controlling the initial magnetization state.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements (SQUID, magnetotransport, pulsed current switching) with theoretical modeling using the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (LLG) equation.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an Fe/Mn2CoAl(MCA)/Pt multilayer heterostructure grown on GaAs(001). The dynamics are modeled using the LLG equation, incorporating SOT torques and effective in-plane anisotropy fields ($H_x, H_y$).

**Key observables.** Critical switching currents (different for opposite polarities), magnetization hysteresis loops, and the resulting Boolean logic output states ($R_{xy}$ vs. pulse number).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The asymmetry is governed by the internal effective field $H_y$, while the switching polarity is controlled by the initial magnetization direction of the IMA layer.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The asymmetry is attributed to intrinsic symmetry breaking by the magnetic field, ruling out Joule heating effects. The logic inputs are encoded via current pulses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the multilayer stack, M-H curves showing switching, symmetry schematics detailing the role of $H_y$, and finally, a truth table demonstrating the realization of multiple Boolean logic gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper first characterizes the material's in-plane magnetic anisotropy using PHE measurements. It then investigates SOT switching, establishing that the asymmetry is due to $H_y$. Finally, it leverages this tunable asymmetry across four initial magnetization states to implement a full set of programmable spin logic operations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin logic devices provide a promising route toward ultralow-power and nonvolatile information processing. In this work, we demonstrate field-free spin-orbit torque-induced asymmetric magnetization switching in in-plane anisotropy (IMA) and perpendicular magnetic anisotropy heterostructures, characterized by different critical switching currents under opposite current polarities. Combined experiments and macrospin simulations reveal that the asymmetric switching originates from an in-plane effective field Hy , which breaks the Myz mirror symmetry of the system. By controlling the magnetization direction of the IMA layer, both the switching polarity and bias direction can be tuned. Building on four distinct types of asymmetric switching behaviors, we realize a reconfigurable spin logic operation within a single Hall-bar device. These results clarify the physical origin of asymmetric magnetization switching and demonstrate an approach for realizing reconfigurable spin logic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes that thermal conductance alone cannot distinguish between topological and kinetic origins of fractional conductance in edge systems. The authors prove that the separation requires analyzing the charge sector, specifically using the Lorenz ratio and shot noise measurements, which are sensitive to the underlying charge dynamics at the superconducting boundary.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework for diagnosing the microscopic origin of quantized transport phenomena in topological materials, suggesting that electrical measurements (charge sector) are necessary when thermal measurements (energy sector) fail to distinguish mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of distinguishing between fractional thermal conductance arising from non-Abelian topology (like Majorana modes) versus that arising from full thermal equilibration of co-propagating edge modes.

**Main result.** A theorem proves that under full thermal equilibration, the thermal conductance ($\kappa_{th}$) depends only on the net chiral central charges, making it impossible to distinguish the two origins using $\kappa_{th}$ alone. Separation requires analyzing the charge sector via the Lorenz ratio and shot noise.

**Method.** The analysis uses boundary Conformal Field Theory (CFT) to derive universal formulas for energy currents and thermal conductance, followed by applying energy conservation principles to composite quantum systems.

**Model / system.** The primary theoretical model is a network of chiral edge segments joined at ideal floating contacts, applied specifically to the topological insulator–superconductor (TI–SC) interface involving Majorana and Integer Quantum Hall (IQH) edge modes.

**Key observables.** Thermal conductance ($\kappa_{th}$), Lorenz ratio ($\mathcal{L}$), and excess shot noise (Fano factor).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Net chiral central charges ($c_i$), temperature ($T$), and magnetic field ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theorem relies on the assumption of full thermal equilibration and ideal floating contacts; the separation mechanism requires the IQH edge not to abut the superconductor.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the general setup for the anomaly-pinned theorem using chiral edge segments; Figure 2 (mentioned) shows the geometry for explicit three-segment calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first proves the general Anomaly Pinning Theorem for thermal conductance in edge networks (Section II), then applies this to the TI–SC interface (Section III/IV), concluding that charge-sector diagnostics (Lorenz ratio, noise) are necessary for resolution (Section V).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A recent experiment by Roy et al. (Nat. Commun. 17, 2853 (2026)) demonstrated a robust half-integer thermal conductance plateau, κ_0 T/2, at a bipolar (ν,ν')=(2,-1) junction in bilayer graphene, produced not by non-Abelian topology but by full equilibration of co-propagating electron and hole edge modes. We prove a theorem that fixes when the two origins can be told apart: under full thermal equilibration, the two-terminal thermal conductance of a network of chiral edge segments joined at ideal floating contacts is a rational function of the net chiral central charges of the segments alone, quantities pinned by the gravitational anomaly and invariant under arbitrary local boundary kinetics. The corollary is an impossibility statement: whenever two realizations present the same anomaly data to the same network, no thermal-conductance measurement can distinguish them. That the equilibrated (2,-1) value equals the central charge of a chiral Majorana mode is an arithmetic fact about one filling combination, but wherever such a coincidence occurs it is beyond the reach of thermometry, and the separation must come from the charge sector, which is not anomaly-pinned at a superconducting boundary. We develop the topological insulator-superconductor interface as the application: a vortex carries fractional charge e/4 from the θ= πmagnetoelectric coupling, the boundary hosts a chiral Majorana mode of central charge 1/2, and a laterally adjacent integer quantum Hall (IQH) region supplies the kinetic realization. The Lorenz ratio (anomalous for the isolated Majorana boundary, L_0 / (1 + 4|ν_{IQH}|) in the composite device) and the excess shot noise (growing with B from e/4 vortex tunneling versus locked to IQH plateaus) together resolve the mechanism, provided the quantum Hall edge does not abut the superconductor.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how an external gate voltage controls the switching dynamics (phase slip rate) in a superconducting nanowire by eliminating substrate leakage paths. By measuring the rate and analyzing its dependence on $V_G$, the authors demonstrate a strong, non-trivial, and asymmetric coupling, providing critical guidance for understanding gate-controlled superconductivity mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a clean experimental platform to study non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in superconductors, linking external electrostatic fields directly to the quantum tunneling/activation process governing superconducting failure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To resolve the ambiguity in the Gate-Controlled Superconductivity (GCS) effect—whether critical current quenching is due to the electric field or leakage current—and to study the gate-induced phase slip rate ($\Gamma$).

**Main result.** The study establishes a clear, non-trivial, and asymmetric connection between the gate voltage and the phase slip rate, suggesting the gate acts directly on the phase-slip free-energy barrier.

**Method.** The researchers used direct measurement of phase slip escape rates ($\Gamma$) from fixed-bias waiting-time statistics in a fully suspended nanowire geometry, complemented by theoretical fitting using the Langer-Ambegaokar-McCumber-Halperin (LAMH) model.

**Model / system.** The system is a fully suspended superconducting nanowire of amorphous MoGe, separated from the gate by a vacuum gap, which eliminates substrate-mediated leakage channels. The dynamics are governed by phase slip events, analyzed via the escape rate $\Gamma$.

**Key observables.** Phase slip escape rate ($\Gamma$) as a function of bias current ($I$) and gate voltage ($V_G$); the evolution of the exponent $b$ in the rate equation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltage ($V_G$), bias current ($I$), temperature ($T$), and the characteristic energy scales defining the phase slip barrier.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fully suspended geometry eliminates leakage; the analysis relies on fitting the escape rate to established theoretical forms (like LAMH) while ruling out alternative mechanisms (field emission, strain).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device geometry and measurement protocol, showing $\Gamma$ vs. $I$ and $\Gamma$ vs. $V_G$, and detailing the evolution of the fitting exponent $b$ with $V_G$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the experimental platform (suspended nanowire) to eliminate leakage. It then measures $\Gamma(V_G)$ and finds an exponential dependence. Subsequent analysis rules out alternative physical mechanisms (field emission, strain) and uses advanced fitting techniques to interpret the non-trivial dependence of $\Gamma$ on $V_G$ and $I$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A gate voltage can suppress the critical current of a superconducting nanowire, and various scenarios have been proposed to describe these observations. The key difficulty is that these scenarios, including both leakage-driven and field-driven mechanisms, are impossible to disentangle in standard experiments. Here, we resolve this issue by studying a fully suspended superconducting nanowire of amorphous MoGe, separated from the gate by vacuum, eliminating all leakage-driven channels. We measure the phase slip escape rate $Γ$ and find a large gate-induced enhancement of $Γ$, exponential over six orders of magnitude. Fitting various escape-rate models reveals a strong deviation from simple phase slip models, hinting at a complex interaction between the electrostatic field and the phase slip centers. Surprisingly, $Γ$ is not invariant under reversal of the gate-voltage polarity. Our work establishes a clear and nontrivial connection between gate voltage and $Γ$, guiding future research into the origins of the effect.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a microscopic theory to model the nonlocal excitonic optical response in monolayer $	ext{WS}_2$ supported by $	ext{hBN}$. By accounting for spatial nonlocality, the authors show that the resulting exciton-polariton dispersion shifts to lower wavevectors, enabling smoother near-field light confinement over larger distances. This highlights the critical role of nonlocality in controlling polaritonic behavior in van der Waals heterostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how spatial nonlocality fundamentally alters the optical properties of excitons in 2D materials, which is crucial for designing advanced nanoscale photonic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is achieving nanoscale light confinement and long-range propagation for visible-frequency modes in nanophotonics, which requires accounting for spatial nonlocality in the excitonic optical response.

**Main result.** Incorporating spatial nonlocality shifts the polariton dispersion toward lower in-plane wavevectors, leading to smoother near-field distributions that extend over larger lateral distances while maintaining nanoscale confinement.

**Method.** The authors developed a microscopic theory by combining a two-band massive Dirac fermion description with a generalized Mott-Wannier treatment to derive a nonlocal electric susceptibility tensor.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on in-plane exciton-polaritons (IPEPs) supported by monolayer $	ext{WS}_2$ encapsulated by $	ext{hBN}$. The model incorporates the center-of-mass and internal relative motion of excitons.

**Key observables.** Polariton dispersion relation ($\mathbf{q}_{\|}$), near-field electromagnetic field distribution, and the nonlocal electric susceptibility tensor $\chi_{\mu
u}(\mathbf{q}_{\|}, \omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** In-plane wavevectors ($\mathbf{q}_{\|}$), excitation energy, and the thickness of the capping $	ext{hBN}$ layer.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The local-response approximation breaks down because field variation length scales become comparable to the exciton size, necessitating the nonlocal treatment.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the $	ext{hBN}$-encapsulated monolayer $	ext{WS}_2$ structure with a point dipole placed above the heterostructure.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a theoretical derivation path: establishing the need for nonlocality, deriving the nonlocal susceptibility using combined fermion/Mott-Wannier theory, and finally applying this susceptibility to analyze the resulting polariton dispersion and near-field properties.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Achieving nanoscale light confinement and long-range propagation is a central challenge in nanophotonics, with important implications for controlling molecular photophysical processes and energy transfer. Monolayer transition-metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) support in-plane exciton-polaritons (IPEPs), deeply subwavelength optical modes in the visible range. In this regime, electromagnetic fields vary on length scales comparable to the exciton size, making spatial nonlocality in the excitonic optical response essential. Here, we develop a microscopic theory of the nonlocal excitonic optical response and investigate the near-field properties of IPEPs in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN)-encapsulated monolayer WS$_2$. Combining a two-band massive Dirac fermion description with a generalized Mott-Wannier treatment, we derive a nonlocal electric susceptibility that accounts for both the center-of-mass and internal relative motion of excitons. Spatial nonlocality in the excitonic optical response shifts the polariton dispersion toward lower in-plane wavevectors compared with the results based on the local-response approximation. This reduces high-wavevector contributions and produces smoother near-field distributions that extend over larger lateral distances while retaining nanoscale confinement. Furthermore, at higher excitation energies, multiple exciton-polariton modes contribute at distinct wavevectors, and their relative contributions can be tuned by varying the thickness of the capping hBN layer. These insights elucidate the fundamental role of spatial nonlocality in the polaritonic properties of van der Waals heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses anti-Stokes Raman scattering on graphene to probe non-equilibrium electron-phonon dynamics. By tuning the Fermi level using advanced electrochemical gating, the authors show that the anti-Stokes signal is strongly suppressed away from the Dirac point, contradicting simple thermal models. This establishes the anti-Stokes response as a sensitive diagnostic tool for electronic state-dependent phonon coupling in nanoscale conductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimental demonstration of how electronic structure (Fermi level) can fundamentally alter phonon scattering signatures, which is relevant to understanding energy dissipation in quantum devices and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the challenge of directly characterizing non-equilibrium electron and phonon populations that govern heat generation and dissipation in nanoscale electronic devices.

**Main result.** The anti-Stokes Raman response in graphene shows a strong, reversible, and gate-dependent suppression away from the Dirac point, indicating that the scattering process is significantly influenced by the electronic phase space rather than just the equilibrium phonon population.

**Method.** The researchers utilized anti-Stokes Raman scattering spectroscopy, employing a microdroplet spectroelectrochemical platform combined with dielectric back-gates and chemical doping to tune the Fermi level ($E_F$) in monolayer graphene.

**Model / system.** The physical system is monolayer graphene, whose electronic and vibrational properties are probed using Raman scattering. The analysis compares the measured anti-Stokes/Stokes intensity ratio against predictions based on equilibrium phonon populations, revealing deviations.

**Key observables.** Anti-Stokes/Stokes intensity ratio ($I_{aS}/I_S$), G-mode anti-Stokes and Stokes intensities, G-mode frequency and linewidth evolution, and the dependence of these observables on the gate-tuned Fermi level ($E_F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fermi level ($E_F$), Dirac point (charge neutrality point), excitation power ($P$), and gate voltage ($V_G$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard interpretation of the anti-Stokes/Stokes ratio as a measure of lattice temperature is challenged by the observed gate-dependent deviations, suggesting a necessary inclusion of electronic contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show gate-dependent heatmaps of G bands, the breakdown of the standard thermometry interpretation, and the power dependence of the anti-Stokes/Stokes ratio, all demonstrating strong electronic control over the scattering signal.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of non-equilibrium characterization, details the experimental setup using advanced gating techniques, presents measurements showing the gate-dependent suppression of $I_{aS}$ while $I_S$ remains stable, and concludes by establishing the anti-Stokes response as a sensitive probe of electron-phonon coupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-equilibrium electron and phonon populations govern heat generation and dissipation in nanoscale devices, yet their direct characterization remains challenging. Anti-Stokes Raman scattering provides a sensitive probe of phonon populations and local temperatures, although its interpretation becomes more complex when electronic and phononic populations are driven out of equilibrium. Here, we investigate the gate-dependent anti-Stokes Raman response of monolayer graphene and find that the anti-Stokes response deviates from that expected from an equilibrium phonon population. Using a spatially localized microdroplet spectroelectrochemical platform, dielectric back-gates, and chemical doping, we observe a strong and reversible suppression of the G-mode anti-Stokes intensity as the Fermi level is tuned away from the Dirac point, while the Stokes intensity remains essentially unchanged. By contrast, the G-mode frequency and linewidth of both the Stokes and anti-Stokes branches evolve with doping as expected from the established Kohn-anomaly picture. The anti-Stokes/Stokes intensity ratio decreases by several-fold over the measured range and displays a pronounced, gate-dependent power response with maximum sensitivity at charge neutrality. Comparison with previous studies of non-equilibrium carrier dynamics in doped graphene suggests an important role of Fermi-level-dependent electronic phase space, which can influence both hot-carrier-driven phonon populations and electronically mediated Stokes-anti-Stokes scattering. Our findings establish anti-Stokes Raman scattering as a sensitive probe of electron-phonon coupling and non-equilibrium phonon dynamics in graphene.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the creation of a highly compact, integrated, and alignment-free single-frequency laser source operating in the mid-infrared using $	ext{Ho}^{3+}$-doped fluoride glass. By integrating femtosecond laser-written waveguides and Bragg gratings, the authors achieved stable lasing at $2062.21 	ext{ nm}$ with excellent spectral purity and linear polarization. This platform offers a robust alternative to traditional bulk laser sources for quantum applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents an advanced demonstration of integrated photonics in the mid-IR, which is crucial for quantum sensing and quantum communication applications that require stable, compact sources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to develop a compact, fully integrated, alignment-free, and linearly polarized single-frequency laser source operating in the mid-infrared using $	ext{Ho}^{3+}$-doped fluoride glass.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated stable single-frequency lasing at $2062.21 	ext{ nm}$ with a narrow linewidth ($< 5 	ext{ MHz}$) and confirmed linear polarization, all within a compact, integrated chip architecture.

**Method.** The system was fabricated by inscribing waveguides and waveguide Bragg gratings (WBGs) into fluoride glass using a femtosecond laser, followed by characterization of lasing performance and polarization state.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an $	ext{Ho}^{3+}$-doped fluoride glass waveguide laser cavity. The cavity is formed by integrating a femtosecond laser-inscribed waveguide and a WBG, creating a compact, chip-scale resonator.

**Key observables.** Lasing wavelength ($2062.21 	ext{ nm}$), linewidth ($< 5 	ext{ MHz}$), polarization state (linear), spectral bandwidth ($	ext{FWHM}$), and coupling coefficient ($\kappa$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mid-infrared range ($\sim 2 \mu	ext{m}$), waveguide width ($\approx 9 \mu	ext{m}$), and grating lengths ($5 	ext{ to } 13.5 	ext{ mm}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The alignment-free nature is attributed to the butt-coupling of the pump light via the PR grating, and polarization selectivity is hypothesized to arise from form birefringence or polarization-dependent scattering losses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup (waveguide/grating placement), spectral measurements (transmission/reflection of gratings), and time-domain measurements showing stable lasing output and polarization analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the fabrication process using femtosecond laser inscription, characterizes the resulting Bragg grating resonances, demonstrates stable single-frequency lasing, and investigates the underlying physics governing polarization selectivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report, for the first time, a fully integrated Ho$^{3+}$-doped fluoride glass waveguide laser incorporating a femtosecond laser-inscribed waveguide and waveguide Bragg grating. The compact 12.4 mm cavity achieves stable single-frequency lasing at 2062.21 nm with a line width of better than 5 MHz. The laser emits linearly polarized light, which is efficiently delivered through an SM1950 single-mode fiber. This alignment-free architecture provides a compact and robust platform for integrated mid-infrared single-frequency laser sources.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates energy transport in 2D molecular crystals by linking excitonic band topology to physical observables. Using STML, the authors image excitons and demonstrate that edge-confined states, predicted by non-trivial band topology, can be directly resolved. This establishes molecular crystals as a viable platform for engineering directional energy flow.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges condensed matter theory (band topology) with experimental quantum optics/spectroscopy (STML), providing a tangible platform for studying coherent energy transfer in molecular systems, which is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum light-matter interaction.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine if the topological features inherent in the excitonic band structure of molecular crystals can generate robust, edge-confined states that facilitate directional energy transport.

**Main result.** The researchers successfully imaged and theoretically confirmed the existence of edge-confined excitonic states in 2D molecular crystals, demonstrating that molecular crystals can be engineered for controlled energy flow.

**Method.** The work combines experimental imaging using Scanning Tunneling Microscopy-induced Luminescence (STML) with theoretical modeling based on a Frenkel-exciton Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The physical system is 2D molecular crystals (e.g., zinc phthalocyanine) hosting Frenkel excitons. The model uses a Hamiltonian describing coherent excitonic coupling, with calculations relying on TDDFT to derive transition charge densities.

**Key observables.** Real-space wavefunctions of excitonic states, spectral features in STML maps, and the identification of edge-localized states linked to non-trivial band topology (Zak phase).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cluster size ($N 	imes N$), energy eigenvalues ($\Delta E_i$), and the coupling strength derived from dipole-dipole interactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes coupling is dominated by dipole-dipole interactions, and that the system can be modeled by restricting the Hamiltonian to the single-excitation subspace.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, STM topographies, and STML spectra, alongside theoretical simulations showing the evolution of excitonic bands from discrete to collective modes, and the spatial mapping of edge states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical problem and experimental technique (STML imaging) to developing the theoretical model (Hamiltonian/TDDFT), and finally demonstrating the key finding: the observation and theoretical confirmation of topologically protected edge states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Collective molecular excitations govern energy transport in light-harvesting and optoelectronic materials. Theory predicts that excitonic band topology can generate edge-confined states, offering a route to direct energy flow in molecular systems, yet experimental evidence is lacking. Here we use scanning tunnelling microscopy-induced luminescence to image Frenkel excitons in two-dimensional zinc phthalocyanine molecular crystals with nanometer resolution. By varying cluster size, we observe the transition from discrete quantum-confined excitations to collective excitonic bands and directly resolve their real-space wavefunctions. We identify an excitonic state localized at the crystal perimeter and show, through comparison with microscopic theory, that it originates from the momentum-resolved non-trivial topology of the bulk excitonic bands. Our results establish molecular crystals as a platform for engineering topological excitonic states and provide a strategy for controlling energy transport through excitonic band engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes solitons in optical systems exhibiting third-order dispersion, treating the system as an analogue for event horizons. The authors compute the Bogoliubov-de Gennes spectrum and measure the Cherenkov radiation emitted by the solitons. The findings show that the radiation rate is much higher than predicted by simple theory, and the results are framed within a kinematic model that treats thermal relations as external inputs.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear optics, quantum field theory in curved spacetime analogues, and soliton dynamics, providing quantitative results on energy loss and spectral properties near an analogue horizon.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the properties of solitons in the presence of third-order dispersion, specifically in the context of optical analogues of event horizons.

**Main result.** The measured Cherenkov emission rate significantly exceeds first-order predictions, and the analysis successfully describes soliton recoil and decay using a kinematic model where thermal relations are treated as imposed inputs.

**Method.** The study employs Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) spectrum analysis, measures steady Cherenkov emission flux, and develops a kinematic model for the analogue horizon.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by the nonlinear Schrödinger equation (GNLSE) in the anomalous-dispersion regime, describing solitons in optical fibers. The analysis linearizes this equation around the stationary state.

**Key observables.** BdG spectrum (Jordan blocks, negative-energy modes), steady Cherenkov emission rate, soliton recoil, and the surface gravity ($\kappa$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Third-order dispersion strength ($\delta_3$), soliton amplitude, and the operating point parameters ($N=1, \delta_3=0.05, \kappa_g=0.3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The thermal relation and entanglement are treated as imposed model inputs, and the analysis is restricted to Raman-free Kerr media.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows Krein-signed BdG spectra for the soliton, comparing the integrable case ($\delta_3=0$) with the operating value ($\delta_3=0.05$), detailing the zero-mode sector.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by analyzing the BdG spectrum, quantifying the Cherenkov emission rate from adiabatically prepared solitons, and finally constructing a kinematic model for the analogue horizon to relate surface gravity to soliton properties.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Third-order dispersion turns the soliton of the nonlinear Schrödinger equation, which underlies optical analogues of event horizons in fibers, into a weakly radiating solitary wave. We compute three of its properties without assuming a thermal emission law. First, the linearized (Bogoliubov-de Gennes) problem must be posed about the stationary state of the dispersive equation: linearizing about the integrable profile produces a complex quartet that is converged in grid and box, grows as the square root of the dispersion strength and is spurious. About the stationary state, on a periodic grid, the zero-mode sector consists of two Jordan blocks of size two and the spectrum is real at the operating point, including negative-energy box modes of the resonant branch. Second, we measure the steady Cherenkov emission of adiabatically prepared solitons from the flux of the radiation behind them, over eight decades. The radiation is phase-matched in the frame of the drifting soliton, and the first-order rate falls short of the measured one by factors of 29 to 45. Expressed through a scaled dispersion coefficient of the local soliton, the measured rate describes launched solitons of three amplitudes; with norm and momentum balance, and no further fitted parameter, it reproduces their recoil and the slow decay of their loss rate; at weak dispersion the loss of a launched soliton is the shedding of its launch mismatch. Third, a census of the asymptotic channels shows that long-wavelength upstream emission requires the chemical potential of the soliton, lowered by the comoving-frame Doppler shift of a drifting soliton, to lie inside the analysis band, which bounds the soliton amplitude. In a kinematic model of the flow the surface gravity has a closed form; the thermal relation and the entanglement built on it are imposed inputs of that model. The construction applies to Raman-free Kerr media.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports a novel, robust fabrication method combining dry and wet etching to create high-quality superconducting edge contacts to buried Ge quantum wells. By achieving near-perfect interface transparency and observing strong Andreev enhancement in quantum point contacts, the authors demonstrate a reliable platform for superconducting hybrid quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides experimental evidence of controlled superconducting proximity effects in solid-state heterostructures, which is foundational for building superconducting quantum circuits and quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is establishing high-quality, reproducible superconducting contacts to deeply buried quantum wells (QWs) within semiconductor-superconductor hybrid systems.

**Main result.** The combination of anisotropic dry etching followed by gentle wet etching proves to be a robust fabrication strategy, yielding Josephson junctions with near-unity interface transparency and quantum point contacts showing strong Andreev enhancement.

**Method.** The authors employed an edge-contact fabrication approach, combining dry and wet etching techniques, followed by electrical characterization of Josephson junctions and quantum point contacts.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a semiconductor-superconductor hybrid structure, specifically involving a buried Germanium (Ge) quantum well contacted by a superconductor (e.g., Al). Measurements probe proximity effects, Andreev bound states, and superconducting transport phenomena.

**Key observables.** Interface transparency, subgap conductance steps in QPCs (approaching $4e^2/h$), quantized supercurrent, and the length dependence of the $I_{sw}R_n$ product.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Junction length ($L$), superconducting gap ($\Delta \approx 186-189 \mu	ext{eV}$), and the ratio of superconducting to normal conductance ($G_S/G_N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the short-junction limit for certain theoretical fits, and the success of the wet etch in preserving the geometry defined by the dry etch.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the combined dry/wet-etch fabrication process, SEM images of junctions, and plots showing $I_{sw}R_n$ vs. length, as well as $I-V$ curves and $G$ vs. $V_g$ measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the fabrication process (dry/wet etching) to create the contacts, followed by electrical characterization of the resulting Josephson junctions and quantum point contacts to verify high-quality superconducting coupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Semiconductor-superconductor hybrid systems form the basis for a broad range of quantum phenomena, such as Andreev bound states, topological superconductivity, and hybrid quantum Hall states. The performance of these hybrid systems can strongly benefit from the exceptional electronic properties of heterostructures with deeply buried quantum wells. However, establishing high-quality superconducting contacts to such quantum wells remains challenging. Here, we address this problem with an edge-contact approach that combines anisotropic dry etching to access the quantum well followed by wet etching for interface preparation. Josephson junctions fabricated using this approach consistently exhibit near-unity interface transparency, while quantum point contacts show strong Andreev enhancement, with the first conductance plateau approaching $4e^2/h$, the limit of perfect Andreev reflection. In superconducting quantum point contacts, the high contact quality is further manifested by quantized supercurrent. These results establish the combination of dry and wet etching as a robust strategy for integrating deeply buried quantum wells into superconducting hybrid devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses Scanning SQUID Microscopy (SSM) to image trapped magnetic flux in superconducting circuits while simultaneously measuring the DC SQUID's electrical performance. By correlating the precise location of trapped vortices with changes in the critical current, the authors prove that flux pinning is governed by specific, reproducible physical sites within the multilayer circuit geometry. This provides crucial feedback for designing robust superconducting quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct, simultaneous measurement linking microscopic flux pinning sites (from SSM) to macroscopic device performance ($I_c$) is highly valuable for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in superconducting circuits, which relates to flux quantization and energy landscape mapping.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to directly correlate the location of trapped magnetic flux (vortices) in the ground plane of superconducting circuits with the measured electrical performance of a DC SQUID.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that preferential pinning sites for fluxons exist regardless of moat configuration, providing a powerful tool for validating circuit simulation models and guiding physical layout design.

**Method.** The authors employ Scanning SQUID Microscopy (SSM) to image trapped flux while simultaneously measuring the DC SQUID's critical current ($I_c$) during the same cooldown process.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a DC SQUID integrated into a multilayer superconducting circuit featuring engineered ground plane 'moats' designed to mitigate residual magnetic flux.

**Key observables.** DC SQUID critical current ($I_c$), location and number of trapped fluxons/vortices (visualized by SSM).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Designed zero-field $I_c$ ($\approx 522 \mu	ext{A}$), loop inductance ($L = 9.1 	ext{pH}$), background flux density ($< 1 \mu	ext{T}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique assumes that flux trapping can be controlled by cooling in a controlled applied magnetic field, and that the measured $I_c$ can be accurately extracted despite parasitic effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the device structure, the correlation between trapped flux number and measured $I_c$ for multi-moat devices, and simulations illustrating how pinned vortices shift the SQUID's critical current modulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the methodology of simultaneous SSM imaging and electrical measurement. It then presents detailed results showing how flux trapping affects $I_c$ in multi-moat and single-moat geometries, culminating in a discussion of how local vs. global fields dictate preferred pinning sites.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Holes in the ground planes of superconducting circuits, commonly referred to as moats, are known to be an effective means of mitigating the deleterious effects of residual magnetic flux in superconducting circuits. Previous studies have utilized scanning SQUID microscopy (SSM) to image the effectiveness of various moat geometries and the maximum magnetic fields where they no longer prevent vortices in the ground plane but have not directly correlated the location of flux with circuit performance measurements. In this study we employ SSM to image the effectiveness of various moat configurations in trapping unwanted flux near a DC SQUID, and when the moats are not effective, the location of vortices in the ground plane. During the same cooldown, and without disturbing the circuit, we measure the circuit performance to directly correlate this with the vortex location. We show that there are preferential pinning sites for fluxons regardless of moat configuration, demonstrating the utility of the SSM in provide feedback for circuit layout, modeling, and failure analysis.

</details>
